Non-Chiral Vertex Operator Algebra Associated To Lorentzian Lattices And Narain CFTs
Ranveer Kumar Singh, Madhav Sinha

Abstract
Frenkel, Lepowsky, and Meurman constructed a vertex operator algebra (VOA) associated to any even, integral, Euclidean lattice. In the language of physics, these are examples of chiral conformal field theories (CFT). In this paper, we define non-chiral vertex operator algebra and some associated notions. We then give a construction of a non-chiral VOA associated to an even, integral, Lorentzian lattice and construct their irreducible modules. We obtain the moduli space of such modular invariant non-chiral CFTs based on even, self-dual Lorentzian lattices of signature ( m , n ) (m,n) assuming the validity of a technical result about automorphisms of the lattice. We finally show that Narain conformal field theories in physics are examples of non-chiral VOA. Our formalism helps us to identify the chiral algebra of Narain CFTs in terms of a particular sublattice and give us the decomposition of its partition function into sum of characters.



1
Introduction

The study of vertex (operator) algebras started with the work of Borcherds
Borcherds:1983sq
and Frenkel, Lepowsky, and Meurman
Frenkel:1988xz
in relation to Monstrous Moonshine and two dimensional conformal field theory. The first non-trivial examples of the theory were constructed starting from an even, integral Euclidean lattice, see
Dolan:1994st
;
Moore:2023zmv
for a more physical construction. These are examples of what are called
chiral conformal field theory
in physics, see Subsection
1.1.1
for an introduction. There are ample examples of non-chiral conformal field theories which cannot be described mathematically in the language of vertex operator algebra. One large class of such theories is the Narain CFTs based on even, self-dual Lorentzian lattices.

In this paper, we define the notion of non-chiral vertex operator algebra and study various related notions. Our definition is based on the notion
1
1

1

See also
Kapustin:2000aa
;
rosellen2004opealgebras
for some earlier, related but different, discussion on non-chiral VOAs.
of
full field algebras
introduced by Huang and Kong in
Huang:2005gz
and
full vertex algebras
introduced by Moriwaki in
Moriwaki:2020cxf
. We use formal calculus as well as complex analysis to formulate our axioms. We replace the Jacobi identity axiom of vertex (operator) algebras by a
locality
axiom which is general enough to imply the
duality
and hence operator product expansion of vertex operators. Various well-known examples of non-chiral conformal field theories in physics are examples of our definition. More concretely, we construct a class of examples of non-chiral VOAs based on Lorentzian lattices which cover the moduli space of Narain CFTs as examples of our definition. We then define modules and intertwiners of non-chiral VOAs on the lines of
Frenkel:1993xz
. In the rest of this section, we describe a dictionary between non-chiral VOAs of this paper and the notion of (non-chiral) conformal field theory in physics.

1.1
The dictionary from non-chiral VOA to non-chiral CFTs

In this section, we give a dictionary between conformal field theories as generally understood in physics and the non-chiral vertex operator algebra definition in this paper. This dictionary, for the case of chiral conformal field theory, is well-known to experts. We extend it to the case of non-chiral VOA. We start by describing the defining data of a conformal field theory in physics.

1.1.1
Physical definition of a CFT

We begin with the Belavin-Polyakov-Zamalodchikov (BPZ) definition of a conformal field theory
Belavin:1984vu
. We follow
DiFrancesco:1997nk
;
Moore:1988qv
;
Moore:1989vd
;
Blumenhagen:2009zz
;
Gaberdiel:1999mc
;
Mukhi:2022bte
for this exposition.

A bosonic CFT is an inner product space
\mathscr{H}
which
2
2

2

Physically, one always has a Hilbert space.
is decomposable as a direct sum of tensor product

\mathscr{H}=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}h,\bar{h}\\
h-\bar{h}\in\mathbb{Z}\end{subarray}}V(h,c)\otimes\overline{V}(\bar{h},\bar{c})\penalty\ ,

(1)

of irreducible highest weight modules of
\text{Vir}_{c}\times\overline{\text{Vir}}_{\bar{c}}
, where where
\text{Vir}_{c}\text{ and }\overline{\text{Vir}}_{\bar{c}}
are two copies of the Virasoro algebra with central charge
c,\bar{c}
:

\begin{split}&\left[L_{m},L_{n}\right]=(m-n)L_{m+n}+\frac{c}{12}m\left(m^{2}-1\right)\delta_{m+n,0}\,,\\
&\left[\bar{L}_{m},\bar{L}_{n}\right]=(m-n)\bar{L}_{m+n}+\frac{\bar{c}}{12}m\left(m^{2}-1\right)\delta_{m+n,0}\,,\\
&\left[L_{m},\bar{L}_{n}\right]=0\,,\end{split}

(2)

such that the following are satisfied :

1.

Identity Property
: There is a unique vector
|0\rangle\in V(0,c)\otimes\overline{V}(0,\bar{c})
which is invariant under the
\mathfrak{sl}(2)\times\overline{\mathfrak{sl}(2)}
-subalgebra of
\text{Vir}_{c}\times\overline{\text{Vir}}_{\bar{c}}
generated by
L_{0},\bar{L}_{0},L_{\pm 1}
, and
\bar{L}_{\pm 1}
.

2.

For each vector
\alpha\in\mathscr{H}
there is an operator
\phi_{\alpha}(z,\bar{z})
acting on
\mathscr{H}
, parameterized by
z\in\mathbb{C}
. Also, for every operator
\phi_{\alpha}
there exists a conjugate operator
\phi_{\alpha^{\vee}}
, partially characterized by the requirement that the operator product expansion (OPE) of
\phi_{\alpha}
and
\phi_{\alpha^{\vee}}
contains a descendant of the identity operator.

3.

L_{n}
Property
: For
\alpha\in V(h,c)\otimes\overline{V}(\bar{h},\bar{c})
a highest weight state of the
(\text{Vir}_{c}\times\overline{\text{Vir}}_{\bar{c}})
-action, we have

\begin{split}\left[L_{n},\phi_{\alpha}(z,\bar{z})\right]=\left(z^{n+1}\frac{d}{dz}+h(n+1)z^{n}\right)\phi_{\alpha}(z,\bar{z})\,,\\
\left[\bar{L}_{n},\phi_{\alpha}(z,\bar{z})\right]=\left(\bar{z}^{n+1}\frac{d}{d\bar{z}}+\bar{h}(n+1)\bar{z}^{n}\right)\phi_{\alpha}(z,\bar{z})\,,\end{split}

(3)

for real numbers
h
and
\bar{h}
.

4.

Duality Property
: The inner products
\left\langle 0\left|\phi_{\alpha_{1}}\left(z_{1},\bar{z}_{1}\right)\ldots\phi_{\alpha_{n}}\left(z_{n},\bar{z}_{n}\right)\right|0\right\rangle
exist for
\left|z_{1}\right|>\ldots>\left|z_{n}\right|>0
and admit an unambiguous real-analytic continuation, independent of ordering
3
3

3

For Fermionic fields, one has to keep track of signs while commuting them past each other.
, to
\mathbb{C}^{n}\setminus\{z_{1},\dots,z_{n}=0,\infty;z_{i}=z_{j}\}
.

5.

Modular invariance property :
The torus partition function and correlation functions, given in terms of traces exist and are modular invariant.

These axioms do not characterise a CFT but are necessary for a well defined CFT. A particular subset of operators which only depend only on
z
or
\bar{z}
are of interest. Such operators are called
chiral
(
z
dependent) and
anti-chiral
(
\bar{z}
dependent) operators. The
maximal
set of chiral and anti-chiral operators form an algebra which we denote by
\mathscr{A}
and
\overline{\mathscr{A}}
respectively
4
4

4

Note that there can be subsets of
\mathscr{A}
and
\overline{\mathscr{A}}
which closes among themselves. Notable example is the set with identity operator and the stress tensor. In the following, whenever we speak of (anti-)chiral algebra, we mean the maximal algebra of (anti-)holomorphic fields. In mathematical terminology,
\mathscr{A},\overline{\mathscr{A}}
, with some added structure, are
vertex operator algebras
and the theory of
(\overline{\mathscr{A}})\penalty\ \mathscr{A}
and its irreducible representations is called a (
anti
-)
chiral conformal field theory
.
. For any CFT,
\mathds{1}\in\mathscr{A}\otimes\overline{\mathscr{A}}
,
T(z)\in\mathscr{A}
and
\overline{T}(\bar{z})\in\overline{\mathscr{A}}
where
T
and
\overline{T}
are the holomorphic and anti-holomorphic stress tensor with modes

L_{n}
and
\bar{L}_{n}
respectively. Let
\{\mathcal{O}^{i}(z)\}
be a basis of
\mathscr{A}
. The OPE of chiral operators takes the form

\mathcal{O}^{i}(z)\mathcal{O}^{j}(w)=\sum_{k}\frac{c_{ijk}}{(z-w)^{h_{ijk}}}\mathcal{O}^{k}(w)\,,

(4)

for some coefficients
c_{ijk}
where
h_{ijk}=h_{i}+h_{j}-h_{k}
. By the usual contour integral manipulations we can write the above OPE as the algebra of modes:

\left[\mathcal{O}_{n}^{i},\mathcal{O}_{m}^{j}\right]=\sum_{k}c_{ijk}(n,m)\mathcal{O}_{n+m}^{k}\,\,,

(5)

where

\mathcal{O}^{j}(z)=\sum_{m}\mathcal{O}_{m}^{j}z^{-m-h_{j}}\,.

(6)

Similar OPE holds for anti-chiral operators. The algebra (
5
) of modes of operators in
\mathscr{A}
is called the
chiral algebra
and the algebra of modes of operators in
\overline{\mathscr{A}}
is called the
anti-chiral algebra
of the CFT. Since the OPE of (anti-)holomorphic operators is equivalent to the algebra of their modes, we will use the same symbol
(\overline{\mathscr{A}})\penalty\ \mathscr{A}
for the (anti-)chiral algebra and the algebra of (anti-)holomorphic operators. In the following, we will only speak of the chiral algebra but all statements hold for anti-chiral algebra equally well. The chiral algebra of any CFT contains the (universal enveloping algebra of) Virasoro algebra since the stress tensor is always a chiral operator. Other
examples of chiral algebra include the affine Kac-Moody algebra
Goddard:1986bp
and the
W_{3}
algebra
Zamolodchikov:1985wn
. Only the zero mode
the of chiral operator
\mathcal{O}(z)
commute with the Hamiltonian
(L_{0}+\bar{L}_{0})
and are hence called the
symmetry-generating algebra
. In the inner product space (
1
), one can talk about subspaces
\mathscr{H}_{i}
which form irreducible representations of the chiral algebra
\mathscr{A}
. For this reason the full chiral algebra is sometimes also called the
spectrum-generating algebra
. We can thus decompose the physical Hilbert space
\mathscr{H}
as:

\mathscr{H}=\bigoplus_{i,\bar{i}}N_{i,\bar{i}}\mathscr{H}_{i}\otimes\overline{\mathscr{H}}_{\bar{i}}\,,

(7)

where
\mathscr{H}_{i},\overline{\mathscr{H}}_{\bar{i}}
are irreducible representations of
\mathscr{A},\overline{\mathscr{A}}
respectively and
N_{i,\bar{i}}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}=\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
, the set of non-negative integers, is the number of times
\mathscr{H}_{i}\otimes\overline{\mathscr{H}}_{\bar{i}}
appears in
\mathscr{H}
. For the index value
i,\bar{i}=0
, we take
\mathscr{H}_{0}
and
\overline{\mathscr{H}}_{0}
to be the subspace of
\mathscr{H}
which contains states corresponding to
\mathscr{A}
and
\overline{\mathscr{A}}
respectively modulo the null states. Hence,
N_{i,0}=\delta_{i,0},N_{0,\bar{i}}

=\delta_{0,\bar{i}}
.
5
5

5

This is again not true if we take
\mathscr{A}
and
\overline{\mathscr{A}}
to be some strictly smaller subalgebra of the maximal chiral algebra. For example, in the
c=\frac{8}{10}
three state Potts CFT , if we take the chiral algebra to be Virasoro, then there is a conformal dimension (3,0) and (0,3) representation of the Virasoro algebra present in the theory and hence
N_{3,0}=1,N_{0,3}=1

Chang:2018iay
. But since these fields are holomorphic and antiholomorphic respectively, according to our definition, they are included in
\mathscr{A},\overline{\mathscr{A}}
respectively and the chiral algebra is extended to
W_{3}
algebra, first constructed in
Zamolodchikov:1985wn
. Several such examples are constructed in
Cappelli:1987xt
.

Let us now describe the relation between the two decompositions (
1
) and (
7
). A general state
|h,\bar{h}\rangle\in\mathscr{H}
is called a
Virasoro primary
of conformal dimension
(h,\bar{h})
if

\begin{split}&L_{0}|h,\bar{h}\rangle=h|h,\bar{h}\rangle\quad\bar{L}_{0}|h,\bar{h}\rangle=\bar{h}|h,\bar{h}\rangle\\
&L_{n}|h,\bar{h}\rangle=\bar{L}_{n}|h,\bar{h}\rangle=0,\quad n>0.\end{split}

(8)

This follows from the OPE

\begin{split}&T(z)\phi(w,\bar{w})=\frac{h}{(z-w)^{2}}\phi(w,\bar{w})+\frac{\partial_{w}\phi(w,\bar{w})}{z-w}+\text{ hol. },\\
&\overline{T}(\bar{z})\phi(w,\bar{w})=\frac{\bar{h}}{(\bar{z}-\bar{w})^{2}}\phi(w,\bar{w})+\frac{\partial_{\bar{w}}\phi(w,\bar{w})}{\bar{z}-\bar{w}}+\text{ anti-hol. },\end{split}

(9)

where
\phi(w,\bar{w})
is the operator corresponding to the
|h,\bar{h}\rangle
. One can identify the state
|h,\bar{h}\rangle
with

|h,\bar{h}\rangle\equiv\lim_{z,\bar{z}\to 0}\phi(z,\bar{z})|0\rangle\penalty\ .

(10)

We will consider
|h,\bar{h}\rangle
as the tensor product of states
|h\rangle,|\bar{h}\rangle
:
|h,\bar{h}\rangle\equiv|h\rangle\otimes|\bar{h}\rangle
. The
Verma modules

V(h,c)
and
\overline{V}(\bar{h},\bar{c})
is given by

\begin{split}&V(h,c):=\text{Span}_{\mathbb{C}}\{L_{-n_{1}}\dots L_{-n_{k}}|h\rangle:n_{1},\dots,n_{k}>0,k\in\mathbb{N}\}\,,\\
&\overline{V}(\bar{h},\bar{c}):=\text{Span}_{\mathbb{C}}\{\bar{L}_{-n_{1}}\dots\bar{L}_{-n_{k}}|\bar{h}\rangle:n_{1},\dots,n_{k}>0,k\in\mathbb{N}\}\,.\end{split}

(11)

The commutators (
2
) and the Virasoro primary condition makes the Verma module
V(h,c)\otimes\overline{V}(\bar{h},\bar{c})
into a
(\text{Vir}_{c}\times\overline{\text{Vir}}_{\bar{c}})
-representation. In general, this representation is
reducible
and one has to quotient out
singular
or
null states

6
6

6

A singular or null state is a vector which is not a highest weight vector but is annihilated by
L_{n},\bar{L}_{n},\penalty\ n>0
, see
(
di1996conformal
, Section 7.1.3)
for a detailed discussion.
from these Verma modules to make them irreducible. We will assume that this has already been done and that
V(h,c)\otimes\overline{V}(\bar{h},\bar{c})
is an irreducible
(\text{Vir}_{c}\times\overline{\text{Vir}}_{\bar{c}})
-module. Note that from the OPE (
9
) we must have
\bar{h}=0
(
h=0
) for a chiral (anti-chiral field). Now we can identify
\mathscr{H}_{0}\otimes\overline{\mathscr{H}}_{0}
as

\mathscr{H}_{0}\otimes\overline{\mathscr{H}}_{0}\cong\left(\bigoplus_{h\in\cal{S}}V(h,c)\right)\otimes\left(\bigoplus_{\bar{h}\in\overline{\cal{S}}}\overline{V}(\bar{h},\bar{c})\right)\subset\mathscr{H},

(12)

where

\begin{split}\mathcal{S}:=\{h\in\mathbb{Z}:|h\rangle\otimes|0\rangle\equiv|h,0\rangle\in\mathscr{H}\}\penalty\ ,\\
\overline{\mathcal{S}}:=\{\bar{h}\in\mathbb{Z}:|0\rangle\otimes|\bar{h}\rangle\equiv|0,\bar{h}\rangle\in\mathscr{H}\}\penalty\ .\end{split}

(13)

A
chiral primary
is a Virasoro primary
|h,\bar{h}\rangle
satisfying

\mathcal{O}_{m}|h,\bar{h}\rangle=\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{m}|h,\bar{h}\rangle=0,\quad m>0\,,

(14)

and is an eigenstate of
\mathcal{O}_{0}
and
\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{0}
for every
\mathcal{O}\in\mathscr{A}
and
\overline{\mathcal{O}}\in\overline{\mathcal{A}}
, the operator corresponding to it will be called the
chiral primary field
. Then each irreducible factor in (
7
) is a subspace of a Verma module constructed over a chiral primary by the modes of every chiral and anti-chiral operator.

One can then talk about characters of the CFT defined for each irreducible factor
\mathscr{H}_{i}\otimes\overline{\mathscr{H}}_{\bar{i}}
:

\chi_{i,\bar{i}}(\tau,\bar{\tau})=\text{Tr}_{\mathscr{H}_{i}\otimes\overline{\mathscr{H}}_{\bar{i}}}q^{L_{0}-\frac{c}{24}}\bar{q}^{\bar{L}_{0}-\frac{\bar{c}}{24}},\quad q=e^{2\pi i\tau},\penalty\ \penalty\ \bar{q}=e^{-2\pi i\bar{\tau}}\penalty\ ,

(15)

where
\tau\in\mathbb{H}:=\{x+iy\in\mathbb{C}:y>0\}
is in the upper half plane.
From the fact that
\mathscr{H}_{i}\otimes\overline{\mathscr{H}}_{\bar{i}}
is built over some highest vector
|h_{i},\bar{h}_{i}\rangle
, we see that the character has the form

\chi_{i,\bar{i}}(\tau,\bar{\tau})=q^{h_{i}-\frac{c}{24}}\bar{q}^{\bar{h}_{i}-\frac{\bar{c}}{24}}\sum_{n,m=0}^{\infty}a(n)\bar{a}(m)q^{n}\bar{q}^{m}\penalty\ ,

(16)

for some integers
a(n),\bar{a}(m)
. Thus we can separate the character into
chiral
and
anti-chiral characters
:

\chi_{i,\bar{i}}(\tau,\bar{\tau})=\chi_{i}(\tau)\overline{\chi}_{\bar{i}}(\bar{\tau})

(17)

where

\chi_{i}(\tau)=q^{h_{i}-\frac{c}{24}}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}a(n)q^{n},\quad\overline{\chi}_{\bar{i}}(\bar{\tau})=\bar{q}^{\bar{h}_{i}-\frac{\bar{c}}{24}}\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\bar{a}(m)\bar{q}^{m}\penalty\ .

(18)

The partition function of the CFT is then given by sum over chiral characters

Z(\tau,\bar{\tau}):=\text{Tr}_{\mathscr{H}}q^{L_{0}-\frac{c}{24}}\bar{q}^{\bar{L}_{0}-\frac{\bar{c}}{24}}=\sum_{i,\bar{i}}N_{i,\bar{i}}\chi_{i}(\tau)\overline{\chi}_{\bar{i}}(\bar{\tau})\penalty\ .

(19)

Modular invariance of the partition function implies that the chiral characters form a weight-zero weakly holomorphic vector valued modular form
7
7

7

See for example
Choie2019
for the definition of vector valued modular forms.
. Using this property of chiral characters, one can classify CFTs with finitely many primary fields and given central charge
Mukhi:2022bte
;
Gaberdiel:2016zke
;
Das:2022uoe
;
Das:2023qns
;
Hampapura:2015cea
;
Mathur:1988gt
;
Mathur:1988na
;
Mathur:1988rx
. A CFT is said to admit a
holomorphic factorization
if the partition function and its correlation functions decompose into a product of an analytic function of
\tau
and an analytic function of
\bar{\tau}
. If a CFT admits a holomorphic factorization, then the CFT is a tensor product of a chiral and an anti-chiral CFT (see Footnote
4
). A non-chiral conformal field theory does not admit a holomorphic factorization and hence cannot be factorized as a tensor product of a chiral and an anti-chiral CFT. Thus to give a mathematical formulation of such CFTs, we want to treat the chiral and anti-chiral algebras on equal footing. This motivates the terminology
non-chiral vertex operator algebras
defined in this paper.

1.1.2
Non-chiral vertex operator algebras

We now briefly describe the notion of non-chiral vertex operator algebras studied in this paper. We refer to Section
2
for precise and detailed discussions. Just as vertex operator algebras, we start with a vector space
V
which is
(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R})
-graded. We have the vertex operator map
Y_{V}:V\longrightarrow\text{End}(V)\{x,\bar{x}\}
where
x,\bar{x}
are two formal variables. We require the existence of a vacuum vector
1
and conformal vectors
\omega,\bar{\omega}
such that
Y_{V}(\textbf{1},x,\bar{x})=\mathds{1}
and the coefficients of the formal series for
Y_{V}(\omega,x,\bar{x}),Y_{V}(\bar{\omega},x,\bar{x})
satisfies two copies of the Virasoro algebra. The vertex operators are required to satisfy certain translation and
L(0)
axioms similar to VOAs. It turns out that that the Jacobi identity for VOAs is difficult to formulate for non-chiral VOAs
Moriwaki:2020cxf
. The appropriate locality axiom is motivated from full field algebras of
Huang:2005gz
. Roughly speaking, locality of vertex operators says that the matrix elements of the product
Y_{V}(v,z_{1},\bar{z}_{1})Y_{V}(w,z_{2},\bar{z}_{2})
and
Y_{V}(v,z_{1},\bar{z}_{1})Y_{V}(w,z_{2},\bar{z}_{2})
, defined when
|z_{1}|>|z_{2}|
and
|z_{2}|>|z_{1}|
respectively, are equal to the same function, which is multi-valued and analytic in
z_{1},\bar{z}_{1},z_{2},\bar{z}_{2}
and single valued when
\bar{z}_{1},\bar{z}_{2}
are complex-conjugates of
z_{1},z_{2}
, defined on
\mathbb{C}^{4}
minus a diagonal subset. This version of locality turns into a statement of analytic continuation of matrix elements for chiral vertex operators and allows us to use contour integration for manipulating the modes of the vertex operators. Moreover, locality allows us to prove
duality
of vertex operators which in turn gives us the operator product expansion of the product of two vertex operators. Modules and intertwiners of a non-chiral VOA are then defined analogous to modules of a VOA.

1.1.3
The Dictionary

Let us now describe the dictionary between non-chiral VOA and its modules and a non-chiral CFT. Given a bosonic CFT, its chiral and anti-chiral algebra is a non-chiral VOA according to our definition. Note that our notion of non-chiral VOA allows for more general structure in the sense that the chiral algebra of a CFT always has the structure of a tensor product
\mathscr{H}_{0}\otimes\overline{\mathscr{H}}_{0}
as described above but non-chiral VOAs are allowed to have more general vector spaces. The chiral and anti-chiral operators are identified with the vertex operators of the VOA. For general non-chiral VOA, chiral and anti-chiral vertex operators form only a subsector of the set of vertex operators, see Definition
2.3
and Theorem
2.3
below.
Next, the irreducibles
\mathscr{H}_{i}\otimes\overline{\mathscr{H}}_{\bar{i}}
must be identified with modules of the VOA. Again in our generic construction we allow for the modules to have more general structure rather than a tensor product. The chiral primary field corresponding to an irreducible
\mathscr{H}_{i}\otimes\overline{\mathscr{H}}_{\bar{i}}
is identified with the intertwiner
\mathcal{Y}^{\penalty\ (i,\bar{i})}_{(i,\bar{i})(0,0)}(w,x,\bar{x})
of type
{(i,\bar{i})\choose(i,\bar{i})(0,0)}
where
(0,0)
indicates the VOA as a module for itself. The state-operator correspondence for the space (
7
) corresponds to the vertex operator map for the VOA and its modules along with the intertwining operators of type
{(i,\bar{i})\choose(i,\bar{i})(0,0)}
. The full dictionary is summarised in Table
1
. We hope to expand the dictionary to include fusion rules and rationality on the CFT side to the notion of tensor product of modules and (strong) regularity of non-chiral VOAs in a future publication.

1.2
Lorentzian Lattice Vertex Operator Algebra (LLVOA)

One of the main constructions of this paper is a concrete example of a non-chiral VOA based on an even integral Lorentzian lattice. The construction is similar to Euclidean lattice VOAs but differs in that it also has anti-holomorphic part. We call it the Lorentzian lattice vertex operator algebra (LLVOA). The modules for a non-chiral VOA introduced in this paper can be defined analogous to modules for usual VOAs. In fact, we are able to construct modules for the LLVOA in one to one correspondence with certain cosets of the lattice. Collecting the VOA and its modules, we define the partition function of the non-chiral CFT (see Definition
4.3
) thus obtained. We call such CFTs
Lorentzian lattice conformal field theory
(LLCFT). We find that the modules of the LLVOA constructed using the cosets of the lattice give rise to a modular invariant partition function. We further attempt to classify all possible modular invariant LLCFTs based on even, integral self-dual Lorentzian lattices of a given signature. This leads us to a conjecture about automorphisms of Lorentzian lattices which we prove for signature
(m,m)
but are unable to prove for general signature
(m,n)
with
m\neq n
. Following the physics terminology, we call the equivalence classes of LLCFTs based on Lorentzian lattices as the moduli space of LLCFTs. As expected from physical arguments, the moduli space in signature
(m,n)
is given by (see Theorem
5.2
)

\mathcal{M}_{m,n}\cong\frac{\mathrm{O}(m,n,\mathbb{R})}{\mathrm{O}(m,\mathbb{R})\times\mathrm{O}(n,\mathbb{R})\times\mathrm{O}(m,n,\mathbb{Z})}.

(20)

The paper is organised as follows: in Section
2
, we introduce the notion of non-chiral VOA and prove some elementary consequences of the definition. Then in Section
3
, we construct the LLVOA and prove that it is an example of a non-chiral VOA. In Section
4
we define the notion of modules and intertwining operators and prove some important consequences and results required later. In Section
5
we construct the modules of LLVOAs. We formulate a precise conjecture about automorphism of Lorentzian lattices and under that assumption, prove that the moduli space of LLCFTs in signature
(m,n)
is given by (
20
). Finally in Section
6
we review the physical construction of Narain CFTs and comment on their relation to LLVOAs. Appendix
A
deals with the independence of central extensions of lattices on the chosen basis of the lattice. Appendix
C
contains some technical results about modules of Heisenberg algebras and Appendix
D
contains the proof of Conjecture
3.3.1
for the special case
m=n
.

2
Non-Chiral Vertex Operator Algebra

2.1
Formal calculus

We begin by collecting some notatations about formal calculus. The reader is referred to
(
Frenkel:1988xz
, Chapter 3,8)
and
(
lepowsky2012introduction
, Chapter 2)
for more details.

Let
x
be a formal variable. For a vector space
V
, we define the following:

V[x]=\left\{\,\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}v_{n}x^{n}:v_{n}\in V,\text{ where only finitely many $v_{n}\neq 0$}\right\}\,,

(21)

V[[x]]=\left\{\,\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}v_{n}x^{n}:v_{n}\in V\right\}\,,

(22)

V\{x\}=\left\{\sum_{n\in\mathbb{F}}v_{n}x^{n}\,:\,v_{n}\in V\right\}\,,

(23)

where
\mathbb{N}_{0}=\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
and
\mathbb{F}
is an arbitrary field of characteristic not 2. We will mostly be interested in the case
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{R}
or
\mathbb{C}
. For a complex number
s\in\mathbb{C}
and formal variables
x_{1},x_{2}
, we will define

(x_{1}+x_{2})^{s}:=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{s\choose n}x_{1}^{s-n}x_{2}^{n}\penalty\ ,

(24)

where the binomial coefficient is defined as

{s\choose n}=\frac{s(s-1)\cdots(s-n+1)}{n!}.

(25)

Note, in a series like this, we will always have non-negative integral powers of the second variable.
With this formula, one can check that

\begin{split}\left(1-\frac{x_{1}}{x_{2}}\right)^{s}x_{2}^{s}&=(x_{2}-x_{1})^{s}.\end{split}

(26)

If we replace
x_{1},x_{2}
by complex variables
z_{1},z_{2}
then by definition
stein2010complex

\begin{split}&(z_{1}-z_{2})^{s}:=\exp(s\log(z_1-z_2)),\\
&(z_{2}-z_{1})^{s}:=\exp(s\log(z_2-z_1)).\end{split}

(27)

Using the fact that

\log(1-z)=-\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{z^{n}}{n},\quad|z|<1,

(28)

and the identity
8
8

8

This identity can be proven by using the relation
(1-x)^{s}=\exp(s\log(1-x))
for any real
x
with
|x|<1
and
s\in\mathbb{C}
.

(-1)^{k}{s\choose k}=\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}\frac{(-s)^{\ell}}{\ell!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1}+\dots+n_{\ell}=k\\
n_{1},\dots,n_{\ell}\geq 1\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{n_{1}\cdots n_{\ell}},

(29)

it is easy to see that
9
9

9

Another way of proving this identity is to use Taylor’s theorem.

\begin{split}&(z_{1}-z_{2})^{s}=\exp(s\log(z_1-z_2))=\sum_{n\geq 0}{s\choose n}(-1)^{n}z_{1}^{s-n}z_{2}^{n},\quad|z_{1}|>|z_{2}|\penalty\ ,\\
&(z_{2}-z_{1})^{s}=\exp(s\log(z_2-z_1))=\sum_{n\geq 0}{s\choose n}(-1)^{n}z_{2}^{s-n}z_{1}^{n},\quad|z_{2}|>|z_{1}|\penalty\ ,\end{split}

(30)

which is consistent with the definition (
24
).
Let
f(x)=\sum v_{n}x^{n}\,\in V[[x,x^{-1}]]
, then we have the formal version of Taylor’s theorem:

\mathrm{e}^{x_{0}\,\frac{d}{dx}}\,f(x)=f(x+x_{0})\,,

(31)

One can prove this by expanding both sides and comparing terms of equal power of
x,x_{0}
. As before, we need to expand the RHS in non-negative integral powers of
x_{0}
.

2.2
Definition of non-chiral VOA

Let

V=\coprod\limits_{(h,\bar{h})\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}}V_{(h,\bar{h})}\,,

(32)

be an
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}
-graded complex vector space vector space. Let

\overline{V}=\prod\limits_{(h,\bar{h})\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}}V_{(h,\bar{h})}\,,

(33)

denote the algebraic completion of
V
. Let

V^{\prime}=\coprod\limits_{(h,\bar{h})\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}}V^{\prime}_{(h,\bar{h})}\,,

(34)

be the contragradient of
V
where
V^{\prime}_{(h,\bar{h})}
is the dual of
V_{(h,\bar{h})}
. A series
\sum f_{n}
in
\overline{V}
is said to be absolutely convergent if for every
f^{\prime}\in V^{\prime}
the series
\sum|\langle f^{\prime},f_{n}\rangle|
is convergent. Here,
\langle f^{\prime},f_{n}\rangle=f^{\prime}(f_{n})\in\mathbb{C}
is just the action of the linear functional on
f^{\prime}
on
f_{n}
.

{defn}

A
non-chiral vertex operator algebra
of
central charge

(c,\bar{c})
is a quintuple
(V,Y_{V},\omega,\bar{\omega},\mathbf{1})
where
V
is an
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}
-graded complex vector space and
Y_{V}
is a linear map, called the
vertex operator map
,

\begin{split}Y_{V}:\,&V\otimes V\longrightarrow V\{x,\bar{x}\}\,,\\
&u\otimes v\longmapsto Y_{V}(u,x,\bar{x})v\,,\end{split}

(35)

or equivalently a map

\begin{split}Y_{V}:\,&\mathbb{C}^{\times}\times\mathbb{C}^{\times}\longrightarrow\text{Hom}(V\otimes V,\overline{V})\\
&(z,\bar{z})\longmapsto Y_{V}(\cdot,z,\bar{z}):u\otimes v\longmapsto Y_{V}(u,z,\bar{z})v,\end{split}

(36)

which is multi-valued and analytic if
z,\bar{z}
are independent complex variables and single valued when
\bar{z}
is the complex conjugate of
z
. The vertex operator
Y_{V}(u,x,\bar{x})
is expanded as a formal power series

Y_{V}(u,x,\bar{x})=\sum_{m,n\in\mathbb{R}}u_{m,n}x^{-m-1}\bar{x}^{-n-1}\in\text{End}(V)\{x^{\pm 1},\bar{x}^{\pm 1}\}\,,

(37)

and when
u\in V_{(h,\bar{h})}
, it can also be expanded as

Y_{V}(u,x,\bar{x})=\sum_{m,n\in\mathbb{R}}x_{m,n}(u)x^{-m-h}\bar{x}^{-n-\bar{h}}\in\text{End}(V)\{x^{\pm 1},\bar{x}^{\pm 1}\}\,,

(38)

so that

x_{m,n}(u)=u_{m+h-1,n+\bar{h}-1},\quad m,n\in\mathbb{Z}\,.

(39)

We call
x_{m,n}(u)
the
modes
of the vertex operators
Y_{V}(u,x,\bar{x})
.
The degree
(h,\bar{h})
is called the
conformal weight
of
u\in V_{(h,\bar{h})}
and we write

\text{wt}(u)=h,\quad\overline{\text{wt}}(u)=\bar{h}\,.

(40)

The vector

\mathbf{1}\in V_{(0,0)}
is called the
vacuum vector
and
\omega\in V_{(2,0)},\bar{\omega}\in V_{(0,2)}
are
chiral
and
anti-chiral conformal vectors
respectively. This data is required to satisfy the following properties:

1.

Identity property:
The vertex operator corresponding to the vacuum vector acts as identity, i.e.

Y_{V}(\mathbf{1},x,\bar{x})u=u,\quad\forall\penalty\ u\in V.

(41)

2.

Grading-restriction property:
For every
(h,\bar{h})\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}
,

\text{dim}(V_{(h,\bar{h})})<\infty\,,

(42)

and there exists
M\in\mathbb{R}
, such that

V_{(h,\bar{h})}=0,\quad\text{ for }h<M\text{ or }\bar{h}<M.

(43)

3.

Single-valuedness property:
For every homogenous subspace
V_{(h,\bar{h})}

h-\bar{h}\in\mathbb{Z}.

(44)

4.

Creation property:
For any
v\in V

\lim_{x,\bar{x}\to 0}Y_{V}(v,x,\bar{x})\mathbf{1}=v\penalty\ ,

(45)

that is
Y_{V}(v,x,\bar{x})\mathbf{1}
involves only non-negative powers of
x,\bar{x}
and the constant term is
v
.

5.

Virasoro property:
The vertex operators
Y_{V}(\omega,x,\bar{x})
and
Y_{V}(\bar{\omega},x,\bar{x})
, called
conformal vertex operators
, have Laurent series in
x,\bar{x}
given by

\begin{split}&T(x):=Y_{V}(\omega,x,\bar{x})=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}L(n)x^{-n-2},\\
&\bar{T}(\bar{x}):=Y_{V}(\bar{\omega},x,\bar{x})=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\bar{L}(n)\bar{x}^{-n-2},\end{split}

(46)

where
L(n),\bar{L}(n)
are operators which satisfy the
Virasoro algebra
with central charge
c,\bar{c}
respectively:

\begin{split}&\left[L(m),L(n)\right]=(m-n)L(m+n)+\frac{c}{12}m\left(m^{2}-1\right)\delta_{m+n,0},\\
&\left[\bar{L}(m),\bar{L}(n)\right]=(m-n)\bar{L}(m+n)+\frac{\bar{c}}{12}m\left(m^{2}-1\right)\delta_{m+n,0},\end{split}

(47)

and

\left[L(m),\bar{L}(n)\right]=0.

(48)

6.

Grading property:
The operator
(L(0),\bar{L}(0))
is the grading operator on
V
, that is for
v\in V_{(h,\bar{h})}

L(0)v=hv,\quad\bar{L}(0)v=\bar{h}v.

(49)

7.

L(0)
-property
:

\begin{split}&[L(0),Y_{V}(u,x,\bar{x})]=x\frac{\partial}{\partial x}Y_{V}(u,x,\bar{x})+Y_{V}(L(0)u,x,\bar{x}),\\
&[\bar{L}(0),Y_{V}(u,x,\bar{x})]=\bar{x}\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{x}}Y_{V}(u,x,\bar{x})+Y_{V}(\bar{L}(0)u,x,\bar{x}).\end{split}

(50)

8.

Translation property:
For any
u\in V

\begin{split}&{\left[L(-1),Y_{V}(u,x,\bar{x})\right]=Y_{V}\left(L(-1)u,x,\bar{x}\right)=\frac{\partial}{\partial x}Y_{V}(u,x,\bar{x}),}\\
&{\left[\bar{L}(-1),Y_{V}(u,x,\bar{x})\right]=Y_{V}\left(\bar{L}(-1)u,x,\bar{x}\right)=\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{x}}Y_{V}(u,x,\bar{x})}.\\
&\end{split}

(51)

9.

Locality property:

For
u_{1},\dots,u_{n}\in V
, there is an operator-valued function
10
10

10

We thank Yi-Zhi Huang for discussions on this point.

m_{n}(u_{1},...,u_{n};z_{1},\bar{z}_{1},...,z_{n},\bar{z}_{n})\penalty\ ,

defined on
11
11

11

The matrix elements of this operator are called
correlation functions
in Physics.

\{(z_{1},\dots,z_{n},\bar{z}_{1},\dots,\bar{z}_{n})\in\mathbb{C}^{2n}\,\lvert\,z_{i},\bar{z}_{i}\neq 0,z_{i}\neq z_{j},\bar{z}_{i}\neq\bar{z}_{j}\}\,,

(52)

which is multi-valued and analytic
when
\bar{z}_{1},...,\bar{z}_{n}
are viewed as independent variables and is single-valued when
\bar{z}_{1},...,\bar{z}_{n}
are equal to the complex conjugates of
z_{1},...,z_{n}
. Moreover, for any permutation
\sigma\in S_{n}
, the product of vertex operators

Y_{V}\left(u_{\sigma(1)},z_{\sigma(1)},\bar{z}_{\sigma(1)}\right)\cdots Y_{V}\left(u_{\sigma(n)},z_{\sigma(n)},\bar{z}_{\sigma(n)}\right)\penalty\ ,

(53)

is the expansion of
m_{n}\left(u_{1},\dots,u_{n};z_{1},\bar{z}_{1},\dots,z_{n},\bar{z}_{n}\right)

in the domain

\left|z_{\sigma(1)}\right|>\left|z_{\sigma(2)}\right|>\dots>|z_{\sigma(n)}|>0
. Here,
\bar{z}_{\sigma(1)},...,\bar{z}_{\sigma(n)}
are complex conjugates of
z_{\sigma(1)},...,z_{\sigma(n)}
respectively. If a function
m_{n}
satisfying above properties exists, we say that the vertex operators

Y_{V}\left(u_{1},z_{1},\bar{z}_{1}\right),\dots,Y_{V}\left(u_{n},z_{n},\bar{z}_{n}\right)
are mutually
local
with respect to each other.

We will often denote the non-chiral VOA by
(V,Y_{V})
or simply by
V
.

{remark}

Let us compare the definition of non-chiral VOA with full vertex algebra of
Moriwaki:2020cxf
. The definition of full vertex algebra in
Moriwaki:2020cxf
requires the locality of only 2-point correlation function and invokes
bootstrap equations
for the locality of multi-point correlation functions. In principal, requiring bootstrap equations imply the locality of multi-point correlation functions. We take the latter approach to be completely explicit.

{remark}

For a homogeneous vector
u\in V_{(h,\bar{h})}
, the sum in (
37
) and (
38
) runs only over the set
\{(m,n)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\lvert\penalty\ m-n\in\mathbb{Z}\}
. To see this, first note that the
L(0)
-property
7
implies the commutator

\begin{split}&\left[L(0),x_{m,n}(u)\right]=-mx_{m,n}(u),\\
&\left[\bar{L}(0),x_{m,n}(u)\right]=-nx_{m,n}(u).\end{split}

(54)

Equivalently,

\begin{split}&\left[L(0),u_{m,n}\right]=(h-m-1)u_{m,n},\\
&\left[\bar{L}(0),u_{m,n}\right]=(\bar{h}-n-1)u_{m,n}.\end{split}

(55)

This implies that

\begin{split}&\text{wt}\penalty\ x_{m,n}(u)=-m,\quad\overline{\text{wt}}\penalty\ x_{m,n}(u)=-n,\\
&\text{wt}\penalty\ u_{m,n}=h-m-1,\quad\overline{\text{wt}}\penalty\ u_{m,n}=\bar{h}-n-1.\end{split}

(56)

The single-valuedness property
3
implies that
m-n\in\mathbb{Z}
in both the sums. We will thus write the expansions of the vertex operators as

\begin{split}Y_{V}(u,x,\bar{x})&=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m,n\in\mathbb{R}\\
(m-n)\in\mathbb{Z}\end{subarray}}u_{m,n}x^{-m-1}\bar{x}^{-n-1}\in\text{End}(V)\{x,\bar{x}\}\\
&=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m,n\in\mathbb{R}\\
(m-n)\in\mathbb{Z}\end{subarray}}x_{m,n}(u)x^{-m-h}\bar{x}^{-n-\bar{h}}.\end{split}

(57)

{remark}

The single-valuedness property
3
implies that the vertex operators (
35
) is single-valued. To prove this, we must show that

Y_{V}(u,z,\bar{z})=Y_{V}(u,e^{2\pi i}z,e^{-2\pi i}\bar{z}).

(58)

From Remark
2.2
, we have

\begin{split}Y_{V}(u,e^{2\pi i}z,e^{-2\pi i}\bar{z})&=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m,n\in\mathbb{R}\\
(m-n)\in\mathbb{Z}\end{subarray}}u_{m,n}z^{-m-1}\bar{z}^{-n-1}e^{2\pi i(-m+n)}\\
&=Y_{V}(u,z,\bar{z}).\end{split}

(59)

{remark}

For
v\in V_{(h,\bar{h})}
, if in the expansion (
38
) the index runs over
m\in\mathbb{Z}-h,\penalty\ n\in\mathbb{Z}-\bar{h}
, then assuming that
z,\bar{z}
are independent complex variables, we can use Cauchy’s residue theorem to write

x_{r,s}(v)=\frac{1}{(2\pi i)^{2}}\oint\oint dzd\bar{z}\penalty\ Y_{V}(v,z,\bar{z})z^{r+h-1}\bar{z}^{s+\bar{h}-1}\penalty\ ,

(60)

where the contour of the integration is a circle around
z=0
and
\bar{z}=0
respectively.

{remark}

The creation property implies also the
injectivity
condition, i.e.

Y_{V}(v,x,\bar{x})=0\text{ implies }v=0,\text{ for }v\in V\,.

(61)

2.3
Some consequences of the definition

We now prove some consequences of the definition. These are well-known for VOAs, see
Frenkel:1988xz
;
Frenkel:1993xz
.

{lemma}

For any
v\in V
, we have

Y_{V}(v,x,\bar{x})\mathbf{1}=\mathrm{e}^{\bar{x}\,\bar{L}(-1)}\mathrm{e}^{x\,L(-1)}v\,.

(62)

{proof}

We use the translation property
8
and Taylor’s theorem (
31
). For another formal variable
x_{0},\bar{x}_{0}
, Taylor’s theorem gives

Y_{V}\left(\mathrm{e}^{xL(-1)}\mathrm{e}^{\bar{x}\bar{L}(-1)}v,x_{0},\bar{x}_{0}\right)=\mathrm{e}^{x\frac{d}{dx}}\mathrm{e}^{\bar{x}\frac{d}{d\bar{x}}}Y_{V}(v,x_{0},\bar{x}_{0})=Y_{V}(v,x+x_{0},\bar{x}+\bar{x}_{0})\,.

(63)

Now applying this operator on
\mathbf{1}
, taking limit
x_{0},\bar{x}_{0}\to 0
we get

\lim_{x_{0},\bar{x}_{0}\to 0}Y_{V}\left(\mathrm{e}^{xL(-1)}\mathrm{e}^{\bar{x}\bar{L}(-1)}v,x_{0},\bar{x}_{0}\right)\mathbf{1}=\lim_{x_{0},\bar{x}_{0}\to 0}Y_{V}(v,x+x_{0},\bar{x}+\bar{x}_{0})\mathbf{1}\,,

(64)

and then using (
45
) we obtain (
62
).

{lemma}

For any
v\in V
we have

{\rm e}^{x_{2}L(-1)}{\rm e}^{\bar{x}_{2}\bar{L}(-1)}Y_{V}(v,x_{1},\bar{x}_{1}){\rm e}^{-x_{2}L(-1)}{\rm e}^{-\bar{x}_{2}\bar{L}(-1)}=Y_{V}(v,x_{1}+x_{2},\bar{x}_{1}+\bar{x_{2}})\,.

(65)

{proof}

Using the BCH formula (
224
) and translation property
8
, we have

\begin{split}{\rm e}^{x_{2}L(-1)}Y_{V}(v,x_{1},\bar{x}_{1}){\rm e}^{-x_{2}L(-1)}&=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{\left[(x_{2}\,L(-1))^{n},Y_{V}(v,x_{1},\bar{x}_{1})\right]}{n!}\,,\\
&=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}x_{2}^{n}\frac{\partial^{n}}{\partial x_{1}^{n}}Y_{V}(v,x_{1},\bar{x}_{1})\,,\\
&={\rm e}^{x_{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{1}}}Y_{V}(v,x_{1},\bar{x}_{1})\,,\\
&=Y_{V}(v,x_{1}+x_{2},\bar{x}_{1})\,,\end{split}

(66)

where in the last step we used Taylor’s theorem (
31
). Similarly we have

{\rm e}^{\bar{x}_{2}\bar{L}(-1)}Y_{V}(v,x_{1},\bar{x}_{1}){\rm e}^{-\bar{x}_{2}\bar{L}(-1)}=Y_{V}(v,x_{1},\bar{x}_{1}+\bar{x}_{2}).

(67)

Since
L(-1)
and
\bar{L}(-1)
commute, the result follows.

We now prove
skew-symmetry
which will be useful in proving the
duality
of vertex operators.

{lemma}

For any
u,v\in V
, we have

Y_{V}(u,z,\bar{z})v=e^{zL(-1)}e^{\bar{z}\bar{L}(-1)}Y_{V}(v,-z,-\bar{z})u.

(68)

{proof}

Using Lemma locality property
9
,
2.3
and Lemma
2.3
, we have

\begin{split}Y_{V}(u,z,\bar{z})Y_{V}(v,z^{\prime},\bar{z}^{\prime})\textbf{1}&\sim Y_{V}(v,z^{\prime},\bar{z}^{\prime})Y_{V}(u,z,\bar{z})\textbf{1}\\
&=Y_{V}(v,z^{\prime},\bar{z}^{\prime})\mathrm{e}^{\bar{z}\,\bar{L}(-1)}\mathrm{e}^{z\,L(-1)}u\\
&=\mathrm{e}^{\bar{z}\,\bar{L}(-1)}\mathrm{e}^{z\,L(-1)}Y_{V}(v,z^{\prime}-z,\bar{z}^{\prime}-\bar{z})u\penalty\ .\end{split}

(69)

Now taking
z^{\prime},\bar{z}^{\prime}\to 0
and using the creation property, we obtain the required result.

The following proposition shows the uniqueness of vertex operators. The proof is on the lines of
Dolan:1994st
.

{prop}

Let
U:V\longrightarrow V\{x,\bar{x}\}
be a linear operator which is local with respect to every other vertex operator, in the sense of Property
9
, and satisfies

U(x,\bar{x})\mathbf{1}=\mathrm{e}^{\bar{x}\,\bar{L}(-1)}\mathrm{e}^{x\,L(-1)}v,

(70)

for some
v\in V
, then

U(z,\bar{z})=Y_{V}(v,z,\bar{z})\,,

(71)

for a non-zero complex number
z
.

{proof}

For any
w\in V
, from Lemma
2.3
and locality property
9
we have

\begin{split}U(z_{1},\bar{z}_{1})\mathrm{e}^{\bar{z}_{2}\,\bar{L}(-1)}\mathrm{e}^{z_{2}\,L(-1)}w&=U(z_{1},\bar{z}_{1})Y_{V}(w,z_{2},\bar{z}_{2})\mathbf{1}\,,\\
&\sim Y_{V}(w,z_{2},\bar{z}_{2})U(z_{1},\bar{z}_{1})\mathbf{1}\,,\\
&=Y_{V}(w,z_{2},\bar{z}_{2})\mathrm{e}^{\bar{z}_{1}\,\bar{L}(-1)}\mathrm{e}^{z_{1}\,L(-1)}v\,,\\
&=Y_{V}(w,z_{2},\bar{z}_{2})Y_{V}(v,z_{1},\bar{z}_{1})\mathbf{1}\,,\\
&\sim Y_{V}(v,z_{1},\bar{z}_{1})Y_{V}(w,z_{2},\bar{z}_{2})\mathbf{1}\penalty\ ,\end{split}

(72)

where
\sim
indicates equality up to analytic extension in the sense of Property
9
.
Now taking
z_{2},\bar{z}_{2}\to 0
we obtain,

U(z_{1},\bar{z}_{1})w=Y_{V}(v,z_{1},\bar{z}_{1})w.

(73)

As the two operators in (
73
) are equal for all
w\in V
, they are equal as operators.

We now prove the
duality
of vertex operators. For VOA, this is proved in
Dolan:1994st
;
Huang:2015eda
.

{prop}

For any
v,w\in V
we have

Y_{V}(v,z_{1},\bar{z}_{1})Y_{V}(w,z_{2},\bar{z}_{2})=Y_{V}(Y_{V}(v,z_{1}-z_{2},\bar{z}_{1}-\bar{z}_{2})w,z_{2},\bar{z}_{2})\,,

(74)

in the domain
|z_{1}|>|z_{2}|>|z_{1}-z_{2}|>0
,
where the RHS is defined by

Y_{V}(Y_{V}(v,z_{1}-z_{2},\bar{z}_{1}-\bar{z}_{2})w,z_{2},\bar{z}_{2})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m,n\in\mathbb{R}\\
(m-n)\in\mathbb{Z}\end{subarray}}Y_{V}(v_{m,n}\cdot w,z_{2},\bar{z}_{2})(z_{1}-z_{2})^{-m-1}(\bar{z}_{1}-\bar{z}_{2})^{-n-1}\,.

(75)

{proof}

The proof is on the lines of
(
Huang:2015eda
, Page 23)
.
For any
u\in V,
we have

\begin{split}Y_{V}(v,z_{1},\bar{z}_{1})&Y_{V}(w,z_{2},\bar{z}_{2})\mathrm{e}^{\bar{z}_{3}\,\bar{L}(-1)}\mathrm{e}^{z_{3}\,L(-1)}u\\
&=Y_{V}(v,z_{1},\bar{z}_{1})Y_{V}(w,z_{2},\bar{z}_{2})Y_{V}(u,z_{3},\bar{z}_{3})\mathbf{1}\,,\\
&\sim Y_{V}(u,z_{3},\bar{z}_{3})Y_{V}(v,z_{1},\bar{z}_{1})Y_{V}(w,z_{2},\bar{z}_{2})\mathbf{1}\,,\\
&=Y_{V}(u,z_{3},\bar{z}_{3})Y_{V}(v,z_{1},\bar{z}_{1})\mathrm{e}^{\bar{z}_{2}\,\bar{L}(-1)}\mathrm{e}^{z_{2}\,L(-1)}w\,,\\
&=Y_{V}(u,z_{3},\bar{z}_{3})\mathrm{e}^{\bar{z}_{2}\,\bar{L}(-1)}\mathrm{e}^{z_{2}\,L(-1)}Y_{V}(v,z_{1}-z_{2},\bar{z}_{1}-\bar{z}_{2})w\,,\\
&=Y_{V}(u,z_{3},\bar{z}_{3})Y_{V}\left(Y_{V}(v,z_{1}-z_{2},\bar{z}_{1}-\bar{z}_{2})w,z_{2},\bar{z}_{2}\right)\mathbf{1}\,,\\
&\sim Y_{V}\left(Y_{V}(v,z_{1}-z_{2},\bar{z}_{1}-\bar{z}_{2})w,z_{2},\bar{z}_{2}\right)Y_{V}(u,z_{3},\bar{z}_{3})\mathbf{1},\end{split}

(76)

where we used Lemma
2.3
, Lemma
2.3
, and Locality property
9
. Now taking
z_{3},\bar{z}_{3}\to 0
and using Proposition
2.3
, we obtain the duality relation. Note that the sum on the RHS of (
75
) converges. Indeed for any
u\in V
, using skew-symmetry
12
12

12

We thank Yi-Zhi Huang for clarification on this point.

(Lemma
2.3
) we have

\begin{split}Y_{V}(Y_{V}(v,&z_{1}-z_{2},\bar{z}_{1}-\bar{z}_{2})w,z_{2},\bar{z}_{2})u\\
&=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m,n\in\mathbb{R}\\
(m-n)\in\mathbb{Z}\end{subarray}}Y_{V}(v_{m,n}\cdot w,z_{2},\bar{z}_{2})(z_{1}-z_{2})^{-m-1}(\bar{z}_{1}-\bar{z}_{2})^{-n-1}u\\
&=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m,n\in\mathbb{R}\\
(m-n)\in\mathbb{Z}\end{subarray}}\mathrm{e}^{\bar{z}_{2}\,\bar{L}(-1)}\mathrm{e}^{z_{2}\,L(-1)}Y_{V}(u,-z_{2},-\bar{z}_{2})v_{m,n}\cdot w(z_{1}-z_{2})^{-m-1}(\bar{z}_{1}-\bar{z}_{2})^{-n-1}\\
&=\mathrm{e}^{\bar{z}_{2}\,\bar{L}(-1)}\mathrm{e}^{z_{2}\,L(-1)}Y_{V}(u,-z_{2},-\bar{z}_{2})\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m,n\in\mathbb{R}\\
(m-n)\in\mathbb{Z}\end{subarray}}v_{m,n}\cdot w(z_{1}-z_{2})^{-m-1}(\bar{z}_{1}-\bar{z}_{2})^{-n-1}\\
&=\mathrm{e}^{\bar{z}_{2}\,\bar{L}(-1)}\mathrm{e}^{z_{2}\,L(-1)}Y_{V}(v,-z_{2},-\bar{z}_{2})Y_{V}(u,z_{1}-z_{2},\bar{z}_{1}-\bar{z}_{2})w\penalty\ .\end{split}

(77)

Since the RHS of the last line is well defined in
|z_{2}|>|z_{1}-z_{2}|
, the operator
Y_{V}(Y_{V}(v,z_{1}-z_{2},\bar{z}_{1}-\bar{z}_{2})w,z_{2},\bar{z}_{2})
is well defined in
|z_{2}|>|z_{1}-z_{2}|
.

Proposition
2.3
shows that a product of two vertex operators can be written as a sum of single vertex operator:

Y_{V}(v,z_{1},\bar{z}_{1})Y_{V}(w,z_{2},\bar{z}_{2})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m,n\in\mathbb{R}\\
(m-n)\in\mathbb{Z}\end{subarray}}Y_{V}(v_{m,n}\cdot w,z_{2},\bar{z}_{2})(z_{1}-z_{2})^{-m-1}(\bar{z}_{1}-\bar{z}_{2})^{-n-1}.

(78)

In physics, we usually ignore the non-singular terms in the expansion above and call it the
operator product expansion
.

{remark}

The sum in the operator product expansion has finitely many terms with negative powers of
(z_{1}-z_{2})
and
(\bar{z}_{1}-\bar{z}_{2})
. To see this, we first expand the vertex operator
Y_{V}(v,x,\bar{x})
for
v\in V_{(h,\bar{h})}
as

Y_{V}(v,x,\bar{x})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m,n\in\mathbb{R}\\
(m-n)\in\mathbb{Z}\end{subarray}}x_{m,n}(v)x^{-m-h}\bar{x}^{-n-\bar{h}},

(79)

Since

\text{wt}\penalty\ x_{m,n}(v)=-m,\quad\overline{\text{wt}}\penalty\ x_{m,n}(v)=-n,

(80)

for
w\in V_{(h^{\prime},\bar{h}^{\prime})}
we have

x_{m,n}(v)\cdot w\in V_{(h^{\prime}-m,\bar{h}^{\prime}-n)}.

(81)

Due to the grading-restriction property
2
, there exists
M\in\mathbb{Z}
such that

x_{m,n}(v)\cdot w=0,\quad m,n>M.

(82)

Thus the operator product expansion is upper truncated.

{prop}

The operator product expansion of the conformal vertex operator
T(x)
with itself is given by

\begin{split}&T(x_{1})T(x_{2})=\frac{c}{2}\frac{1}{(x_{1}-x_{2})^{4}}+\frac{2\,T(x_{2})}{(x_{1}-x_{2})^{2}}+\frac{1}{(x_{1}-x_{2})}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{2}}T(x_{2})+G_{1}(x_{1},x_{2})\\
&\bar{T}(\bar{x}_{1})\bar{T}(\bar{x}_{2})=\frac{\bar{c}}{2}\frac{1}{(\bar{x}_{1}-\bar{x}_{2})^{4}}+\frac{2\,\bar{T}(\bar{x}_{2})}{(\bar{x}_{1}-\bar{x}_{2})^{2}}+\frac{1}{(\bar{x}_{1}-\bar{x}_{2})}\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{x}_{2}}\bar{T}(\bar{x}_{2})+G_{2}(\bar{x}_{1},\bar{x}_{2})\\
&T(x_{1})\bar{T}(\bar{x}_{2})=G_{3}(x_{1},\bar{x}_{2}),\end{split}

(83)

where
G_{1}(x_{1},x_{2}),G_{3}(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathrm{End}(V)[[x_{2}^{\pm 1},(x_{1}-x_{2})]],G_{2}(\bar{x}_{1},\bar{x}_{2})\in\mathrm{End}(V)[[\bar{x}_{2}^{\pm 1},(\bar{x}_{1}-\bar{x}_{2})]].

{proof}

The proof is straightforward using the Virasoro algebra (
47
), see
(
Frenkel:2004jn
, Chapter 3)
for more details.

Of particular interest are the
chiral
and
anti-chiral
vertex operators.

{defn}

A vector
u\in V
is called a
chiral
(
anti-chiral
) vector if the corresponding vertex operator
Y_{V}(u,x,\bar{x})
belongs in End
(V)\{x^{\pm 1}\}
(End
(V)\{\bar{x}^{\pm 1}\}
) or equivalently only depends on
z
(
\bar{z}
). Such vertex operators will be called chiral (anti-chiral) vertex operators.

{remark}

From the translation property
8
we see that the vertex operator corresponding to
v
is chiral if and only if
\bar{L}(-1)v=0
and anti-chiral if and only if
L(-1)v=0
. The algebra of the modes of chiral (anti-chiral) vertex operators is called the
chiral (anti-chiral) algebra
in physics, see Corollary
2.3
.

{remark}

In the locality property
9
involving a chiral (resp. anti-chiral) vertex operator
Y_{V}(u_{1},z_{1})(\text{resp. }Y_{V}(u_{1},\bar{z}_{1}))
and another vertex operator
Y_{V}(u_{2},z_{2},\bar{z}_{2})
,
we will often denote the function
m
by

R(Y_{V}(u_{1},z_{1})Y_{V}(u_{2},z_{2},\bar{z}_{2}))\quad(\text{resp. }R(Y_{V}(u_{1},\bar{z}_{1})Y_{V}(u_{2},z_{2},\bar{z}_{2})))

(84)

so that

R(Y_{V}(u_{1},z_{1})Y_{V}(u_{2},z_{2},\bar{z}_{2}))=\begin{cases}Y_{V}\left(u_{1},z_{1}\right)Y_{V}\left(u_{2},z_{2},\bar{z}_{2}\right)&\text{for }|z_{1}|>|z_{2}|,\\
Y_{V}\left(u_{2},z_{2},\bar{z}_{2}\right)Y_{V}\left(u_{1},z_{1}\right)&\text{for }|z_{2}|>|z_{1}|\end{cases}

(85)

and

R(Y_{V}(u_{1},\bar{z}_{1})Y_{V}(u_{2},z_{2},\bar{z}_{2}))=\begin{cases}Y_{V}\left(u_{1},\bar{z}_{1}\right)Y_{V}\left(u_{2},z_{2},\bar{z}_{2}\right)&\text{for }|z_{1}|>|z_{2}|,\\
Y_{V}\left(u_{2},z_{2},\bar{z}_{2}\right)Y_{V}\left(u_{1},\bar{z}_{1}\right)&\text{for }|z_{2}|>|z_{1}|\end{cases}

(86)

respectively.
In physics, this is called
radial ordering
. Here
z_{2},\bar{z}_{2}
are complex conjugates of each other.

{lemma}

Let
u,v
be homogeneous chiral and anti-chiral vector. Then the associated chiral and anti-chiral vertex operator has an expansion of the form

\begin{split}&Y_{V}(u,x)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}x_{n}(u)x^{-n-(\mathrm{wt}\,u-\overline{\mathrm{wt}}\,u)}\in\mathrm{End}(V)[[x^{\pm 1}]]\,,\\
&Y_{V}(v,\bar{x})=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\bar{x}_{n}(v)\bar{x}^{-n-(\overline{\mathrm{wt}}\,v-\mathrm{wt}\,v)}\in\mathrm{End}(V)[[\bar{x}^{\pm 1}]]\,,\end{split}

(87)

where

x_{n}(u):=x_{n-\overline{\mathrm{wt}}\,u,-\overline{\mathrm{wt}}\,u}(u),\quad\bar{x}_{n}(v):=x_{-\mathrm{wt}\,v,n-\mathrm{wt}\,v}(v).

(88)

{proof}

From the expansion (
57
), we see that
Y_{V}(u,x,\bar{x})
will be independent of
\bar{x}
if and only if

x_{m,n}(u)=0\text{ unless }n=-\overline{\mathrm{wt}}\,u\,.

(89)

But as
m-n\in\mathbb{Z}
we then have

x_{m,n}(u)=0\text{ unless }n=-\overline{\mathrm{wt}}\,u,\penalty\ m\in\mathbb{Z}-\overline{\mathrm{wt}}\,u.

(90)

This gives us the required expansion.
The proof for anti-chiral vector
v
is similar. The fact that
Y_{V}(u,x)\in\mathrm{End}(V)[[x^{\pm 1}]],Y_{V}(v,\bar{x})\in\mathrm{End}(V)[[\bar{x}^{\pm 1}]]
follows from the single-valuedness property
3
.

{remark}

By the above lemma, for chiral and anti-chiral vertex operators, the requirements in Remark
2.2
is satisfied and hence we can write

\begin{split}&x_{n}(u)=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint dz\penalty\ Y_{V}(u,z)z^{n+(\mathrm{wt}\,u-\overline{\mathrm{wt}}\,u)-1}\,,\\
&\bar{x}_{n}(v)=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint d\bar{z}\penalty\ Y_{V}(v,\bar{z})\bar{z}^{n+(\overline{\mathrm{wt}}\,v-\mathrm{wt}\,v)-1}\,,\end{split}

(91)

where
u,v
are chiral and anti-chiral vectors respectively and the contour of integration is a circle around
z=0,\bar{z}=0
respectively.

We now derive the commutator of the modes of two vertex operators and the
Borcherd’s identity

Borcherds:1983sq
.

{thm}

Let
u_{i}\in V_{(h_{i},\bar{h}_{i})}
and
u_{j}\in V_{(h_{j},h_{j}^{\prime})}

(v_{i}\in V_{(h_{i}^{\prime},\bar{h}^{\prime}_{i})}
and
v_{j}\in V_{(h_{j}^{\prime},\bar{h}^{\prime}_{j})})
be homogeneous chiral (resp. anti-chiral) vectors with corresponding vertex operators

\begin{split}&Y_{V}(u_{i},x)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}x_{n}(u_{i})x^{-n-(h_{i}-\bar{h}_{i})},\quad\quad Y_{V}(u_{j},x)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}x_{n}(u_{j})x^{-n-(h_{j}-\bar{h}_{j})},\\
&Y_{V}(v_{i},\bar{x})=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\bar{x}_{n}(v_{i})\bar{x}^{-n-(\bar{h}^{\prime}_{i}-h^{\prime}_{i})},\quad\quad Y_{V}(v_{j},\bar{x})=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\bar{x}_{n}(v_{j})\bar{x}^{-n-(\bar{h}^{\prime}_{j}-h^{\prime}_{j})}.\end{split}

(92)

Then the vectors
x_{p}(u_{i})\cdot u_{j}
and
\bar{x}_{p}(v_{k})\cdot v_{\ell}
are chiral and anti-chiral vectors respectively.
Further, we have

\begin{split}&[x_{n}(u_{i}),x_{k}(u_{j})]=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\geq-(h_{i}-\bar{h}_{i})+1\end{subarray}}{n+(h_{i}-\bar{h}_{i})-1\choose p+(h_{i}-\bar{h}_{i})-1}x_{k+n}(x_{p}(u_{i})\cdot u_{j})\,,\\
&[\bar{x}_{n}(v_{i}),\bar{x}_{k}(v_{j})]=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\geq-(\bar{h}^{\prime}_{i}-h^{\prime}_{i})+1\end{subarray}}{n+(\bar{h}^{\prime}_{i}-h^{\prime}_{i})-1\choose p+(\bar{h}^{\prime}_{i}-h^{\prime}_{i})-1}\bar{x}_{k+n}(\bar{x}_{p}(v_{i})\cdot v_{j})\,,\\
&[x_{n}(u_{i}),\bar{x}_{k}(v_{j})]=0\,.\end{split}

(93)

In particular,

\begin{split}&[L(n),x_{k}(u_{i})]=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\geq-1\end{subarray}}{n+1\choose p+1}x_{k+n}(L(p)\cdot u_{i})\,,\\
&[L(n),\bar{x}_{k}(v_{i})]=0\,,\\
&[\bar{L}(n),\bar{x}_{k}(v_{i})]=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\geq-1\end{subarray}}{n+1\choose p+1}\bar{x}_{k+n}(\bar{L}(p)\cdot v_{i})\,,\\
&[\bar{L}(n),x_{k}(u_{i})]=0\,.\end{split}

(94)

More generally, for
m\in\mathbb{Z}\,\,\text{ and }m_{+}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
we have the Borcherd’s identity:

\begin{split}\sum_{r\geq 0}{m\choose r}\Big((-1)^{r}&x_{n+m-r}(u_{i})x_{k+r}(u_{j})-(-1)^{m+r}x_{k+m-r}(u_{j})x_{n+r}(u_{i})\Big)\\
&=\sum_{p\geq 1-(h_{i}-\bar{h}_{i})}{n+(h_{i}-\bar{h}_{i})-1\choose p+(h_{i}-\bar{h}_{i})-1}x_{k+n+m+\bar{h}_{i}-\bar{h}_{j}}(x_{p+m}(u_{i})\cdot u_{j}),\end{split}

(95)

\begin{split}\sum_{r\geq 0}{m\choose r}\Big((-1)^{r}&\bar{x}_{n+m-r}(v_{i})\bar{x}_{k+r}(v_{j})-(-1)^{m+r}\bar{x}_{k+m-r}(v_{j})\bar{x}_{n+r}(v_{i})\Big)\\
&=\sum_{p\geq 1-(\bar{h}^{\prime}_{i}-h^{\prime}_{i})}{n+(\bar{h}^{\prime}_{i}-h^{\prime}_{i})-1\choose p+(\bar{h}^{\prime}_{i}-h^{\prime}_{i})-1}\bar{x}_{k+n+h^{\prime}_{i}-h^{\prime}_{j}}(\bar{x}_{p+m}(v_{i})\cdot v_{j}),\end{split}

(96)

\sum_{r\geq 0}{m_{+}\choose r}\Big((-1)^{r}x_{n+m_{+}-r}(u_{i})\bar{x}_{k+r}(v_{j})-(-1)^{m_{+}+r}\bar{x}_{k+m_{+}-r}(v_{j})x_{n+r}(u_{i})\Big)=0.

(97)

{proof}

We first show that
x_{p}(u_{i})\cdot u_{j}
and
\bar{x}_{p}(v_{k})\cdot v_{\ell}
are chiral and anti-chiral vectors respectively. Indeed by the translation property
8

[\bar{L}(-1),x_{p}(u_{i})]=0\,,

(98)

which implies that

\bar{L}(-1)\cdot(x_{p}(u_{i})\cdot u_{j})=x_{p}(u_{i})\cdot\bar{L}(-1)u_{j}=0\,.

(99)

Similarly

L(-1)\cdot(\bar{x}_{p}(v_{k})\cdot v_{\ell})=0\,.

(100)

Now, we will follow the usual contour integration procedure, see for example
(
Frenkel:2004jn
, Section 3.3.10)
. First note that

Y_{V}(u_{i},z_{1})Y_{V}(u_{j},z_{2}),Y_{V}(u_{j},z_{2})Y_{V}(u_{i},z_{1}),R(Y_{V}(u_{i},z_{1})Y_{V}(u_{j},z_{2}))\,,

(101)

are single-valued and analytic in
z_{1},z_{2}
since their partial derivative with respect to
\bar{z}_{1},\bar{z}_{2}
is zero. So we can use Cauchy’s residue theorem to integrate over
z_{1},z_{2}
on any contour. Now
let
r_{1}>r_{2}>r_{3}>0
be real numbers. Let
C^{a}_{i}(z)
denote a contour in the variable
z_{i}
, in counterclockwise direction, of radius
a
and centered around
z
. Further,
C_{i}^{r}:=C_{i}^{r}(0)
. Let
f(z_{1},z_{2})
be a rational function analytic in
z_{1},z_{2}
with poles only at
z_{1}=0,z_{2}=0,z_{1}=z_{2}
. The integrals

\begin{split}\oint_{C^{r_{2}}_{2}}dz_{2}\oint_{C^{r_{1}}_{1}}dz_{1}\penalty\ Y_{V}(u_{i},z_{1})Y_{V}(u_{j},z_{2})f(z_{1},z_{2})\quad\text{and}\\
\oint_{C^{r_{2}}_{2}}dz_{2}\oint_{C^{r_{3}}_{1}}dz_{1}\penalty\ Y_{V}(u_{j},z_{2})Y_{V}(u_{i},z_{1})f(z_{1},z_{2})\,,\end{split}

(102)

are well-defined. By the locality property
9
and the OPE (
75
), we see that

\begin{split}&\oint_{C^{r_{2}}_{2}}dz_{2}\oint_{C^{r_{1}}_{1}}dz_{1}\penalty\ Y_{V}(u_{i},z_{1})Y_{V}(u_{j},z_{2})f(z_{1},z_{2})\\
&-\oint_{C^{r_{2}}_{2}}dz_{2}\oint_{C^{r_{3}}_{1}}dz_{1}\penalty\ Y_{V}(u_{j},z_{2})Y_{V}(u_{i},z_{1})f(z_{1},z_{2})\\
&=\oint_{C^{r_{2}}_{2}}dz_{2}\oint_{C^{r_{1}}_{1}-C^{r_{3}}_{1}}dz_{1}\penalty\ R(Y_{V}(u_{i},z_{1})Y_{V}(u_{j},z_{2}))f(z_{1},z_{2})\\
&=\oint_{C^{r_{2}}_{2}}dz_{2}\oint_{C^{\delta}_{1}(z_{2})}dz_{1}\penalty\ Y_{V}(Y_{V}(u_{i},z_{1}-z_{2})u_{j},z_{2})f(z_{1},z_{2})\\
&=\oint_{C^{r_{2}}_{2}}dz_{2}\oint_{C^{\delta}_{1}(z_{2})}dz_{1}\penalty\ \sum_{p\in\mathbb{Z}}Y_{V}(x_{p}(u_{i})\cdot u_{j},z_{2})(z_{1}-z_{2})^{-p-(h_{i}-\bar{h}_{i})}f(z_{1},z_{2})\,,\end{split}

(103)

where
\delta
is some small real number, see
(
Frenkel:2004jn
, Section 3.3.10)
for details of the change in contour. If we now choose

f=z_{1}^{n+(h_{i}-\bar{h}_{i})-1}z_{2}^{k+(h_{j}-\bar{h}_{j})-1}\,,

(104)

then using (
91
) the LHS is
13
13

13

There is a factor of
(2\pi i)^{2}
which cancels on both sides, so we ignore it.

[x_{n}(u_{i}),x_{k}(u_{j})]
while
Cauchy’s residue theorem gives the RHS to be

\begin{split}\oint_{C^{r_{2}}_{2}}dz_{2}\penalty\ \sum_{p\geq-(h_{i}-\bar{h}_{i})+1}{n+(h_{i}-\bar{h}_{i})-1\choose p+(h_{i}-\bar{h}_{i})-1}&Y_{V}(x_{p}(u_{i})\cdot u_{j},z_{2})z_{2}^{k+(h_{j}-\bar{h}_{j})+n-p-1}\,,\end{split}

(105)

where we used the identity

\oint_{C^{\delta}_{1}(z_{2})}dz_{1}\penalty\ \frac{z_{1}^{n+(h_{i}-\bar{h}_{i})-1}}{(z_{1}-z_{2})^{p+(h_{i}-\bar{h}_{i})}}={n+(h_{i}-\bar{h}_{i})-1\choose p+(h_{i}-\bar{h}_{i})-1}z_{2}^{n-p}.

(106)

Note, that it is necessary that
(h_{i}-\bar{h}_{i})\in\mathbb{Z}
, which is true by the single valuedness property
3
, for (
106
) to hold.
Finally, using (
91
) and the fact that

x_{p}(u_{i}).u_{j}\in V_{h_{j}-p+\bar{h}_{i},\bar{h}_{i}+\bar{h}_{j}},

(107)

the RHS becomes

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\geq-(h_{i}-\bar{h}_{i})+1\end{subarray}}{n+(h_{i}-\bar{h}_{i})-1\choose p+(h_{i}-\bar{h}_{i})-1}x_{k+n}(x_{p}(u_{i})\cdot u_{j}).

(108)

The second commutator is similar. To prove the third commutator, note that since

\partial_{\bar{z}_{1}}R(Y_{V}(u_{i},z_{1})Y_{V}(v_{j},\bar{z}_{2}))=\partial_{z_{2}}R(Y_{V}(u_{i},z_{1})Y_{V}(v_{j},\bar{z}_{2}))=0,

(109)

R(Y_{V}(u_{i},z_{1})Y_{V}(v_{j},\bar{z}_{2}))
cannot have any dependence on
(z_{1}-z_{2})
or
(\bar{z}_{1}-\bar{z}_{2})
. Moreover, from the proof of the OPE in Proposition
2.3
, we see that it cannot also have
(z_{1}-\bar{z}_{2})
dependence as well. This implies that the contour integral on the RHS of (
103
) vanishes and we get

[x_{m}(u_{i}),\bar{x}_{n}(v_{j})]=0\,.

(110)

The three Borcherd’s identity follow by using

\begin{split}&f_{1}=z_{1}^{n+(h_{i}-\bar{h}_{i})-1}z_{2}^{k+(h_{j}-\bar{h}_{j})-1}(z_{1}-z_{2})^{m}\,,\\
&f_{2}=\bar{z}_{1}^{n+(\bar{h}^{\prime}_{i}-h^{\prime}_{i})-1}\bar{z}_{2}^{k+(\bar{h}^{\prime}_{i}-h^{\prime}_{i})-1}(\bar{z}_{1}-\bar{z}_{2})^{m}\,,\\
&f_{3}=z_{1}^{n+(h_{i}-\bar{h}_{i})-1}\bar{z}_{2}^{k+(\bar{h}_{j}-h^{\prime}_{j})-1}(z_{1}-\bar{z}_{2})^{m}\,,\end{split}

(111)

where for the second Borcherd’s identity, we need to integrate against
d\bar{z}_{1},d\bar{z}_{2}
on the curves
C_{\bar{z}_{1}}^{r_{1}},C_{\bar{z}_{2}}^{r_{2}}
respectively and for the third Borcherd’s identity, we need to integrate against
dz_{1},d\bar{z}_{2}
on the curves
C_{z_{1}}^{r_{1}},C_{\bar{z}_{2}}^{r_{2}}
respectively.

{remark}

For
n=0,-1
in (
94
) we obtain the
L(0)
-property
7
and the translation property
8
of chiral vertex operators. Note that we already used these properties in proving the OPE.

{remark}

The commutator of the modes of chiral and anti-chiral vertex operators is closed. The algebra in (
93
) thus obtained
is called the
chiral
and
anti-chiral algebra
respectively of the non-chiral VOA
(V,Y_{V})
.

{defn}

Let
(V,Y_{V})
be a non-chiral VOA with central charge
(c,\bar{c})
. The
graded dimension
or
character
of
V
is defined by

\chi_{V}(\tau,\bar{\tau}):=\text{Tr}_{V}\,q^{L(0)-\frac{c}{24}}\bar{q}^{\bar{L}(0)-\frac{\bar{c}}{24}}=\sum_{(h,\bar{h})\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}}\left(\text{dim}\,V_{(h,\bar{h})}\right)q^{h-\frac{c}{24}}\bar{q}^{\bar{h}-\frac{\bar{c}}{24}}\,,

(112)

where
q=e^{2\pi i\tau},\penalty\ \bar{q}=e^{-2\pi i\bar{\tau}}
and
\tau\in\mathbb{H}:=\{\tau=x+iy:y>0\}.

Note that the single-valuedness property implies that
\chi_{V}(\tau+1,\bar{\tau}+1)=\chi_{V}(\tau,\bar{\tau})
if

c-\bar{c}=24k\,,

(113)

for some integer
k
.

Let
(V_{1},Y_{V_{1}},\omega_{V_{1}},\bar{\omega}_{V_{1}},\mathbf{1}_{V_{1}}),(V_{2},Y_{V_{2}},\omega_{V_{2}},\bar{\omega}_{V_{2}},\mathbf{1}_{V_{2}})
be two non-chiral VOAs with the same central charge. Then a map
f:V_{1}\rightarrow V_{2}
is called a non-chiral VOA homomorphism if it is a grading-preserving linear map such that

f(Y_{V_{1}}(u,x,\bar{x})v)=Y_{V_{2}}(f(u),x,\bar{x})f(v)\text{ for }\,u,v\in V_{1}\,,

(114)

or equivalently,

f\left(u_{n,m}\cdot v\right)=f(u)_{n,m}f(v)\text{ for }u,v\in V_{1},\quad n,m\in\mathbb{R}\,,

(115)

and such that

f(\mathbf{1}_{V_{1}})=\mathbf{1}_{V_{2}},\quad f(\omega_{V_{1}})=\omega_{V_{2}},\quad f(\bar{\omega}_{V_{1}})=\bar{\omega}_{V_{2}}\,.

(116)

An isomorphism of non-chiral VOAs is a bijective homomorphism. An endomorphism of a non-chiral VOA
V
is a homomorphism from
V
to itself, and an automorphism of
V
is a bijective endomorphism. In particular, an automorphism can be defined as a linear isomorphism
f:V\to V
such that

\begin{gathered}f\circ Y_{V}(v,x,\bar{x})\circ f^{-1}=Y_{V}(f(v),x,\bar{x})\text{ for }v\in V\,,\\
f(\omega)=\omega,\quad f(\bar{\omega})=\bar{\omega}\,.\end{gathered}

(117)

It follows that
f
is grading-preserving and
f(\mathbf{1}_{V})=\mathbf{1}_{V}
.

It is easy to see that the graded dimension of isomorphic non-chiral VOAs are identical.

3
Lorentzian Lattice Vertex Operator Algebra (LLVOA)

In this section, we will construct a non-chiral vertex operator algebra corresponding to an even, integral Lorentzian lattice

\Lambda\subset\mathbb{R}^{m,n}
. In the first subsection, we recall some basic facts about Lorentzian lattices and set up the notations for the rest of the paper. We also record some results we will need later. In the next subsection, we gather the ingredients needed to construct a non-chiral vertex operator algebra, i.e. we will construct a vector space
V_{\Lambda}
associated to the lattice, a vertex operator map
Y_{V_{\Lambda}}
for this vector space, a vacuum
\mathbf{1}
, and conformal vectors
\omega_{L}
,
\omega_{R}
. In the last subsection, we will prove that
(V_{\Lambda},Y_{V_{\Lambda}},\omega_{L},\omega_{R},\mathbf{1})
is a non-chiral VOA, which we will call the Lorentzian lattice vertex operator algebra (LLVOA).

3.1
Lorentzian lattices

We begin with some basic definitions. Let
\mathbb{R}^{m}
be the Euclidean space equipped with a symmetric bilinear form
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{m}
. Let
\mathbb{R}^{m,n}
denote the
(m+n)
-dimensional vector space
\mathbb{R}^{m+n}
equipped with the symmetric bilinear form

\bm{x}\circ\bm{x}^{\prime}:=\langle\vec{x},\vec{x}^{\prime}\rangle_{m}-\langle\vec{y},\vec{y}^{\prime}\rangle_{n}\,,

(118)

where

\bm{x}=(x^{1},\dots,x^{m},y^{1},\dots,y^{n})\equiv(\vec{x},\vec{y})\,,

(119)

and similarly
\bm{x}^{\prime}
. We will omit the subscript on
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{m}
to make the notation lighter.

{defn}

1.

A
d=(m+n)
-dimensional
Lorentzian lattice
of signature
(m,n)
is a subset
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{R}^{m,n}
which is also a free
\mathbb{Z}
-module spanned by
m+n
vectors
\lambda_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{m,n},1\leq j\leq m+n
, linearly independent in
\mathbb{R}^{m,n}
. More explicitly

\Lambda=\left\{\sum_{j=1}^{m+n}n_{j}\lambda_{j}:n_{j}\in\mathbb{Z}\right\}.

(120)

\{\lambda_{j}\}_{j=1}^{m+n}
is called an
integral basis
of
\Lambda
.
When
n=0
we call
\Lambda
a
Euclidean lattice
. We will simply refer them as lattices when we do not need to specify their signature.

2.

The
dual lattice
of a lattice
\Lambda
, denoted by
\Lambda^{\star}
, is defined as

\Lambda^{\star}=\{\bm{x}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{m,n}:\bm{x}\circ\bm{x}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}\penalty\ \forall\penalty\ \bm{x}\in\Lambda\}\,.

(121)

The lattice
\Lambda
is said to be
integral
if
\Lambda\subseteq\Lambda^{\star}
, i.e.
\bm{x}\circ\bm{y}\in\mathbb{Z}
for all
\bm{x},\bm{x}^{\prime}\in\Lambda
and
self-dual
if
\Lambda=\Lambda^{\star}
. The lattice
\Lambda
is said to be
even
if

\bm{x}\circ\bm{x}=||\vec{x}||^{2}-||\vec{y}||^{2}\in 2\mathbb{Z}\,,

(122)

for all
\bm{x}=(\vec{x},\vec{y})\in\Lambda
, where
||\vec{x}||^{2}:=\langle\vec{x},\vec{x}\rangle
.

3.

A generator matrix for
\Lambda
is an
(m+n)\times(m+n)
matrix such that the
\mathbb{Z}
-span of its rows is
\Lambda
.

4.

A
lattice homomorphism
of two lattices
f:\Lambda\longrightarrow\tilde{\Lambda}
of the same signature is simply a
\mathbb{Z}
-module morphism which also preserves the bilinear form:

f(\bm{x})\circ f(\bm{x}^{\prime})=\bm{x}\circ\bm{x}^{\prime},\quad\forall\penalty\ \penalty\ \bm{x},\bm{x}^{\prime}\in\Lambda\,.

(123)

A bijective lattice homomorphism is called a lattice isomorphism.
Two lattices are said to be isomorphic if there exists a lattice isomorphism between them.

5.

An
automorphism of the lattice

\Lambda
is a lattice isomorphism from the
\Lambda
to itself. The group of all automorphisms (the group operation being composition) is called the automorphism group of
\Lambda
and denoted by Aut
(\Lambda)
.

A generator matrix for the lattice
\Lambda
in (
120
) is given by

\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}=\begin{pmatrix}\lambda_{1}^{1}&\lambda_{1}^{2}&\cdots&\lambda_{1}^{m+n}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\cdots&\vdots\\
\lambda_{m+n}^{1}&\lambda_{m+n}^{1}&\cdots&\lambda_{m+n}^{m+n}\end{pmatrix}\,,

(124)

where
\lambda_{i}=(\lambda_{i}^{1},\dots,\lambda_{i}^{m+n})
is a basis vector of
\Lambda
. It is not hard to show that two generator matrices
\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda},\mathcal{G}^{\prime}_{\Lambda}
generate the same lattice if and only if they are related by an
(m+n)\times(m+n)

unimodular matrix
14
14

14

A matrix
U
is called unimodular if det
(U)=\pm 1
.

U\in\mathrm{GL}(m+n,\mathbb{Z})
:

\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}=U\mathcal{G}^{\prime}_{\Lambda}\,.

(125)

Indeed
U
is the change of basis matrix between the primed and unprimed generator matrices since it is invertible and since it is also integral, it preserves the lattice.
If we take the symmetric bilinear form
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
on
\mathbb{R}^{m},\mathbb{R}^{n}
to be the standard inner product, that is,

\langle\vec{x},\vec{x}^{\prime}\rangle=\sum_{i=1}^{m}x^{i}x^{\prime i}\,,

(126)

where
\vec{x}=(x^{1},\dots,x^{m})\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
and similarly
\vec{x}^{\prime}
and analogous inner product on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, then a lattice isomorphism between lattices of signature
(m,n)
can be identified with an element of
\mathrm{O}(m,n,\mathbb{R})
where
\mathrm{O}(m,n,\mathbb{R})
is the group of matrices,
A
, satisfying

A^{T}g_{m,n}A=g_{m,n},\quad g_{m,n}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathds{1}_{m}&0\\
0&-\mathds{1}_{n}\end{pmatrix}\,.

(127)

We have the following theorem:

{thm}
(
serre1973course
, Chapter V)
An even, self-dual lattice of signature
(m,n)
exists if and only if
(m-n)\equiv 0\bmod 8
. Moreover, there is a unique such lattice when
n\geq 1
up to an
\mathrm{O}(m,n,\mathbb{R})
transformation.

The canonical choice of an even, self-dual lattice of signature
\mathbb{R}^{m,n}
, denoted by
\mathrm{II}_{m,n}
, is

\mathrm{II}_{m,n}=\left\{(a_{1},\dots,a_{m+n})\in\mathbb{R}^{m,n}:a_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}\text{ or }a_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2},\penalty\ \sum_{i=1}^{m+n}a_{i}\in 2\mathbb{Z}\right\}\,.

(128)

For
m+n\in 4\mathbb{Z}
, a generator matrix for this lattice is

\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{II}_{m,n}}=\begin{pmatrix}[cccc|cccccc]1&0&\cdots&0&0&0&\cdots&0&0&-1\\
0&1&\cdots&0&0&0&\cdots&0&0&-1\\
\vdots&\vdots&\cdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\cdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\\
0&0&\cdots&1&0&0&\cdots&0&0&-1\\
\hline\cr 0&0&\cdots&0&1&0&\cdots&0&0&-1\\
0&0&\cdots&0&0&1&\cdots&0&0&-1\\
\vdots&\vdots&\cdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\cdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\\
0&0&\cdots&0&0&0&\cdots&1&0&-1\\
0&0&\cdots&0&0&0&\cdots&0&0&2\\
\frac{1}{2}&\frac{1}{2}&\cdots&\frac{1}{2}&\frac{1}{2}&\frac{1}{2}&\cdots&\frac{1}{2}&\frac{1}{2}&\frac{1}{2}\end{pmatrix}\,.

(129)

We will use this lattice to elucidate many of the notations which we now introduce.
Consider a
d
-dimensional even, integral, Lorentzian lattice
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{R}^{m,n}
with Lorentzian inner product, denoted as before by
\circ
, where
m+n=d
. We will often write a vector
\lambda\in\Lambda
as
\lambda=(\alpha^{\lambda},\beta^{\lambda})
, where
\alpha^{\lambda}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
and
\beta^{\lambda}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Then we can write

\lambda_{1}\circ\lambda_{2}=\langle\alpha^{\lambda_{1}},\alpha^{\lambda_{2}}\rangle-\langle\beta^{\lambda_{1}},\beta^{\lambda_{2}}\rangle\in\mathbb{Z}.

(130)

Note that in general
\langle\alpha^{\lambda_{1}},\alpha^{\lambda_{2}}\rangle,\langle\beta^{\lambda_{1}},\beta^{\lambda_{2}}\rangle\not\in\mathbb{Z}
.
We define the
\mathbb{Z}
-modules

\begin{split}&\Lambda_{1}=\{\alpha^{\lambda}\,\lvert\,\lambda=(\alpha^{\lambda},\beta^{\lambda})\in\Lambda\text{ for some }\beta^{\lambda}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{m},\\
&\Lambda_{2}=\{\beta^{\lambda}\,\lvert\,\lambda=(\alpha^{\lambda},\beta^{\lambda})\in\Lambda\text{ for some }\alpha^{\lambda}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}.\end{split}

(131)

Let
\{\lambda_{i}\equiv(\alpha^{\lambda_{i}},\beta^{\lambda_{i}})\}_{i=1}^{d}
be a basis of
\Lambda
. Then it is easy to see that

\Lambda_{1}=\text{Span}_{\mathbb{Z}}\{\alpha^{\lambda_{i}}\}_{i=1}^{d},\quad\Lambda_{2}=\text{Span}_{\mathbb{Z}}\{\beta^{\lambda_{i}}\}_{i=1}^{d}.

(132)

Note that in general
\Lambda_{1}
and
\Lambda_{2}
are not lattices, they are just finitely generated
\mathbb{Z}
modules possibly with non-trivial torsion. For the lattice
\mathrm{II}_{m,n}
in (
129
), it is easy to see that

\begin{split}(\mathrm{II}_{m,n})_{1}=\mathbb{Z}^{m}\bigcup\left(\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}\right)^{m},\\
(\mathrm{II}_{m,n})_{2}=\mathbb{Z}^{n}\bigcup\left(\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}\right)^{n}.\end{split}

(133)

We further identify even, integral, Euclidean sublattices of
\Lambda
as follows:

\begin{split}&\Lambda_{1}^{0}:=\{(\alpha,0)\in\Lambda\,\lvert\,\alpha\in\mathbb{R}^{m}\},\\
&\Lambda_{2}^{0}:=\{(0,\beta)\in\Lambda\,\lvert\,\beta\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\}\,.\end{split}

(134)

These can be identified naturally with submodules of
\Lambda_{1}
and
\Lambda_{2}
respectively. Clearly,
\Lambda_{1}^{0},\Lambda_{2}^{0}
are sublattices of
\Lambda
since any submodule of a finitely generated free module over a principal ideal domain is free.
We also introduce the notation

\Lambda_{0}:=\Lambda_{1}^{0}\oplus\Lambda_{2}^{0}\,.

(135)

Note that the direct sum of
\Lambda_{1}^{0}
and
\Lambda_{2}^{0}
is meaningful as they are two
\mathbb{Z}
-modules. For the lattice
\mathrm{II}_{m,n}
in (
129
),
(\mathrm{II}_{m,n})_{0}=(\mathrm{II}_{m,n})_{1}^{0}\oplus(\mathrm{II}_{m,n})_{2}^{0}
is easily seen to be generated by

\mathcal{G}_{(\mathrm{II}_{m,n})_{0}}:=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathcal{G}_{m}&0_{m\times n}\\
0_{n\times m}&\mathcal{G}_{n}\,,\end{array}\right)

(136)

where

\mathcal{G}_{m}:=\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0&\cdots&0&-1\\
0&1&0&\cdots&0&-1\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\cdots&\vdots&\vdots\\
0&0&0&\cdots&1&-1\\
0&0&0&\cdots&0&2\end{pmatrix}_{m\times m}\,,

(137)

and
\mathcal{G}_{n}
is defined similarly.
It is useful to characterize the automorphisms of
\Lambda
. We will take the symmetric bilinear form on
\mathbb{R}^{m},\mathbb{R}^{n}
to be the standard inner product for brevity. We have the following important result.

{thm}

Let
\Lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{m,n}
be an integral Lorentzian lattice. Then
\mathrm{Aut}(\Lambda)\cong\mathrm{O}_{\Lambda}(m,n,\mathbb{Z})
where

\mathrm{O}_{\Lambda}(m,n,\mathbb{Z}):=\{A\in\mathrm{GL}(m+n,\mathbb{Z}):\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}^{-1}A\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}\in\mathrm{O}(m,n,\mathbb{R})\}\,.

(138)

{proof}

Choose an integral basis
\{\lambda_{i}\}
of
\Lambda
. Then the group of
\mathbb{Z}
-module automorphisms of
\Lambda
can be identified with
\mathrm{GL}(m+n,\mathbb{Z})
. Now given any
\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}\in\Lambda
there exists coulumn vectors
\vec{n},\vec{n}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m+n}
such that
\lambda=\vec{n}^{T}\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}
and
\lambda^{\prime}=\vec{n}^{{}^{\prime}T}\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}
. Any module automorphism
A\in\mathrm{GL}(m+n,\mathbb{Z})
acts by

A(\lambda)=\vec{n}^{T}A\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}\,.

(139)

For
A
to preserve inner product, we must have

\begin{split}A(\lambda)\circ A(\lambda^{\prime})&=\vec{n}^{T}A\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}g_{m,n}\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}^{T}A^{T}\vec{n}^{\prime}\,,\\
&=\vec{n}^{T}\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}g_{m,n}\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}^{T}\vec{n}^{\prime}\,,\end{split}

(140)

which implies

A\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}g_{m,n}\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}^{T}A^{T}=\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}g_{m,n}\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}^{T}\,.

(141)

Since
\{\lambda_{i}\}
is a basis for
\mathbb{R}^{m,n}
and
A
must preserve the inner products of
\lambda_{i}
’s, we must have that
A=\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}O\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}^{-1}
for some
O\in\mathrm{O}(m,n,\mathbb{R})
.

3.2
Construction of the LLVOA

Let
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{R}^{m,n}
be a
d=(m+n)
-dimensional Lorentzian lattice.
We denote by
\mathbb{C}[\Lambda]
the group algebra of the lattice
\Lambda
and denote the element
\lambda\in\Lambda
embedded in
\mathbb{C}[\Lambda]
by
{\rm e}^{\lambda}
. The multiplication in
\mathbb{C}[\Lambda]
is defined
15
15

15

Technically speaking,
\mathbb{C}[\Lambda]
is the group algebra of the formal group
{\rm e}^{\Lambda}:=\{{\rm e}^{\lambda}:\lambda\in\Lambda\}
with group multiplication given by
{\rm e}^{\lambda_{1}}\cdot{\rm e}^{\lambda_{2}}={\rm e}^{\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}}
, i.e.
\mathbb{C}[\Lambda]=\mathbb{C}[{\rm e}^{\Lambda}]
by

{\rm e}^{\lambda_{1}}\cdot{\rm e}^{\lambda_{2}}={\rm e}^{\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}}\,.

(142)

Define the vector space

\mathfrak{h}_{i}:=\Lambda_{i}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{C},\quad i=1,2\,,

(143)

and extend the bilinear form on
\Lambda_{i}
to
\mathfrak{h}_{i}

\mathbb{C}
-linearly. Here
\Lambda_{i}
is defined as in (
131
).
We define the Lie algebra

\begin{split}\hat{\mathfrak{h}}&:=\left(\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}r,s\in\mathbb{Z}\end{subarray}}(\mathfrak{h}_{1}\otimes t^{r})\oplus(\mathfrak{h}_{2}\otimes\bar{t}^{\,s})\right)\oplus(\mathbb{C}\textbf{k}\oplus\mathbb{C}\bar{\textbf{k}}).\end{split}

(144)

Introduce the notation

\alpha(r):=\alpha\otimes t^{r},\quad\beta(s):=\beta\otimes\bar{t}^{\,s},\quad\alpha\in\mathfrak{h}_{1},\beta\in\mathfrak{h}_{2}.

(145)

The non-zero Lie bracket on
\hat{\mathfrak{h}}
is

\begin{split}[\,\alpha(r_{1}),\alpha^{\prime}(r_{2})\,]&=r_{1}\,\left\langle\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\right\rangle\,\delta_{r_{1}+r_{2},0}\,\textbf{k},\\
[\,\beta(s_{1}),\beta^{\prime}(s_{2})\,]&=s_{1}\,\left\langle\beta,\beta^{\prime}\right\rangle\,\delta_{s_{1}+s_{2},0}\,\bar{\textbf{k}}\ .\end{split}

(146)

Note that

\hat{\mathfrak{h}}=\hat{\mathfrak{h}}_{1}^{\star}\oplus\hat{\mathfrak{h}}_{2}^{\star}\oplus\hat{\mathfrak{h}}_{1}^{0}\oplus\hat{\mathfrak{h}}_{2}^{0}\,,

(147)

where
\hat{\mathfrak{h}}_{1}^{\star},\hat{\mathfrak{h}}_{2}^{\star}
are the standard Heisenberg algebras associated to the abelian Lie algebras
\mathfrak{h}_{1},\mathfrak{h}_{2}
respectively
(
Frenkel:1988xz
, Chapter 1)
and

\hat{\mathfrak{h}}_{1}^{0}:=\mathfrak{h}_{1}\otimes t^{0}\cong\mathfrak{h}_{1},\quad\hat{\mathfrak{h}}_{2}^{0}:=\mathfrak{h}_{2}\otimes\bar{t}^{0}\cong\mathfrak{h}_{2}.

(148)

Define

\begin{split}\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{-}:=\left(\bigoplus_{r,s<0}(\mathfrak{h}_{1}\otimes t^{r})\oplus(\mathfrak{h}_{2}\otimes\bar{t}^{\,s})\right),&\quad\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{0}:=(\mathfrak{h}_{1}\otimes t^{0})\oplus(\mathfrak{h}_{2}\otimes\bar{t}^{0})\oplus\mathbb{C}\textbf{k}\oplus\mathbb{C}\bar{\textbf{k}}\,,\\
\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{+}:=\Bigg(\bigoplus_{r,s>0}(\mathfrak{h}_{1}&\otimes t^{r})\oplus(\mathfrak{h}_{2}\otimes\bar{t}^{\,s})\Bigg).\end{split}

(149)

Note that

\hat{\mathfrak{h}}=\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{-}\oplus\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{0}\oplus\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{+}.

(150)

We now define the space

\begin{split}V_{\Lambda}&:=S(\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{-})\otimes\mathbb{C}[\Lambda_{1}^{0}\oplus\Lambda_{2}^{0}]\,,\end{split}

(151)

where
\Lambda_{1}^{0}
and
\Lambda_{2}^{0}
is defined as in (
134
) and for any Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
,
S(\mathfrak{g})
is the symmetric algebra for
\mathfrak{g}
, and
\mathbb{C}[\Lambda_{1}^{0}\oplus\Lambda_{2}^{0}]\equiv\mathbb{C}[\Lambda_{0}]
is considered as a subspace of
\mathbb{C}[\Lambda]
.
The space
V_{\Lambda}
is generated by elements of the form

\begin{split}&\left(\alpha_{1}(-m_{1})\cdot\alpha_{2}(-m_{2})\cdots\alpha_{k}(-m_{k})\cdot\beta_{1}(-\bar{m}_{1})\cdot\beta_{2}(-\bar{m}_{2})\cdots\beta_{\bar{k}}(-\bar{m}_{\bar{k}})\right)\otimes{\rm e}^{(\alpha,\beta)}\end{split}

(152)

for
m_{i},\,\bar{m}_{i}>0,\,k,\bar{k}\geq 0,(\alpha,\beta)\in\Lambda_{0}
,
\alpha_{i}\in\mathfrak{h}_{1}
, and
\beta_{i}\in\mathfrak{h}_{2}
. The space

V_{\Lambda}
is a natural module of
\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{-}
. We define the action of
\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{0}
on
\mathbb{C}[\Lambda]
, and hence on
\mathbb{C}[\Lambda_{0}]
, by

\begin{split}\alpha^{\prime}(0)\,e^{\lambda}&=\langle\alpha^{\prime},\alpha^{\lambda}\rangle\,e^{\lambda}\,,\\
\beta^{\prime}(0)\,e^{\lambda}&=\langle\beta^{\prime},\beta^{\lambda}\rangle\,e^{\lambda}\,,\end{split}

(153)

where
\alpha^{\prime}(0)\in\hat{\mathfrak{h}}_{1}^{0},\penalty\ \beta^{\prime}(0)\in\hat{\mathfrak{h}}_{2}^{0}
. The central elements
k
and
\bar{\textbf{k}}
act on
\mathbb{C}[\Lambda]
as identity.
Let
\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{+}
act on
\mathbb{C}[\Lambda]
by 0. We can extend the action of these subspaces of
\hat{\mathfrak{h}}
to
V_{\Lambda}
by using the Lie bracket given in (
146
). This makes
V_{\Lambda}
into an
\hat{\mathfrak{h}}
-module.

We define a
\mathbb{Z}
-bilinear map
\epsilon:\Lambda\times\Lambda\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
, which acts on the basis as
huang

\epsilon\left(\lambda_{i},\lambda_{j}\right)=\begin{cases}\lambda_{i}\circ\lambda_{j}&i>j\,,\\
0&i\leq j\,,\end{cases}\ \ \\

(154)

where
\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m+n}
is an integral basis of
\Lambda
.
The action of
\epsilon
on general vectors is defined by the
\mathbb{Z}
-bilinearity of
\epsilon
.
Consider
\hat{\Lambda}=\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\Lambda
, with the multiplication on it given by

\begin{split}(\theta,\lambda)\cdot(\tau,\lambda^{\prime})=&\left(\theta\tau\,(-1)^{\epsilon(\lambda,\lambda^{\prime})},\lambda+\lambda^{\prime}\right).\end{split}

(155)

We now consider the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
central extension of the lattice
\Lambda
:

0\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}\longrightarrow\hat{\Lambda}\longrightarrow\Lambda\longrightarrow 0\ .

(156)

We denote elements
(1,\lambda),(\theta,0)\in\hat{\Lambda}
by
{\rm e}_{\lambda}=(1,\lambda)
and
\theta=(\theta,0)
respectively. Then it is easy to check that

(\theta,\lambda)=\theta\,{\rm e}_{\lambda}={\rm e}_{\lambda}\theta\,,

(157)

and

{\rm e}_{\lambda}{\rm e}_{\mu}=(-1)^{\epsilon(\lambda,\mu)}\,{\rm e}_{\lambda+\mu}\ .

(158)

Using the above relation, it can be shown that (see Lemma
A
for proof)

{\rm e}_{\lambda}{\rm e}_{\mu}=(-1)^{\lambda\circ\mu}\,{\rm e}_{\mu}\,{\rm e}_{\lambda}\,.

(159)

This property requires that the lattice be even and integral. Note that we chose an integral basis of
\Lambda
to define the central extension. In Appendix
A
we show that a cocycle
\tilde{\epsilon}
defined analogous to (
154
) for a different choice of basis is cohomologous to
\epsilon
, and hence gives rise to an isomorphic central extension.

\hat{\Lambda}
acts on
\mathbb{C}[\Lambda]
as follows

(\theta,\lambda^{\prime})\,e^{\lambda}=\theta(-1)^{\epsilon(\lambda^{\prime},\lambda)}\,e^{\lambda+\lambda^{\prime}}\,.

(160)

In particular for
(\theta,\lambda^{\prime})=(1,\lambda^{\prime})={\rm e}_{\lambda^{\prime}}
, we have

{\rm e}_{\lambda^{\prime}}\,{\rm e}^{\lambda}=(-1)^{\epsilon(\lambda^{\prime},\lambda)}\,{\rm e}^{\lambda+\lambda^{\prime}}\,.

(161)

Note that the same cocycle
\epsilon
restricted to
\Lambda_{0}

\epsilon:\Lambda_{0}\times\Lambda_{0}\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}\,,

(162)

defines a central extension
\hat{\Lambda}_{0}:=\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\Lambda_{0}\subset\hat{\Lambda}
:

0\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}\longrightarrow\hat{\Lambda}_{0}\longrightarrow\Lambda_{0}\longrightarrow 0\ .

(163)

Moreover, the action (
160
) restricted to
\hat{\Lambda}_{0}
makes
\mathbb{C}[\Lambda_{0}]
into a
\hat{\Lambda}_{0}
-module.
This makes
V_{\Lambda}
into a
\hat{\Lambda}_{0}
-module where
\hat{\Lambda}_{0}
acts only on
\mathbb{C}[\Lambda_{0}]
. Let
x,\bar{x}
be formal variables.
For any vector
\lambda=(\alpha^{\lambda},\beta^{\lambda})
, define the operators
x^{\alpha^{\lambda}},\bar{x}^{\beta^{\lambda}}
by the following actions

\begin{split}x^{\alpha^{\lambda}}(u\otimes{\rm e}^{\lambda^{\prime}})=x^{\langle\alpha^{\lambda},\,\alpha^{\lambda^{\prime}}\rangle}(u\otimes{\rm e}^{\lambda^{\prime}})\,,\\
\bar{x}^{\beta^{\lambda}}(u\otimes{\rm e}^{\lambda^{\prime}})=\bar{x}^{\langle\beta^{\lambda},\,\beta^{\lambda^{\prime}}\rangle}(u\otimes{\rm e}^{\lambda^{\prime}})\,,\end{split}

(164)

where
u\in S(\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{-}),\lambda^{\prime}\in\Lambda_{0}
. Note that
x^{\alpha^{\lambda}},\bar{x}^{\beta^{\lambda}}
acts as
x^{\alpha^{\lambda}(0)},\bar{x}^{\beta^{\lambda}(0)}
.
For
\lambda=(\alpha^{\lambda},\beta^{\lambda})\in\Lambda_{0}
, define the vertex operators

\begin{split}Y_{V_{\Lambda}}({\rm e}^{\lambda},x,\bar{x}):=&\left[\exp\left(-\sum_{r<0}\frac{\alpha^{\lambda}(r)}{r}x^{-r}\right)\exp\left(-\sum_{r>0}\frac{\alpha^{\lambda}(r)}{r}x^{-r}\right)\right.\\
&\left.\exp\left(-\sum_{r<0}\frac{\beta^{\lambda}(r)}{r}\bar{x}^{-r}\right)\exp\left(-\sum_{r>0}\frac{\beta^{\lambda}(r)}{r}\bar{x}^{-r}\right)\right]{\rm e}_{\lambda}x^{\alpha^{\lambda}}\bar{x}^{\beta^{\lambda}}\ .\end{split}

(165)

From the Lie bracket in (
146
), it is easy to show

[\alpha^{\lambda}(r),\beta^{\lambda}(s)]=0

(166)

for all
r,s\in\mathbb{Z}
, so that the order of exponentials with
\alpha^{\lambda}(r)
and
\beta^{\lambda}(r)
does not matter.
For a formal variable
x
, we introduce the notation

\alpha^{\lambda}(x)=\underbrace{\sum_{r>0}\alpha^{\lambda}(r)x^{-r-1}}_{\alpha^{\lambda}(x)^{+}}+\underbrace{\sum_{r<0}\alpha^{\lambda}(r)x^{-r-1}}_{\alpha^{\lambda}(x)^{-}}+\,\alpha^{\lambda}(0)x^{-1}\ ,

(167)

Similarly, we can also define
\beta^{\lambda}(\bar{x})
. We define the formal integration as the map by

\int dx\penalty\ x^{r}=\frac{x^{r+1}}{r+1},\quad n\neq-1\,.

(168)

We can then write the vertex operator as

\begin{split}Y_{V_{\Lambda}}({\rm e}^{\lambda},x,\bar{x})=\exp\left(\int dx\penalty\ \alpha^{\lambda}(x)^{-}\right)&\exp\left(\int dx\penalty\ \alpha^{\lambda}(x)^{+}\right)\\
\times&\exp\left(\int d\bar{x}\penalty\ \beta^{\lambda}(\bar{x})^{-}\right)\exp\left(\int d\bar{x}\penalty\ \beta^{\lambda}(\bar{x})^{+}\right){\rm e}_{\lambda}\,x^{\alpha^{\lambda}}\bar{x}^{\beta^{\lambda}}.\end{split}

(169)

For a general vector
v
of the form (
152
), the vertex operator is defined as
