\begin{split}&Y_{V_{\Lambda}}(w,z_{2},\bar{z}_{2})Y_{V_{\Lambda}}(v,z_{1},\bar{z}_{1})=\left(z_{2}-z_{1}\right)^{\langle\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\rangle}\left(\bar{z}_{2}-\bar{z}_{1}\right)^{\langle\beta,\beta^{\prime}\rangle}(-1)^{\lambda\circ\lambda^{\prime}}\\
&\times\exp\left(\int\alpha(z_{1})^{-}\right)\left(\int\alpha^{\prime}(z_{2})^{-}\right)\exp\left(\int\beta(\bar{z}_{1})^{-}\right)\exp\left(\int\beta^{\prime}(\bar{z}_{2})^{-}\right)\\
&\times\typecolon\prod_{r=1}^{k}\left[\frac{1}{(m_{r}-1)!}\frac{d^{m_{r}-1}\alpha_{r}(z_{1})}{dz_{1}^{m_{r}-1}}-\frac{\langle\alpha^{\prime},\alpha_{r}\rangle}{(z_{2}-z_{1})^{m_{r}}}-(-1)^{m_{r}-1}\frac{\langle\alpha^{\prime},\alpha_{r}\rangle}{z_{1}^{m_{r}}}\right]\\
&\times\prod_{s=1}^{\bar{k}}\left[\frac{1}{(\bar{m}_{s}-1)!}\frac{d^{\bar{m}_{s}-1}\beta_{s}(\bar{z}_{1})}{d\bar{z}_{1}^{\bar{m}_{s}-1}}-\frac{\langle\beta^{\prime},\beta_{s}\rangle}{(\bar{z}_{2}-\bar{z}_{1})^{\bar{m}_{s}}}-(-1)^{\bar{m}_{s}-1}\frac{\langle\beta^{\prime},\beta_{s}\rangle}{\bar{z}_{1}^{\bar{m}_{s}}}\right]\typecolon\\
&\times\typecolon\prod_{p=1}^{\ell}\left[\frac{1}{(n_{p}-1)!}\frac{d^{n_{p}-1}\alpha_{p}^{\prime}(z_{2})}{dz_{2}^{n_{p}-1}}+(-1)^{n_{p}-1}\left(\frac{\langle\alpha^{\prime},\alpha_{p}\rangle}{(z_{2}-z_{1})^{n_{p}}}-\frac{\langle\alpha^{\prime},\alpha_{p}\rangle}{z_{2}^{n_{p}}}\right)\right]\\
&\times\prod_{q=1}^{\bar{\ell}}\left[\frac{1}{(\bar{n}_{q}-1)!}\frac{d^{\bar{n}_{q}-1}\beta_{q}^{\prime}(\bar{z}_{2})}{d\bar{z}_{2}^{\bar{n}_{q}-1}}+(-1)^{\bar{n}_{q}-1}\left(\frac{\langle\beta^{\prime},\beta_{q}\rangle}{(\bar{z}_{2}-\bar{z}_{1})^{\bar{n}_{q}}}-\frac{\langle\beta^{\prime},\beta_{q}\rangle}{\bar{z}_{2}^{\bar{n}_{q}}}\right)\right]\typecolon\\
&\times\exp\left(\int\alpha(z_{2})^{+}\right)\exp\left(\int\beta(\bar{z}_{2})^{+}\right)\exp\left(\int\alpha^{\prime}(z_{1})^{+}\right)\exp\left(\int\beta^{\prime}(\bar{z}_{1})^{+}\right)\\
&\times{\rm e}_{\lambda}{\rm e}_{\lambda^{\prime}}z_{1}^{\alpha}\bar{z}_{1}^{\beta}z_{2}^{\alpha^{\prime}}\bar{z}_{2}^{\beta^{\prime}}\,.\end{split}

(257)

Here it is important that we understand
(z_{1}-z_{2})^{s}
as the power series since we obtained it by replacing
x_{1}\to z_{1},x_{2}\to z_{2}
in
(x_{1}-x_{2})^{s}
which is a formal series.
In this step we have used the fact that the two normal ordered products commute. To see this, note that the normal ordered product can be written as the product without normal order plus a multiple of the central element
\textbf{k},\bar{\textbf{k}}
using (
146
). Then since
\alpha(z_{1})
and
\alpha^{\prime}(z_{2})
commute by (
216
), hence their derivatives and normal ordered products commute too.

Now the operators in (
255
) and (
256
) are the same. Thus locality follows if we can show that the functions appearing in (
255
) and (
256
) are the expansions of a single smooth function in the domains
|z_{1}|>|z_{2}|
and
|z_{2}|>|z_{1}|
respectively. We have already proved in Proposition
3.3.1
that the functions
\left(z_{1}-z_{2}\right)^{\langle\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\rangle}\left(\bar{z}_{1}-\bar{z}_{2}\right)^{\langle\beta,\beta^{\prime}\rangle}
and

(-1)^{\lambda\circ\lambda^{\prime}}\left(z_{2}-z_{1}\right)^{\langle\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\rangle}\left(\bar{z}_{2}-\bar{z}_{1}\right)^{\langle\beta,\beta^{\prime}\rangle}
, understood as power series as explained above, are the expansion of the function

\exp\left(\langle\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\rangle\log(z_1-z_2)\right)\exp\left(\langle\beta,\beta^{\prime}\rangle\log(\bar{z}_1-\bar{z}_2)\right).

(258)

It remains to prove that the functions appearing in the normal ordered products are also expansions of a single smooth function. It can easily be checked that the functions

(-1)^{m_{r}-1}\left(\frac{\langle\alpha^{\prime},\alpha_{r}\rangle}{(z_{1}-z_{2})^{m_{r}}}-\frac{\langle\alpha^{\prime},\alpha_{r}\rangle}{z_{1}^{m_{r}}}\right),\quad|z_{1}|>|z_{2}|,

(259)

and

-\frac{\langle\alpha^{\prime},\alpha_{r}\rangle}{(z_{2}-z_{1})^{m_{r}}}-(-1)^{m_{r}-1}\frac{\langle\alpha^{\prime},\alpha_{r}\rangle}{z_{1}^{m_{r}}},\quad|z_{2}|>|z_{1}|\,,

(260)

are the expansions of the function

(-1)^{m_{r}-1}\left(\frac{\langle\alpha^{\prime},\alpha_{r}\rangle}{\exp(m_r\log(z_1-z_2))}-\frac{\langle\alpha^{\prime},\alpha_{r}\rangle}{z_{1}^{m_{r}}}\right)\,,

(261)

in the respective domains
except for poles at
z_{1}=z_{2}
and
z_{1}=0
.

{remark}

A similar calculation as in Remark
3.3.1
shows that formal commutativity holds for general vertex operators:

(x_{1}-x_{2})^{K}(\bar{x}_{1}-\bar{x}_{2})^{\bar{K}}\left[Y_{V_{\Lambda}}(v,x_{1},\bar{x}_{1}),Y_{V_{\Lambda}}(w,x_{2},\bar{x}_{2})\right]=0\,,

(262)

with
v,w\in V_{\Lambda}.

{remark}

The proof of locality goes through even if we take vertex operators corresponding to vectors of the form (
152
) with
\mathrm{e}^{\lambda}\in\mathbb{C}[\Lambda]
(see Remark
3.2
for definition of such vertex operators). This requires
\Lambda
to be an integral Lorentzian lattice. It is worth noting that formal commutativity fails to hold for general vertex operators since
\langle\alpha,\alpha\rangle,\langle\beta,\beta\rangle\not\in\mathbb{Z}
in general.

The graded dimension of the LLVOA can be easily computed. Using the structure of the vector space
V_{\Lambda}
and the general discussion in
(
Frenkel:1988xz
, Section 1.10)
, we find that

\chi_{V_{\Lambda}}(\tau,\bar{\tau})=\frac{1}{\eta(\tau)^{m}\overline{\eta(\tau)}^{n}}\sum_{(\alpha,\beta)\in\Lambda_{0}}q^{\frac{\langle\alpha,\alpha\rangle}{2}}\bar{q}^{\frac{\langle\beta,\beta\rangle}{2}},

(263)

where
\eta(\tau)
is the Dedekind eta function

\eta(\tau)=q^{\frac{1}{24}}\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}(1-q^{n}).

(264)

We now give explicit examples of isomorphisms and automorphisms of the LLVOA.

{thm}

Let
(V_{\Lambda},Y_{\Lambda},\omega_{L},\omega_{R},\mathbf{1}_{V_{\Lambda}})
and
(V_{\tilde{\Lambda}},Y_{\tilde{\Lambda}},\tilde{\omega}_{L},\tilde{\omega}_{R},\mathbf{1}_{V_{\tilde{\Lambda}}})
be LLVOAs corresponding to lattices
\Lambda,\tilde{\Lambda}\subset\mathbb{R}^{m,n}
. Suppose
\Lambda
and
\tilde{\Lambda}
are related by an
\mathrm{O}(m,\mathbb{R})\times\mathrm{O}(n,\mathbb{R})
-transformation, then the two LLVOAs are isomorphic
(V_{\Lambda},Y_{\Lambda})\cong(V_{\tilde{\Lambda}},Y_{\tilde{\Lambda}})
.

{proof}

Suppose
f:\Lambda\longrightarrow\tilde{\Lambda}
is the isomorphism relating
\Lambda
and
\tilde{\Lambda}
, then for any
\lambda=(\alpha^{\lambda},\beta^{\lambda})\in\Lambda

f(\alpha^{\lambda},\beta^{\lambda})=(O_{1}\cdot\alpha^{\lambda},O_{2}\cdot\beta^{\lambda}),\,

(265)

where
O_{1}
and
O_{2}
lie in
O(m,\mathbb{R})
and
O(n,\mathbb{R})
respectively. Further from the action (
265
), it is clear that
f(\Lambda_{1}^{0})=\tilde{\Lambda}_{1}^{0}
and
f(\Lambda_{2}^{0})=\tilde{\Lambda}_{2}^{0}
and further that the restrictions to
\Lambda_{1}^{0},\Lambda_{2}^{0}
are norm-preserving isomorphisms. Using
f
we can define the maps

\begin{split}f_{i}:\Lambda_{i}\longrightarrow\tilde{\Lambda}_{i},\penalty\ \penalty\ i=1,2\,,\\
\alpha^{\lambda}\mapsto O_{1}\cdot\alpha^{\lambda},\quad\beta^{\lambda}\mapsto O_{2}\cdot\beta^{\lambda}\,,\end{split}

(266)

which are norm-preserving maps when we consider
\Lambda_{1}
and
\tilde{\Lambda}_{1}
(
\Lambda_{2}
and
\tilde{\Lambda}_{2}
) as subspaces of
\mathbb{R}^{m}

(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

Since an integral basis of
\Lambda
and
\tilde{\Lambda}
is also a basis of
\mathbb{R}^{m,n}
, it is clear that the dimension

\text{dim}(\mathfrak{h}_{1})=\text{dim}(\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{1})=m,\,\text{dim}(\mathfrak{h}_{2})=\text{dim}(\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{2})=n\,,

(267)

where

\mathfrak{h}_{i}=\Lambda_{i}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{C},\quad\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{i}=\tilde{\Lambda}_{i}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{C},\quad i=1,2\,,

(268)

this implies that the central charges of the two LLVOAs are the same.
We then extend
f_{1}:\mathfrak{h}_{1}\to\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{1}
and
f_{2}:\mathfrak{h}_{2}\to\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{2}
by
\mathbb{C}
-linearity and observe that the bilinear form on
\mathfrak{h}_{i}
’s are preserved under this map. We then extend
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
to
\hat{\mathfrak{h}}_{1},\hat{\mathfrak{h}}_{2}
, by mapping
k
and
\bar{\textbf{k}}
back to themselves.

Consider the orthonormal bases, which were chosen when defining the conformal vectors
\omega_{L},\omega_{R},\tilde{\omega}_{L},
and
\tilde{\omega}_{R}
, i.e.
\{u_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m},\{v_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
and
\{\tilde{u}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m},\{\tilde{v}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
of
\mathfrak{h}_{1},\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{h}_{2},\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{2}
respectively so that

\begin{split}&\omega_{L}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\left(u_{i}(-1)^{2}\right)\otimes\mathbf{1}_{V_{\Lambda}},\quad\omega_{R}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(v_{i}(-1)^{2}\right)\otimes\mathbf{1}_{V_{\Lambda}}\\
&\tilde{\omega}_{L}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\left(\tilde{u}_{i}(-1)^{2}\right)\otimes\mathbf{1}_{V_{\tilde{\Lambda}}},\quad\tilde{\omega}_{R}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\tilde{v}_{i}(-1)^{2}\right)\otimes\mathbf{1}_{V_{\tilde{\Lambda}}}.\end{split}

(269)

Define the isomorphism of complex vector spaces

\begin{split}\mathfrak{h}_{1}\longrightarrow\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{1},\quad&\mathfrak{h}_{2}\longrightarrow\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{2}\\
u_{i}\mapsto\tilde{u}_{i},\quad&v_{j}\mapsto\tilde{v}_{j},\quad i=1,\dots,m,\penalty\ \penalty\ j=1,\dots,n.\end{split}

(270)

Denote by
\tilde{\alpha}\in\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{1},\tilde{\beta}\in\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{2}
the image of
\alpha\in\mathfrak{h}_{1},\beta\in\mathfrak{h}_{2}
under the above map.
Define the map
\psi:V_{\Lambda}\longrightarrow V_{\tilde{\Lambda}}
by

\begin{split}\psi\big(\alpha_{1}(-&m_{1})\cdot\alpha_{2}(-m_{2})\cdots\alpha_{k}(-m_{k})\,\beta_{1}(-\bar{m}_{1})\cdot\beta_{2}(-\bar{m}_{2})\cdots\beta_{\bar{k}}(-\bar{m}_{\bar{k}})\otimes{\rm e}^{(\alpha,\beta)}\big)\\
&=\tilde{\alpha}_{1}(-m_{1})\cdot\tilde{\alpha}_{2}(-m_{2})\cdots\tilde{\alpha}_{k}(-m_{k})\,\tilde{\beta_{1}}(-\bar{m}_{1})\cdot\tilde{\beta_{2}}(-\bar{m}_{2})\\
&\hskip 113.81102pt\cdots\tilde{\beta}_{\bar{k}}(-\bar{m}_{\bar{k}})\otimes{\rm e}^{(f_{1}(\alpha),f_{2}(\beta))}.\end{split}

(271)

Clearly

\psi(\textbf{1}_{V_{\Lambda}})=\textbf{1}_{V_{\tilde{\Lambda}}}\penalty\ ,\quad\psi(\omega_{i})=\tilde{\omega}_{i}\penalty\ ,\text{ where $i=L,R$.}

(272)

Since
f_{i}
is norm preserving, from (
172
) it is clear that
\psi
is grading preserving. We now check that (
114
) is satisfied. Let us first check (
114
) for
u=\mathrm{e}^{\lambda^{\prime}}
and
v
of the form (
152
). From the definition (
37
) we see that

\begin{split}&Y_{V_{\Lambda}}({\rm e}^{\lambda^{\prime}},x,\bar{x})v=\left[\exp\left(-\sum_{s<0}\frac{\alpha^{\lambda^{\prime}}(s)}{s}x^{-s}\right)\exp\left(-\sum_{s>0}\frac{\alpha^{\lambda^{\prime}}(s)}{s}x^{-s}\right)\exp\left(-\sum_{s<0}\frac{\beta^{\lambda^{\prime}}(s)}{s}\bar{x}^{-s}\right)\right.\\
&\hskip 142.26378pt\left.\exp\left(-\sum_{s>0}\frac{\beta^{\lambda^{\prime}}(s)}{s}\bar{x}^{-s}\right)\right]{\rm e}_{\lambda^{\prime}}x^{\alpha^{\lambda^{\prime}}}\bar{x}^{\beta^{\lambda^{\prime}}}v\\
&=(-1)^{\epsilon(\lambda^{\prime},\lambda)}x^{\langle\alpha^{\lambda^{\prime}},\alpha^{\lambda}\rangle}\bar{x}^{\langle\beta^{\lambda^{\prime}},\beta^{\lambda}\rangle}\left[\exp\left(-\sum_{s<0}\frac{\alpha^{\lambda^{\prime}}(s)}{s}x^{-s}\right)\exp\left(-\sum_{s>0}\frac{\alpha^{\lambda^{\prime}}(s)}{s}x^{-s}\right)\right.\\
&\hskip 85.35826pt\left.\exp\left(-\sum_{s<0}\frac{\beta^{\lambda^{\prime}}(s)}{s}\bar{x}^{-s}\right)\exp\left(-\sum_{s>0}\frac{\beta^{\lambda^{\prime}}(s)}{s}\bar{x}^{-s}\right)\right]\\
&\alpha_{1}(-m_{1})\cdot\alpha_{2}(-m_{2})\cdots\alpha_{k}(-m_{k})\cdot\beta_{1}(-\bar{m}_{1})\cdot\beta_{2}(-\bar{m}_{2})\cdots\beta_{\bar{k}}(-\bar{m}_{\bar{k}})\otimes{\rm e}^{\lambda^{\prime}+(\alpha,\beta)}.\end{split}

(273)

Now expanding the exponentials, we obtain a linear combination of terms of the form

\begin{split}&\alpha^{\lambda^{\prime}}(n_{1})\cdot\alpha^{\lambda^{\prime}}(n_{2})\cdots\alpha^{\lambda^{\prime}}(n_{p})\cdot\beta^{\lambda^{\prime}}(\bar{n}_{1})\cdot\beta^{\lambda^{\prime}}(\bar{n}_{2})\cdots\beta^{\lambda^{\prime}}(\bar{n}_{\bar{p}})\\
&\alpha_{1}(-m_{1})\cdot\alpha_{2}(-m_{2})\cdots\alpha_{k}(-m_{k})\cdot\beta_{1}(-\bar{m}_{1})\cdot\beta_{2}(-\bar{m}_{2})\cdots\beta_{\bar{k}}(-\bar{m}_{\bar{k}})\otimes{\rm e}^{\lambda^{\prime}+(\alpha,\beta)}\\
&(-1)^{\epsilon(\lambda^{\prime},\lambda)}x^{\langle\alpha^{\lambda^{\prime}},\alpha^{\lambda}\rangle}\bar{x}^{\langle\beta^{\lambda^{\prime}},\beta^{\lambda}\rangle}x^{\ell}\bar{x}^{\bar{\ell}}\penalty\ ,\end{split}

(274)

with
n_{i},\bar{n}_{i}\in\mathbb{Z},p,\bar{p}\geq 0
and the sum is over
\ell,\bar{\ell}
with

\ell=-\sum_{i=1}^{p}n_{i},\quad\ell=-\sum_{i=1}^{\bar{p}}\bar{n}_{i}\penalty\ .

(275)

Now we can use the Heisenberg algebra (
191
) to commute the operators
\alpha^{\lambda^{\prime}}(n_{i}),\beta^{\lambda^{\prime}}(\bar{n}_{i})
with
n_{j},\bar{n}_{j}>0
past other operators and then annihilate
{\rm e}^{\lambda^{\prime}+(\alpha,\beta)}
. The result will be a vector of the form (
152
) with some factors of the form
\langle\alpha^{\lambda^{\prime}},\alpha^{\lambda^{\prime}}\rangle,\langle\alpha^{\lambda^{\prime}},\alpha_{i}\rangle
and
\langle\beta^{\lambda^{\prime}},\beta^{\lambda^{\prime}}\rangle,\langle\beta^{\lambda^{\prime}},\beta_{j}\rangle
. Thus under the map
\psi
in (
271
), we see that
\psi(Y_{V_{\Lambda}}({\rm e}^{\lambda^{\prime}},x,\bar{x})v)
is a linear combination of terms as on the right hand side of (
271
) with factors of the form

\begin{split}&(-1)^{\epsilon(\lambda^{\prime},\lambda)}x^{\langle\alpha^{\lambda^{\prime}},\alpha^{\lambda}\rangle}\bar{x}^{\langle\beta^{\lambda^{\prime}},\beta^{\lambda}\rangle},\quad\langle\alpha^{\lambda^{\prime}},\alpha^{\lambda^{\prime}}\rangle,\quad\langle\alpha^{\lambda^{\prime}},\alpha_{i}\rangle,\quad\langle\beta^{\lambda^{\prime}},\beta^{\lambda^{\prime}}\rangle,\quad\langle\beta^{\lambda^{\prime}},\beta_{j}\rangle\\
&=(-1)^{\epsilon(\lambda^{\prime},\lambda)}x^{\left\langle f_{1}(\alpha^{\lambda^{\prime}}),f_{1}(\alpha^{\lambda})\right\rangle}\bar{x}^{\left\langle f_{2}(\beta^{\lambda^{\prime}}),f_{2}(\beta^{\lambda})\right\rangle},\quad\left\langle f_{1}(\alpha^{\lambda^{\prime}}),f_{1}(\alpha^{\lambda^{\prime}})\right\rangle,\quad\left\langle f_{1}(\alpha^{\lambda^{\prime}}),f_{1}(\alpha_{i})\right\rangle\\
&\quad\left\langle f_{2}(\beta^{\lambda^{\prime}}),f_{2}(\beta^{\lambda^{\prime}})\right\rangle,\quad\left\langle f_{2}(\beta^{\lambda^{\prime}}),f_{2}(\beta_{j})\right\rangle,\end{split}

(276)

where by equality we mean the first term in L.H.S is equal to the first term on R.H.S, and so on. Further, we used the fact that
f_{1},f_{2}
are norm preserving maps to show this equality. Consider now the LLVOA obtained from the lattice
\tilde{\Lambda}
but with the central extension
\hat{\tilde{\Lambda}}
of
\tilde{\Lambda}
constructed from the cocycle
\tilde{\epsilon}
using the integral basis
\{f(\lambda_{i})\}_{i=1}^{m+n}
of
\tilde{\Lambda}
where
\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m+n}
is the integral basis of
\Lambda
used to define the cocycle for
\hat{\Lambda}
. By Proposition
A
, central extensions corresponding to cocycles defined using different bases of
\tilde{\Lambda}
are equivalent, thus the LLVOA constructed using those central extensions are isomorphic. Hence, we may assume that the cocycle
\tilde{\epsilon}
for the central extension
\hat{\tilde{\Lambda}}
is defined by (
154
) using the basis
\{f(\lambda_{i})\}_{i=1}^{m+n}
of
\tilde{\Lambda}
. Now, following the same calculation as above and using the map
\psi
, it is clear that
Y_{V_{\tilde{\Lambda}}}(\mathrm{e}^{f(\lambda^{\prime})},x,\bar{x})\psi(v)
is given by the exact same linear combinations terms of the form of the right hand side of (
271
) as for
Y_{V_{\tilde{\Lambda}}}(\mathrm{e}^{f(\lambda^{\prime})},x,\bar{x})\psi(v)
but with factors

\begin{split}&(-1)^{\tilde{\epsilon}(f(\lambda^{\prime}),f(\lambda))}x^{\left\langle f_{1}(\alpha^{\lambda^{\prime}}),f_{1}(\alpha^{\lambda})\right\rangle}\bar{x}^{\left\langle f_{2}(\beta^{\lambda^{\prime}}),f_{2}(\beta^{\lambda})\right\rangle},\quad\left\langle f_{1}(\alpha^{\lambda^{\prime}}),f_{1}(\alpha^{\lambda^{\prime}})\right\rangle,\penalty\ \left\langle f_{1}(\alpha^{\lambda^{\prime}}),f_{1}(\alpha_{i})\right\rangle\\
&\quad\left\langle f_{2}(\beta^{\lambda^{\prime}}),f_{2}(\beta^{\lambda^{\prime}})\right\rangle,\quad\left\langle f_{2}(\beta^{\lambda^{\prime}}),f_{2}(\beta_{j})\right\rangle.\end{split}

(277)

Now, consider any
\lambda^{\prime}=\sum_{i}c_{i}\lambda_{i},\,\lambda=\sum_{i}d_{i}\lambda_{i}\in\Lambda
, observe that

\begin{split}\tilde{\epsilon}(f(\lambda^{\prime}),f(\lambda))&=\sum\limits_{i,j=1}^{m+n}c_{i}d_{j}\tilde{\epsilon}(f(\lambda_{i}),f(\lambda_{j}))=\sum\limits_{i,j=1}^{m+n}c_{i}d_{j}\,\tilde{\epsilon}(f(\lambda_{i}),f(\lambda_{j}))\\
&=\sum\limits_{i,j=1}^{m+n}c_{i}d_{j}\,\epsilon(\lambda_{i},\lambda_{j})=\epsilon(\lambda^{\prime},\lambda)\,,\end{split}

(278)

where the third equality follows from the fact that
f
is norm-preserving.
Using the fact that
\epsilon(\lambda^{\prime},\lambda)=\tilde{\epsilon}(f(\lambda^{\prime}),f(\lambda))
we see that the factors in (
277
) are equal to the factors on the R.H.S of (
276
), and hence

Y_{V_{\tilde{\Lambda}}}(\psi(\mathrm{e}^{\lambda^{\prime}}),x,\bar{x})\psi(v)=\psi(Y_{V_{\Lambda}}({\rm e}^{\lambda^{\prime}},x,\bar{x})v).

(279)

When we take
u
to be a more general vector, the corresponding vertex operator has products of operators which can again be expanded and dealt with as above.
This completes the proof of the proposition.

{cor}

Let
f\in\mathrm{Aut}(\Lambda)
such that
f(\Lambda_{i}^{0})=\Lambda_{i}^{0},\penalty\ i=1,2
. Then
f
can be extended to an automorphism of the LLVOA associated to
\Lambda
.

{proof}

We define the map

\psi(\mathrm{e}^{\lambda})=\mathrm{e}^{f(\lambda)},\quad\lambda\in\Lambda_{0}.

(280)

Then define
\psi:V_{\Lambda}\longrightarrow V_{\Lambda}
analogous to (
271
) which acts as identity on the factors
\alpha_{i}(-m_{i})
and
\beta_{j}(-\bar{m}_{j})
and as (
280
) on
\mathbb{C}[\Lambda_{0}]
. It can be checked that
\psi
defines an automorphism of the LLVOA
V_{\Lambda}
by following the same calculation as in Theorem
3.3.1
.

From Corollary
3.3.1
, automorphisms of the lattice whuch preserve
\Lambda_{i}^{0},\penalty\ i=1,2
can be extended to automorphisms of the LLVOA. We then propose the following

{conj}

Any automorphism
f\in\mathrm{Aut}(\Lambda)
preserves
\Lambda_{i}^{0},\,i=1,2
.

We prove this conjecture for
m=n
in Appendix
D
. Although we were not able to prove this conjecture for
m\neq n
there are physical reasons to believe this conjecture: T-duality group in string theory acts by automorphism of a reference Lorentzian lattice
Polchinski:1998rq
. By definition, T-duality must preserve the chiral and anti-chiral algebra of the CFT. In our formalism, the chiral and anti-chiral algebra is identified with the algebra of modes of the chiral and anti-chiral vertex operators of non-chiral VOA (see Table
1
), thus the automorphism of the reference lattice must act as automorphism of the LLVOA. This physical consideration supports the conjecture. We will assume the truth of this conjecture and derive the moduli space of LLVOAs later in Section
5
below.

4
Modules and intertwining operators

4.1
Modules

We now define modules of a non-chiral VOA.

{defn}

Let
(V,Y_{V},\omega,\bar{\omega},\mathbf{1})
be a non-chiral VOA. A
module
for
V
is a tuple
(W,Y_{W})
where
W
is an
(\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{C})
-graded complex vector space,
Y_{W}
is a linear map, called the
module vertex operator map
,

\begin{split}Y_{W}:\,&V\otimes W\longrightarrow W\{x,\bar{x}\}\\
&u\otimes w\longmapsto Y_{W}(u,x,\bar{x})w\end{split}

(281)

or equivalently a map

\begin{split}Y_{W}:\,&\mathbb{C}^{\times}\times\mathbb{C}^{\times}\longrightarrow\text{Hom}(V\otimes W,\overline{W})\\
&(z,\bar{z})\longmapsto Y_{W}(\cdot,z,\bar{z}):u\otimes w\longmapsto Y_{W}(u,z,\bar{z})w,\end{split}

(282)

which is multi-valued and analytic if
z,\bar{z}
are independent complex variables and single valued when
\bar{z}
is the complex conjugate of
z
. As before the vertex operator
Y_{W}(u,x,\bar{x})
for
u\in V_{(h,\bar{h})}
is expanded as a formal power series

\begin{split}Y_{W}(u,x,\bar{x})&=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m,n\in\mathbb{C}\\
(m-n)\in\mathbb{Z}\end{subarray}}u^{W}_{m,n}x^{-m-1}\bar{x}^{-n-1}\\
&=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m,n\in\mathbb{C}\\
(m-n)\in\mathbb{Z}\end{subarray}}x^{W}_{m,n}(u)x^{-m-h}\bar{x}^{-n-\bar{h}}\in\text{End}(W)\{x,\bar{x}\}.\end{split}

(283)

The following properties must be satisfied:

1.

Identity property:
The vertex operator corresponding to the vacuum vector acts as identity, i.e.

Y_{W}(\mathbf{1},x,\bar{x})w=w,\quad\forall\penalty\ w\in W.

(284)

2.

Grading-restriction property:
For every
21
21

21

Note that
h,\bar{h}
are
not
complex conjugates of each other. We will explicitly specify this when this is the case.

(h,\bar{h})\in\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{C}
,

\text{dim}(W_{(h,\bar{h})})<\infty,

(285)

there exists
M\in\mathbb{R}
, such that

W_{(h,\bar{h})}=0,\quad\text{ for }\text{Re}(h)<M\text{ or }\text{Re}(\bar{h})<M.

(286)

3.

Single-valuedness property:
For every homogenous subspace
W_{(h,\bar{h})}
:

h-\bar{h}\in\mathbb{Z}.

(287)

4.

Virasoro property:
The vertex operators
Y_{W}(\omega,x,\bar{x})
and
Y_{W}(\bar{\omega},x,\bar{x})
, called
conformal vertex operators
, have Laurent series in
x,\bar{x}
given by

\begin{split}&Y_{W}(\omega,x,\bar{x})=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}L^{W}(n)x^{-n-2},\\
&Y_{W}(\bar{\omega},x,\bar{x})=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\bar{L}^{W}(n)\bar{x}^{-n-2},\end{split}

(288)

where
L^{W}(n),\bar{L}^{W}(n)
are operators which satisfy the Virasoro algebra (
47
) with central charge
c,\bar{c}
respectively.

5.

Grading property:
For
w\in W_{(h,\bar{h})}

L^{W}(0)w=hw,\quad\bar{L}^{W}(0)w=\bar{h}w.

(289)

6.

L^{W}(0)
-property
:

\begin{split}&[L^{W}(0),Y_{W}(u,x,\bar{x})]=x\frac{\partial}{\partial x}Y_{W}(u,x,\bar{x})+Y(L(0)u,x,\bar{x}),\\
&[\bar{L}^{W}(0),Y_{W}(u,x,\bar{x})]=\bar{x}\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{x}}Y_{W}(u,x,\bar{x})+Y_{W}(\bar{L}(0)u,x,\bar{x}).\end{split}

(290)

7.

Translation property:
For any
u\in V

\begin{split}&{\left[L^{W}(-1),Y_{W}(u,x,\bar{x})\right]=Y_{W}\left(L(-1)u,x,\bar{x}\right)=\frac{\partial}{\partial x}Y_{W}(u,x,\bar{x}),}\\
&{\left[\bar{L}^{W}(-1),Y_{W}(u;x,\bar{x})\right]=Y_{W}\left(\bar{L}(-1)u,x,\bar{x}\right)=\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{x}}Y_{W}(u,x,\bar{x}).}\\
&\end{split}

(291)

8.

Locality and Duality property:
The module vertex operators must be local, that is given
n
module vertex operators
Y_{W}(u_{i},z_{i},\bar{z}_{i}),\penalty\ i=1,\dots,n
, there exists an operator-valued function
m_{n}(u_{1},\dots,u_{n},z_{1},\dots,z_{n},\bar{z}_{1},\dots,\bar{z}_{n})
satisfying the requirements in Property
9
of Definition
2.2
. Moreover,
for
u_{1},u_{2}\in V
,

\begin{split}&Y_{W}\left(u_{1},z_{1},\bar{z}_{1}\right)Y_{W}\left(u_{2},z_{2},\bar{z}_{2}\right),\\
&Y_{W}\left(u_{2},z_{2},\bar{z}_{2}\right)Y_{W}\left(u_{1},z_{1},\bar{z}_{1}\right),\\
&Y_{W}\left(Y_{V}\left(u_{1},z_{1}-z_{2},\bar{z}_{1}-\bar{z}_{2}\right)u_{2},z_{2},\bar{z}_{2}\right),\end{split}

(292)

are the expansions of a function

m\left(u_{1},u_{2},z_{1},\bar{z}_{1},z_{2},\bar{z}_{2}\right)

in the sets given by
\left|z_{1}\right|>\left|z_{2}\right|>0
,
\left|z_{2}\right|>\left|z_{1}\right|>0
, and
|z_{2}|>|z_{1}-z_{2}|>0
, respectively, where
\bar{z}_{1}
,
\bar{z}_{2}
are the complex conjugates of
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
respectively. Also
m
is an
\text{End}(W)
-valued function, linear in
u_{1},u_{2}
, defined on

\{(z_{1},z_{2})\in\mathbb{C}^{2}\,\lvert\,z_{1},z_{2}\neq 0,z_{1}\neq z_{2}\}\,,

(293)

multi-valued and analytic
when
\bar{z}_{1},\bar{z}_{2}
are viewed as independent variables and is single-valued when
\bar{z}_{1},\bar{z}_{2}
are equal to the complex conjugates of
z_{1},z_{2}
respectively. We say that the module vertex operators

Y_{W}\left(u_{1},z_{1},\bar{z}_{1}\right)
and
Y_{W}\left(u_{2},z_{2},\bar{z}_{2}\right)
satisfy locality and duality with respect to each other if they satisfy (
292
).

{remark}

The module for non-chiral VOA defined here is related to the notion of
module
in
Frenkel:1988xz
and
Frenkel:1993xz
, and
ordinary module
in
abe2002rationality
.

{remark}

The equality of only the first two expressions of (
292
) is the usual locality of two module vertex operators and the equality of first and third expressions in (
292
) is called duality of module vertex operators. From Proposition
2.3
, we see that locality implies duality for vertex operators of a non-chiral VOA, while for module vertex operators, Proposition
4.2
below gives a sufficient condition for locality to imply duality in terms of existence of a certain intertwining operator (see Definition
4.2
).

Chiral and anti-chiral module vertex operators are defined analogous to chiral and anti-chiral vertex operators. For
v\in V_{\Lambda}
with conformal weights
(h,\bar{h})
, we will expand the module vertex operator
Y_{W}(v,x,\bar{x})
as in (
283
).
As in Lemma
2.3
, for chiral and anti-chiral vectors
u\in V_{(h,\bar{h})},v\in V_{(h^{\prime},\bar{h}^{\prime})}
, we expand the module vertex operators as

\begin{split}&Y_{W}(u,x)=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}x^{W}_{m}(u)x^{-m-(h-\bar{h})},\\
&Y_{W}(v,\bar{x})=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\bar{x}^{W}_{m}(v)\bar{x}^{-m-(\bar{h}^{\prime}-h^{\prime})}.\end{split}

(294)

The proof of Theorem
2.3
goes through even for module vertex operators. We record the result for later reference.

{thm}

Let
u_{i}\in V_{(h_{i},\bar{h}_{i})}
and
v_{i}\in V_{(h_{i}^{\prime},\bar{h}^{\prime}_{i})}
be homogeneous chiral and anti-chiral vectors respectively with corresponding vertex operators

\begin{split}&Y_{W}(u_{i},x)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}x^{W}_{n}(u_{i})x^{-n-(h_{i}-\bar{h}_{i})},\\
&Y_{W}(v_{j},\bar{x})=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\bar{x}^{W}_{n}(v_{i})\bar{x}^{-n-(\bar{h}^{\prime}_{i}-h^{\prime}_{i})}.\end{split}

(295)

Then we have

\begin{split}&[x^{W}_{n}(u_{i}),x^{W}_{k}(u_{j})]=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\geq-(h_{i}-\bar{h}_{i})+1\end{subarray}}{n+(h_{i}-\bar{h}_{i})-1\choose p+(h_{i}-\bar{h}_{i})-1}x^{W}_{k+n}(x_{p}(u_{i})\cdot u_{j}),\\
&[\bar{x}^{W}_{n}(v_{i}),\bar{x}^{W}_{k}(v_{j})]=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\geq-(\bar{h}^{\prime}_{i}-h^{\prime}_{i})+1\end{subarray}}{n+(\bar{h}^{\prime}_{i}-h^{\prime}_{i})-1\choose p+(\bar{h}^{\prime}_{i}-h^{\prime}_{i})-1}\bar{x}^{W}_{k+n}(\bar{x}_{p}(v_{i})\cdot v_{j}),\\
&[x^{W}_{n}(u_{i}),\bar{x}^{W}_{k}(v_{j})]=0.\end{split}

(296)

In particular,

\begin{split}&[L^{W}(n),x^{W}_{k}(u_{i})]=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\geq-1\end{subarray}}{n+1\choose p+1}x^{W}_{k+n}(L^{W}(p)\cdot u_{i}),\\
&[L^{W}(n),\bar{x}^{W}_{k}(v_{i})]=0,\\
&[\bar{L}^{W}(n),\bar{x}^{W}_{k}(v_{i})]=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\geq-1\end{subarray}}{n+1\choose p+1}\bar{x}^{W}_{k+n}(\bar{L}^{W}(p)\cdot v_{i}),\\
&[\bar{L}^{W}(n),x^{W}_{k}(u_{i})]=0.\end{split}

(297)

More generally, for
m\in\mathbb{Z}
we have the Borcherd’s identity

\begin{split}\sum_{r\geq 0}{m\choose r}\Big((-1)^{r}&x^{W}_{n+m-r}(u_{i})x^{W}_{k+r}(u_{j})-(-1)^{m+r}x^{W}_{k+m-r}(u_{j})x^{W}_{n+r}(u_{i})\Big)\\
&=\sum_{p\geq 1-(h_{i}-\bar{h}_{i})}{n+(h_{i}-\bar{h}_{i})-1\choose p+(h_{i}-\bar{h}_{i})-1}x^{W}_{k+n+m+\bar{h}_{i}-\bar{h}_{j}}(x_{p+m}(u_{i})\cdot u_{j}),\end{split}

(298)

\begin{split}\sum_{r\geq 0}{m\choose r}\Big((-1)^{r}&\bar{x}^{W}_{n+m-r}(v_{i})\bar{x}^{W}_{k+r}(v_{j})-(-1)^{m+r}\bar{x}^{W}_{k+m-r}(v_{j})\bar{x}^{W}_{n+r}(v_{i})\Big)\\
&=\sum_{p\geq 1-(\bar{h}^{\prime}_{i}-h^{\prime}_{i})}{n+(\bar{h}^{\prime}_{i}-h^{\prime}_{i})-1\choose p+(\bar{h}^{\prime}_{i}-h^{\prime}_{i})-1}\bar{x}^{W}_{k+n+m+h^{\prime}_{i}-h^{\prime}_{j}}(\bar{x}_{p+m}(v_{i})\cdot v_{j}),\end{split}

(299)

\sum_{r\geq 0}{m\choose r}\left((-1)^{r}x^{W}_{n+m-r}(u_{i})\bar{x}^{W}_{k+r}(v_{j})-(-1)^{m+r}\bar{x}^{W}_{k+m-r}(v_{j})x^{W}_{n+r}(u_{i})\right)=0.

(300)

The graded dimension or character of a module
W
of a non-chiral VOA is defined similar to that of the VOA:

\chi_{W}(\tau,\bar{\tau})=\text{Tr}_{W}\,q^{L^{W}(0)-\frac{c}{24}}\bar{q}^{\bar{L}^{W}(0)-\frac{\bar{c}}{24}}=\sum_{(h,\bar{h})\in\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{C}}\left(\text{dim}\,W_{(h,\bar{h})}\right)q^{h-\frac{c}{24}}\bar{q}^{\bar{h}-\frac{\bar{c}}{24}}.

(301)

Let
(W,Y_{W})
be a module of a non-chiral VOA
V
. A
V
-
submodule
of
W
is a vector subspace
W_{1}\subset W
such that the vertex operator map restricts to a map on
W_{1}
:

\begin{split}Y_{W}:\,&V\otimes W_{1}\longrightarrow W_{1}\{x,\bar{x}\}\\
&u\otimes w\longmapsto Y_{W}(u,x,\bar{x})w\end{split}

(302)

and is a
V
-module in its own right. A
V
-module is called
irreducible
if it has no non-zero proper submodules. Irreducible modules are also called
simple
modules.
Direct sum of two
V
-modules is another
V
-module with the obvious definition of vertex operator map.
A homomorphism between two
V
-modules
(W_{1},Y_{W_{1}})
and
(W_{2},Y_{W_{2}})
is a grading preserving linear map
f:W_{1}\longrightarrow W_{2}
satisfying

f(Y_{W_{1}}(v,x,\bar{x})w)=Y_{W_{2}}(v,x,\bar{x})f(w),\quad\forall\penalty\ v\in V,w\in W_{1}.

(303)

The notion of isomorphisms and automorphisms are defined analogous to the non-chiral VOA. Again, isomorphic modules have identical graded dimension.
A
semi-simple

V
-module is a
V
-module isomorphic to the direct sum of finitely many simple
V
-modules.

4.2
Intertwining operators

In this section, we define intertwining operators and study some of their properties.

{defn}

Let
(V,Y_{V},\omega,\bar{\omega},\mathbf{1})
be a non-chiral vertex operator algebra and let
\left(W_{i},Y_{i}\right)
,
\left(W_{j},Y_{j}\right)
and
\left(W_{k},Y_{k}\right)
be three
V
-modules. An
intertwining operator
of type
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}W_{i}\\
W_{j}W_{k}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
is a linear map

\begin{split}&\mathcal{Y}:W_{j}\otimes W_{k}\longrightarrow W_{i}\{x,\bar{x}\}\\
&w_{(j)}\otimes w_{(k)}\mapsto\mathcal{Y}(w_{(j)},x,\bar{x})w_{(k)},\end{split}

(304)

or equivalently a map

\begin{split}\mathcal{Y}\,:\,&\mathbb{C}^{\times}\times\mathbb{C}^{\times}\longrightarrow\text{Hom}(W_{j}\otimes W_{k},\overline{W}_{i})\\
&(z,\bar{z})\longmapsto\mathcal{Y}(\cdot,z,\bar{z}):w_{(j)}\otimes w_{(k)}\longmapsto\mathcal{Y}(w_{(j)},z,\bar{z})w_{(k)},\end{split}

(305)

which is multi-valued and analytic if
z,\bar{z}
are independent complex variables and single valued when
\bar{z}
is the complex conjugate of
z
. The intertwining operator
\cal{Y}
(w_{(j)},x,\bar{x})
is expanded as

\mathcal{Y}(w_{(j)},x,\bar{x})=\sum_{n,m\in\mathbb{C}}(w_{(j)})_{n,m}x^{-n-1}\bar{x}^{-m-1}\in\operatorname{Hom}\left(W_{k},W_{i}\right)\{x,\bar{x}\}.

(306)

The following properties must be satisfied:

1.

L(0)
-property:
For any
w_{(j)}\in W_{j}

\begin{split}&[L(0),\mathcal{Y}(w_{(j)},x,\bar{x})]=x\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\mathcal{Y}(w_{(j)},x,\bar{x})+Y_{j}(L^{W_{j}}(0)w_{(j)},x,\bar{x}),\\
&[\bar{L}(0),\mathcal{Y}(w_{(j)},x,\bar{x})]=\bar{x}\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{x}}\mathcal{Y}(w_{(j)},x,\bar{x})+Y_{j}(\bar{L}^{W_{j}}(0)w_{(j)},x,\bar{x}),\end{split}

(307)

where the commutator on the LHS is understood to be

\begin{split}&[L(0),\mathcal{Y}(w_{(j)},x,\bar{x})]=L^{W_{i}}(0)\mathcal{Y}(w_{(j)},x,\bar{x})-\mathcal{Y}(w_{(j)},x,\bar{x})L^{W_{k}}(0),\\
&[\bar{L}(0),\mathcal{Y}(w_{(j)},x,\bar{x})]=\bar{L}^{W_{i}}(0)\mathcal{Y}(w_{(j)},x,\bar{x})-\mathcal{Y}(w_{(j)},x,\bar{x})\bar{L}^{W_{k}}(0).\end{split}

(308)

2.

Translation property:
For any
w_{(j)}\in W_{j}

\begin{split}&{[L(-1),\mathcal{Y}(w_{(j)},x,\bar{x})]=\mathcal{Y}\left(L^{W_{j}}(-1)w_{(j)},x,\bar{x}\right)=\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\mathcal{Y}(w_{(j)},x,\bar{x}),}\\
&{[\bar{L}(-1),\mathcal{Y}(w_{(j)},x,\bar{x})]=\mathcal{Y}\left(\bar{L}^{W_{j}}(-1)w_{(j)},x,\bar{x}\right)=\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{x}}\mathcal{Y}(w_{(j)},x,\bar{x})}\end{split}

(309)

where the commutativity is understood as above.

3.

Locality property:
The module vertex operators and the intertwiner must be local, that is given vectors
u_{1},\dots,u_{n-1}\in V,w_{(j)}\in W_{j}
, there exists an operator-valued function
m_{n}(u_{1},\dots,u_{n-1},w_{(j)},z_{1},\dots,z_{n},\bar{z}_{1},\dots,\bar{z}_{n})
satisfying the requirements in Property
9
of Definition
2.2
. Here, the product of vertex operators in (
53
) is replaced by

\begin{split}Y_{i}\left(u_{\sigma(1)},z_{\sigma(1)},\bar{z}_{\sigma(1)}\right)\cdots Y_{i}\left(u_{\sigma(a-1)},z_{\sigma(a-1)},\bar{z}_{\sigma(a-1)}\right)\mathcal{Y}\left(w_{(j)},z_{a},\bar{z}_{a}\right)\\
Y_{k}\left(u_{\sigma(a+1)},z_{\sigma(a+1)},\bar{z}_{\sigma(a+1)}\right)\cdots Y_{k}\left(u_{\sigma(n)},z_{\sigma(n)},\bar{z}_{\sigma(n)}\right)\penalty\ .\end{split}

(310)

We will denote the intertwining operator by

\mathcal{Y}_{jk}^{i}\text{ or }\mathcal{Y}_{W_{j}W_{k}}^{\penalty\ W_{i}}\text{, }

when we need to indicate its type.

{remark}

The vertex operator map
Y_{V}(\cdot,x,\bar{x})
acting on a non-chiral VOA
V
is an example of an intertwining operator of type
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}V\\
VV\end{smallmatrix}\right)
and
Y_{W}(\cdot,x,\bar{x})
acting on a
V
-module
W
is an example of an intertwining operator of type
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}W\\
VW\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.

{remark}

Following the proof of (
54
) and using the
L(0)
-property
1
along with the grading-restriction property
2
of modules, one can show the following
lower truncation property
for intertwiners: for
w_{(j)}\in W_{j}
and
w_{(k)}\in W_{k}
,

(w_{(j)})_{n,m}w_{(k)}=0\text{ for }n,m\text{ sufficiently large}\,.

(311)

{prop}

Let
(V,Y_{V})
be a non-chiral VOA and
(W,Y_{W})
be a
V
-module. Suppose there exists an intertwining operator of type
\mathcal{Y}^{\penalty\ W}_{WV}
where
(V,Y_{V})
is considered as a module for itself. Suppose further that the intertwining operator satisfies

\lim_{x,\bar{x}\to 0}\mathcal{Y}^{\penalty\ W}_{WV}(w,x,\bar{x})\mathbf{1}=w,\quad w\in W\,.

(312)

Then the locality property of module vertex operators implies the duality property (see Remark
4.1
for terminology):

Y_{W}(u,z_{1},\bar{z}_{1})Y_{W}(v,z_{2},\bar{z}_{2})=Y_{W}(Y_{V}(u,z_{1}-z_{2},\bar{z}_{1}-\bar{z}_{2})v,z_{2},\bar{z}_{2}),\quad u,v\in V\,.

(313)

In particular, we have the OPE:

Y_{W}(u,z_{1},\bar{z}_{1})Y_{W}(v,z_{2},\bar{z}_{2})=\sum_{m,n\in\mathbb{C}}Y_{W}(u_{m,n}\cdot v,z_{2},\bar{z}_{2})(z_{1}-z_{2})^{-m-1}(\bar{z}_{1}-\bar{z}_{2})^{-n-1}\,,

(314)

where
u_{m,n}\in\mathrm{End}(V)
defined using the expansion (
37
).

{proof}

The proof is analogous to the proof of Proposition
2.3
. Let
w\in W
be an arbitrary vector. First note that (
312
) along with the translation property
2
implies that Lemma
2.3
is true for the intertwiner
\mathcal{Y}^{\penalty\ W}_{WV}(w,x,\bar{x})
:

\mathcal{Y}^{\penalty\ W}_{WV}(w,x,\bar{x})\mathbf{1}=\mathrm{e}^{\bar{x}\,\bar{L}(-1)}\mathrm{e}^{x\,L(-1)}w.

(315)

Then we have

\begin{split}Y_{W}(u,z_{1},\bar{z}_{1})&Y_{W}(v,z_{2},\bar{z}_{2})\mathrm{e}^{\bar{z}_{3}\,\bar{L}(-1)}\mathrm{e}^{z_{3}\,L(-1)}w\\
&=Y_{W}(u,z_{1},\bar{z}_{1})Y_{W}(v,z_{2},\bar{z}_{2})\mathcal{Y}^{\penalty\ W}_{WV}(w,z_{3},\bar{z}_{3})\mathbf{1}\\
&=\mathcal{Y}^{\penalty\ W}_{WV}(w,z_{3},\bar{z}_{3})Y_{V}(u,z_{1},\bar{z}_{1})Y_{V}(v,z_{2},\bar{z}_{2})\mathbf{1}\\
&=\mathcal{Y}^{\penalty\ W}_{WV}(w,z_{3},\bar{z}_{3})Y_{V}\left(Y_{V}(u,z_{1}-z_{2},\bar{z}_{1}-\bar{z}_{2})v,z_{2},\bar{z}_{2}\right)\mathbf{1}\\
&=Y_{W}\left(Y_{V}(u,z_{1}-z_{2},\bar{z}_{1}-\bar{z}_{2})v,z_{2},\bar{z}_{2}\right)\mathcal{Y}^{\penalty\ W}_{WV}(w,z_{3},\bar{z}_{3})\mathbf{1},\end{split}

(316)

where we used the locality property of intertwiner
\mathcal{Y}^{\penalty\ W}_{WV}
and the duality of vertex operators in Proposition
2.3
. Now taking the limit
z_{3},\bar{z}_{3}\to 0
gives the required result.

4.3
Non-chiral CFT and modular invariance

Given a non-chiral VOA
(V,Y_{V})
with the set of all isomorphism classes of simple modules
\{(W_{i},Y_{W_{i}})\}
, one can construct a non-chiral CFT by taking the non-chiral VOA and a subset of the simple modules
22
22

22

A crucial requiremnt in choosing what subset of simple modules to include is to make sure that the OPE of appropriate intertwiners between modules closes in the sense that the right hand side of the OPE contains interwiners and vertex operators for modules which are included in the subset we choose. We will explore these
fusion rules
for intertwiners in a future work. For the purposes of this paper, we will work with the simplistic definition given below.
. Note that one is allowed to choose copies of the same module.

{defn}

A non-chiral VOA
(V,Y_{V})
along with a subset
\{(W_{\alpha},Y_{W_{\alpha}})\}_{\alpha\in I}
of simple modules, with possibly
(W_{\alpha},Y_{W_{\alpha}})\cong(W_{\beta},Y_{W_{\beta}})
for some
\alpha,\beta\in I
, will be called a non-chiral CFT.

Two non-chiral CFTs are said to be equivalent if the underlying non-chiral VOAs and their simple modules are isomorphic. Note that the isomorphism is allowed to permute the (non-trivial) modules but not the non-chiral VOA which is considered as a module for itself.

Let
\{W_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in I}
with
W_{0}\cong V
being the non-chiral VOA
V
considered as a module for itself be a non-chiral CFT.
The
torus partition function
of the non-chiral CFT is defined by

Z_{V}(\tau,\bar{\tau}):=\sum_{i\in I}\chi_{W_{i}}(\tau,\bar{\tau})\,.

(317)

It is clear that equivalent non-chiral CFTs have identical partition function.

A non-chiral CFT is called
modular invariant
if its torus partition function is modular invariant:

Z_{V}(\gamma\tau,\gamma\bar{\tau})=Z_{V}(\tau,\bar{\tau}),\quad\gamma\in\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})\,,

(318)

where

\gamma\tau=\frac{a\tau+b}{c\tau+d},\quad\gamma\bar{\tau}=\frac{a\bar{\tau}+b}{c\bar{\tau}+d},\quad\gamma=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z}).

(319)

Given a non-chiral CFT, its torus partition function need not be modular invariant. Modular invariance is a physical requirement and it puts strong constraints on which modules of a non-chiral VOA is allowed to construct the non-chiral CFT.

5
Moduli space of non-chiral CFTs over Lorentzian lattices

5.1
Construction of modules of LLVOA

Given any
[\mu]=[(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})]\in\Lambda/\Lambda_{0}
be a coset. We will construct a module for the LLVOA corresponding to this coset. First observe that there is a one-to-one correspondence between cosets
\Lambda/\Lambda_{0}
and
\mathbb{C}[\Lambda]/\mathbb{C}[\Lambda_{0}]
given by the map

[\mu]\mapsto\mathbb{C}[\mu+\Lambda_{0}]=e^{\mu}\cdot\mathbb{C}[\Lambda_{0}]=\text{Span}_{\mathbb{C}}\{e^{\mu+\lambda}:\lambda\in\Lambda_{0}\}\,.

(320)

Using the coset
\mathbb{C}[\mu+\Lambda_{0}]
, define the vector space

W_{\mu}:=S(\mathfrak{h}^{-})\otimes\mathbb{C}[\mu+\Lambda_{0}].

(321)

Note that
W_{\mu}
is generated by elements of the form

w:=\left(\alpha_{1}(-m_{1})\cdot\alpha_{2}(-m_{2})\cdots\alpha_{k}(-m_{k})\,\beta_{1}(-\bar{m}_{1})\cdot\beta_{2}(-\bar{m}_{2})\cdots\beta_{\bar{k}}(-\bar{m}_{\bar{k}})\right)\otimes{\rm e}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})+(\alpha,\beta)}

(322)

for
m_{i},\,\bar{m}_{\bar{i}}>0,\,k,\bar{k}\geq 0,(\alpha,\beta)\in\Lambda_{0}
.
The vertex operator map is exactly the same as for
(Y_{V_{\Lambda}},V_{\Lambda})
:

Y_{W_{\mu}}(\cdot,x,\bar{x})=Y_{V_{\Lambda}}(\cdot,x,\bar{x}).

(323)

Note that
Y_{W_{\mu}}(\cdot,x,\bar{x})
acts on
W_{\mu}
for which
the required action of
\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{0},\textbf{k},\bar{\textbf{k}}
on
\mathbb{C}[\mu+\Lambda_{0}]
is defined in (
153
).The action of
x^{\alpha},\bar{x}^{\beta}
is defined exactly the same as in (
164
) and the action of
\hat{\Lambda}_{0}
on
\mathbb{C}[\mu+\Lambda]
is given in (
161
). The grading on
W_{\mu}
is given by defining the
conformal weights
of
w
in (
322
) to be

h=\frac{\langle\mu_{1}+\alpha,\mu_{1}+\alpha\rangle}{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}m_{i},\quad\bar{h}=\frac{\langle\mu_{2}+\beta,\mu_{2}+\beta\rangle}{2}+\sum_{j=1}^{\bar{k}}\bar{m}_{j}\,.

(324)

{remark}

In view of (
323
), the modes
x^{W_{\mu}}_{m,n}(u)
of the vertex operator
Y_{W_{\mu}}(u,x,\bar{x})
is the same as the mode
x_{m,n}(u)
of
Y_{V_{\Lambda}}(u,x,\bar{x})
but now acting on
W_{\mu}
, see Remark
3.2
.

{thm}

For every
[\mu]\in\Lambda/\Lambda_{0}
, the tuple
(W_{\mu},Y_{W_{\mu}})
is a
V_{\Lambda}
-module.

{proof}

We prove the properties in Section
4
to show
(W_{\mu},Y_{W_{\mu}})
is a
V_{\Lambda}
-module. The proof of Properties
1
through
7
, except Property
2
are exactly the same as in the case of the LLVOA, which we had shown in Subsection
3.3
.

Now, it can be seen that
h
and
\bar{h}
in (
324
) are both positive numbers, as
m_{i}
are positive integers and the bilinear forms, inherited from
\mathbb{R}^{m}
and
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, on
\Lambda_{1}
and
\Lambda_{2}
are positive definite. Hence,
M=0
for Property
2
. To show dim
(W_{(h,\bar{h})})<\infty
, we show that for any
h,\bar{h}\in\mathbb{R}
the number of distinct
\lambda=(\alpha,\beta)\in\Lambda_{0}
satisfying

\langle\mu_{1}+\alpha,\mu_{1}+\alpha\rangle\leq 2h\text{ and }\langle\mu_{2}+\beta,\mu_{2}+\beta\rangle\leq 2\bar{h}\,,\text{ where }\alpha\in\Lambda_{1},\beta\in\Lambda_{2}\,,

(325)

can be only finitely many. We basically mimic the proof for the grading restriction property for the LLVOA, noting that
\mu_{1}+\Lambda_{1}
and
\mu_{2}+\Lambda_{2}
are also discrete.

The proof of the locality of module vertex operators is same as for the LLVOA case. We prove the duality property. We will show that there exists an intertwining operator of type
\mathcal{Y}^{W_{\mu}}_{W_{\mu}V_{\Lambda}}
satisfying the hypothesis of Proposition
4.2
. Indeed, for
w\in W_{\mu}
of the form (
322
), consider the operator

\begin{split}\mathcal{Y}^{\penalty\ W_{\mu}}_{W_{\mu}V_{\Lambda}}(w,x,\bar{x})=\typecolon\prod_{r=1}^{k}\prod_{s=1}^{\bar{k}}\left(\frac{1}{(m_{r}-1)!}\frac{d^{m_{r}-1}\alpha_{r}(x)}{dx^{m_{r}-1}}\right)&\left(\frac{1}{(\bar{m}_{s}-1)!}\frac{d^{\bar{m}_{s}-1}\beta_{s}(\bar{x})}{d\bar{x}^{\bar{m}_{s}-1}}\right)\\
&\mathcal{Y}^{\penalty\ W_{\mu}}_{W_{\mu}V_{\Lambda}}(\mathrm{e}^{\mu+(\alpha,\beta)},x,\bar{x})\typecolon,\end{split}

(326)

where

\mathcal{Y}^{\penalty\ W_{\mu}}_{W_{\mu}V_{\Lambda}}(\mathrm{e}^{\mu+(\alpha,\beta)},x,\bar{x})=Y_{V_{\Lambda}}(\mathrm{e}^{\mu+(\alpha,\beta)},x,\bar{x}).

(327)

The operators appearing in the intertwiner above act on
V_{\Lambda}
in the obvious way. The axioms of intertwiners along with the hypothesis of Proposition
4.2
for
\mathcal{Y}^{\penalty\ W_{\mu}}_{W_{\mu}V_{\Lambda}}
follows from the general proofs in Subsection
3.3
.

We now show that these modules are irreducible.

{prop}

Any
V_{\Lambda}
-module
(W,Y_{W})
is also an
(\hat{\mathfrak{h}}_{1}^{\star}\oplus\hat{\mathfrak{h}}_{2}^{\star})
-module, where
\hat{\mathfrak{h}}_{i}^{\star}
are Heisenberg algebras associated to
\mathfrak{h}_{i}
.

{proof}

For any
\alpha\in\mathfrak{h}_{1}
, consider
\alpha(-1)\otimes\mathbf{1}\in V_{\Lambda}
. The corresponding vertex operator is

Y_{W}(\alpha(-1)\otimes\mathbf{1},x,\bar{x}):=\alpha^{W}(x):=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\alpha^{W}(n)x^{-n-1}.

(328)

This implies that
W
is also an
\hat{\mathfrak{h}}_{1}^{\star}
-module. Similarly considering the vector
\beta(-1)\otimes\mathbf{1}\in V_{\Lambda}
and its vertex operator, we see that
W
is also an
\hat{\mathfrak{h}}_{2}^{\star}
-module.

{remark}

When
W=W_{\mu}
is the module of the LLVOA corresponding to the coset
[\mu]\in\Lambda/\Lambda_{0}
, then
\alpha^{W}(x)=\alpha(x)
and
\alpha^{W}(n)=\alpha(n)
, see Remark
5.1
.

{thm}

For
[\mu]\in\Lambda/\Lambda_{0}
, the
V_{\Lambda}
-module
(W_{\mu},Y_{W_{\mu}})
is irreducible.

{proof}

Suppose
W\subset W_{\mu}
is a
V_{\Lambda}
-submodule. Then
W
is also an
(\hat{\mathfrak{h}}_{1}^{\star}\oplus\hat{\mathfrak{h}}_{2}^{\star})
-module. By Theorem
C.2

W\cong S(\hat{\mathfrak{h}}_{1}^{-})\otimes S(\hat{\mathfrak{h}}_{2}^{-})\otimes\Omega_{W}\cong S(\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{-})\otimes\Omega_{W},

(329)

where
\Omega_{W}
is the vacuum space of
W
, see Appendix
C
for definition. Since the vacuum space of
W_{\mu}
is
\mathbb{C}[\mu+\Lambda_{0}]
we have

\Omega_{W}\subset\mathbb{C}[\mu+\Lambda_{0}]=\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\mu+\Lambda_{0}}\mathbb{C}\mathrm{e}^{\lambda}.

(330)

Since
W
is invariant under
\alpha(0),\beta(0)
for all
\alpha\in\mathfrak{h}_{1},\beta\in\mathfrak{h}_{2}
and
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{e}^{\lambda}
are eigenspaces for
\alpha(0),\beta(0)
, we must have

\Omega_{W}=\mathbb{C}[M]\,,

(331)

for some non-empty subspace
M\subset\mu+\Lambda_{0}
. Finally note that for any
\lambda\in\Lambda_{0}
we have

\begin{split}{\rm e}_{\lambda}=\exp\left(-\int dx\penalty\ \alpha^{\lambda}(x)^{-}\right)&\exp\left(-\int d\bar{x}\penalty\ \beta^{\lambda}(\bar{x})^{-}\right)Y_{V_{\Lambda}}({\rm e}^{\lambda},x,\bar{x})\\
&\times\exp\left(-\int dx\penalty\ \alpha^{\lambda}(x)^{+}\right)\exp\left(-\int d\bar{x}\penalty\ \beta^{\lambda}(\bar{x})^{+}\right)x^{-\alpha^{\lambda}}\bar{x}^{-\beta^{\lambda}}.\end{split}

(332)

Noting that
x^{-\alpha^{\lambda}},\bar{x}^{-\beta^{\lambda}}
acts as
x^{-\alpha^{\lambda}(0)},\bar{x}^{-\beta^{\lambda}(0)}
respectively,
we see that
W
must be invariant under
{\rm e}_{\lambda}
for all
\lambda\in\Lambda_{0}
. This means that
M=\mu+\Lambda_{0}
since
\{{\rm e}_{\lambda}:\lambda\in\Lambda_{0}\}
acts transitively on
\mathbb{C}[\mu+\Lambda_{0}]
.

The graded dimension for the module
W_{\mu}
for any
[\mu]\in\Lambda/\Lambda_{0}
can be easily calculated. As for LLVOA, we obtain

\chi_{W_{\mu}}(\tau,\bar{\tau})=\frac{1}{\eta(\tau)^{m}\overline{\eta(\tau)}^{n}}\sum_{(\alpha,\beta)\in\mu+\Lambda_{0}}q^{\frac{\langle\alpha,\alpha\rangle}{2}}\bar{q}^{\frac{\langle\beta,\beta\rangle}{2}}.

(333)

Using (
263
) and (
333
), we see that the partition function of the non-chiral CFT consisting of the LLVOA
(V_{\Lambda},Y_{V_{\Lambda}})
and its modules
23
23

23

We stress that these are not all the modules of the LLVOA. But if we want the non-chiral CFT to be modular invariant, we need to restrict to this set of modules, see Theorem
5.2
.

\{(W_{\mu},Y_{W_{\mu}})\}_{[\mu]\in\Lambda/\Lambda_{0}}
is given by

\begin{split}Z_{V_{\Lambda}}(\tau,\bar{\tau})&=\sum_{[\mu]\in\Lambda/\Lambda_{0}}\chi_{W_{\mu}}(\tau,\bar{\tau})\\
&=\frac{1}{\eta(\tau)^{m}\overline{\eta(\tau)}^{n}}\sum_{(\alpha,\beta)\in\Lambda}q^{\frac{\langle\alpha,\alpha\rangle}{2}}\bar{q}^{\frac{\langle\beta,\beta\rangle}{2}}.\end{split}

(334)

5.2
Moduli space of modular invariant non-chiral CFTs over Lorentzian lattices

Given a Lorentzian lattice
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{R}^{m,n}
, we have constructed a non-chiral vertex operator algebra based on
\Lambda
and constructed a set of its irreducible modules. In general, these non-chiral CFTs, consisting of the LLVOA and its irreducible modules, are not modular invariant. To construct a modular invariant non-chiral CFT we restrict to even self-dual lattices and only consider the irreducible modules constructed here which are in 1-1 correspondence with the cosets
\Lambda/\Lambda_{0}
. We call such CFTs as Lorentzian lattice CFTs (LLCFTs). Indeed, we have the following theorem.

{thm}

Let
\Lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{m,n}
be an even self-dual lattice such that
m-n\equiv 0\bmod 24
. Then the LLCFT consisting of the LLVOA
V_{\Lambda}
and its modules
\{W_{\mu}\}_{[\mu]\in\Lambda/\Lambda_{0}}
is a modular invariant non-chiral CFT.

{proof}

The partition function of the non-chiral CFT in the statement of the theorem is given by
24
24

24

We are using a different notation for the partition to emphasize modular invariance.
(
334
):

\begin{split}Z^{\text{mod}}_{V_{\Lambda}}(\tau,\bar{\tau})&:=\frac{1}{\eta(\tau)^{m}\overline{\eta(\tau)}^{n}}\sum_{(\alpha,\beta)\in\Lambda}q^{\frac{\langle\alpha,\alpha\rangle}{2}}\bar{q}^{\frac{\langle\beta,\beta\rangle}{2}}\\
&=\frac{1}{\eta(\tau)^{m}\overline{\eta(\tau)}^{n}}\Theta_{\Lambda}(\tau,\bar{\tau})\,,\end{split}

(335)

where
\Theta_{\Lambda}(\tau,\bar{\tau})
is the Siegel-Narain theta function
siegel
;
Narain:1985jj
associated to the lattice
\Lambda
.
Invariance under
\tau\to\tau+1
:

Z^{\text{mod}}_{V_{\Lambda}}(\tau+1,\bar{\tau}+1)=Z^{\text{mod}}_{V_{\Lambda}}(\tau,\bar{\tau}),

(336)

follows from
m-n\equiv 0\bmod 24
because

\eta(\tau+1)=e^{2\pi i/24}\eta(\tau)\,,

(337)

Invariance under the modular transformation:

\begin{split}Z^{\text{mod}}_{V_{\Lambda}}\left(-\frac{1}{\tau},-\frac{1}{\bar{\tau}}\right)&=\frac{e^{-i\pi\frac{m-n}{4}}}{\sqrt{|\text{det}\,\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}|}}\tau^{m/2}\bar{\tau}^{n/2}(-i\tau)^{-m/2}(i\bar{\tau})^{-n/2}\frac{1}{\eta(\tau)^{m}\overline{\eta(\tau)}^{n}}\sum_{(\alpha,\beta)\in\Lambda^{\star}}q^{\frac{\langle\alpha,\alpha\rangle}{2}}\bar{q}^{\frac{\langle\beta,\beta\rangle}{2}}\\
&=Z^{\text{mod}}_{V_{\Lambda}}\left(\tau,\bar{\tau}\right)\,,\end{split}

(338)

follows from the fact that
\Lambda
is unimodular and self-dual. Here we used the modular transformation of the Dedekind eta function:

\eta\left(-\frac{1}{\tau}\right)=\sqrt{-i\tau}\eta(\tau)\,,

(339)

and the modular transformation of the Siegel-Narain theta function
Ashwinkumar:2021kav
;
siegel
.

We now want to classify all LLCFTs based on Lorentzian lattices of signature
(m,n)
up to isomorphism. Following the physics convention, we call the set of isomorphism classes the moduli space of modular invariant LLCFTs over Lorentzian lattices and denote it by
\mathcal{M}_{m,n}
.

{thm}

Under the assumptions of Theorem
3.3.1
,
the LLCFTs based on
\Lambda,\tilde{\Lambda}
are isomorphic.

{proof}

Let
(W_{\mu},Y_{W_{\mu}})_{[\mu]\in\Lambda/\Lambda_{0}}
and
(\tilde{W}_{\mu},\tilde{Y}_{\tilde{W}_{\mu}})_{[\mu]\in\tilde{\Lambda}/\tilde{\Lambda}_{0}}
be the isomorphism classes of irreducible modules of the corresponding LLVOAs
(V_{\Lambda},Y_{V_{\Lambda}})
and
(V_{\tilde{\Lambda}},Y_{V_{\tilde{\Lambda}}})
.
By Theorem
3.3.1
the two LLVOAs are isomorphic. It now suffices to show that for
0\neq[\mu]\in\Lambda/\Lambda_{0}
, there exists
0\neq[\nu]\in\tilde{\Lambda}/\tilde{\Lambda}_{0}
such that

(W_{\mu},Y_{W_{\mu}})\cong(\tilde{W}_{\nu},\tilde{Y}_{\tilde{W}_{\nu}})\,.

(340)

Pick a representative
\mu\in[\mu]
and let
\nu=f(\mu)
. Then
define the map

\begin{split}&\varphi:W_{\mu}\longrightarrow\tilde{W}_{\nu}\\
&\left(\alpha_{1}(-m_{1})\cdot\alpha_{2}(-m_{2})\cdots\alpha_{k}(-m_{k})\,\beta_{1}(-\bar{m}_{1})\cdot\beta_{2}(-\bar{m}_{2})\cdots\beta_{\bar{k}}(-\bar{m}_{\bar{k}})\right)\otimes{\rm e}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})+(\alpha,\beta)}\\
&\mapsto\left(\tilde{\alpha}_{1}(-m_{1})\cdot\tilde{\alpha}_{2}(-m_{2})\cdots\tilde{\alpha}_{k}(-m_{k})\,\tilde{\beta}_{1}(-\bar{m}_{1})\cdot\tilde{\beta}_{2}(-\bar{m}_{2})\cdots\tilde{\beta}_{\bar{k}}(-\bar{m}_{\bar{k}})\right)\\
&\hskip 341.43306pt\otimes{\rm e}^{(f_{1}(\mu_{1}+\alpha),f-2(\mu_{2}+\beta)},\end{split}

(341)

where
f_{i}:\Lambda_{i}\longrightarrow\tilde{\Lambda}_{i},\penalty\ \penalty\ i=1,2
is defined as in (
266
) and extended to
f_{i}:\hat{\mathfrak{h}}_{i}\longrightarrow\hat{\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}}_{i}
by
\mathbb{C}
-linearity. Here
\hat{\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}}_{i}
is constructed as in (
143
) and (
144
) for
\tilde{\Lambda}
. This map is grading preserving since
f_{1},f_{2}
are norm-presering on
\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2}
respectively. One can now show that
\varphi
now defines an isomorphism of modules of isomorphic LLVOA by following the same calculations as in the proof of Theorem
3.3.1
.

It is known that all even self-dual Lorentzian lattices of signature
(m,n)
are related by an
\mathrm{O}(m,n,\mathbb{R})
transformation
serre1973course
. Thus the set of all non-chiral CFTs based on Lorentzian lattices in signature
(m,n)
can be identified with
\mathrm{O}(m,n,\mathbb{R})
. But in view of Theorem
5.2
, many of the lattices determine isomorphic LLCFTs. Moreover, for any LLCFT based on
\Lambda
, from Corollary
3.3.1
, one can identify a discrete subgroup of
\mathrm{O}(m,n,\mathbb{R})
which acts as automorphisms of the LLCFT. If we believe the truth of Conjecture
3.3.1
, then we can identify this discrete subgroup of
\mathrm{O}(m,n,\mathbb{R})
as the automorphism group of the lattice. More precisely, from Theorem
3.1
it is easy to see that the discrete subgroup is isomorphic to
\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}\mathrm{O}_{\Lambda}(m,n,\mathbb{Z})\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}^{-1}\subset\mathrm{O}(m,n,\mathbb{R})
. This subgroup, somewhat inaccurately, but conventionally
Polchinski:1998rq
;
Polchinski:1998rr
is denoted by
\mathrm{O}(m,n,\mathbb{Z})
. Thus we have the following theorem.

{thm}

Assuming Conjecture
3.3.1
, the moduli space
\mathcal{M}_{m,n}
of modular invariant LLCFTs based on Lorentzian lattices of signature
(m,n)
is isomorphic to

\mathcal{M}_{m,n}\cong\frac{\mathrm{O}(m,n,\mathbb{R})}{\mathrm{O}(m,\mathbb{R})\times\mathrm{O}(n,\mathbb{R})\times\mathrm{O}(m,n,\mathbb{Z})}\,,

(342)

where
\mathrm{O}(m,\mathbb{R})\times\mathrm{O}(n,\mathbb{R})
acts on
\mathrm{O}(m,n,\mathbb{R})
by right multiplication and
\mathrm{O}(m,n,\mathbb{Z})
acts by left multiplication.

{proof}

Choose a reference Lorentzian lattice
\Lambda_{\text{ref}}
with generator matrix
\mathcal{G}_{\text{ref}}
. Then the set of all Lorentzian lattices can be identified with
\mathrm{O}(m,n,\mathbb{R})
under the map
25
25

25

Recall that in our convention, lattice vectors are written as rows rather than columns.

\mathcal{O}\mapsto\mathcal{G}_{\text{ref}}\mathcal{O}\,.

(343)

From above discussion, the non-chiral CFT based on
\mathcal{G}_{\text{ref}}\mathcal{O}
and
\mathcal{G}_{\text{ref}}\mathcal{O}O
with
O\in\mathrm{O}(m,\mathbb{R})\times\mathrm{O}(n,\mathbb{R})
is isomorphic. Thus we must quotient out by the right action of
\mathrm{O}(m,\mathbb{R})\times\mathrm{O}(n,\mathbb{R})
on
\mathrm{O}(m,n,\mathbb{R})
. Also automorphisms of
\Lambda_{\text{ref}}
, which is isomorphic to a discrete subgroup of
\mathrm{O}(m,n,\mathbb{R})
and denoted by
\mathrm{O}(m,n,\mathbb{Z})
, act by right multiplication on
\mathcal{G}_{\text{ref}}
. So we must quotient by the left action of
\mathrm{O}(m,n,\mathbb{Z})
on
\mathrm{O}(m,n,\mathbb{R})
. This gives the required structure of the moduli space.

{remark}

In deriving the moduli space of LLCFTs based on Lorentzian lattices, we imposed modular invariance as a requirement. This was crucial in restricting the lattices to self-dual ones. If we lift the modular invariance requirement, we obtain more general non-chiral CFTs recently discussed in
Ashwinkumar:2023ctt
and called
generalised Narain theories

26
26

26

We thank Masahito Yamazaki for discussion on this point.

.

{remark}

At a general point in the moduli space (
342
), the sublattice
\Lambda_{0}
is trivial and the LLVOA is simply
S(\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{-})\cong S(\hat{\mathfrak{h}}_{1}^{-})\otimes S(\hat{\mathfrak{h}}_{2}^{-})
. The chiral and anti-chiral algebra is then generated by the vertex operators

u_{i}(x)=\sum_{r\in\mathbb{Z}}u_{i}(r)x^{-r-1},\quad v_{j}(\bar{x})=\sum_{r\in\mathbb{Z}}v_{i}(r)\bar{x}^{-r-1},\quad i=1,\dots m,\penalty\ \penalty\ j=1,\dots,n\,,

(344)

corresponding to states
\{u_{i}(-1)\cdot\textbf{1}\}_{i=1}^{m}
and
\{v_{i}(-1)\cdot\textbf{1}\}_{i=1}^{n}
where
\{u_{i}\},\{v_{i}\}
are orthonormal basis of
\hat{\mathfrak{h}}_{1},\hat{\mathfrak{h}}_{2}
respectively. All other chiral vertex operators are given by products of derivatives of
u_{i}(x),v_{j}(\bar{x})
(see (
170
)). In physics, these are called
Kac-Moody currents
and their modes generate
\mathrm{U}(1)^{m}\times\mathrm{U}(1)^{n}

Kac-Moody algebra
in the LLCFT. Thus at a generic point in the moduli space, the chiral and anti-chiral algebra is extended from Virasoro to
\mathrm{U}(1)^{m}\times\mathrm{U}(1)^{n}
Kac-Moody algebra. At certain points in the moduli space where
\Lambda_{0}\neq 0
, the chiral and anti-chiral algebra is further extended to some enhanced symmetry algebra
27
27

27

We thank Anatoly Dymarsky for discussions on this point.
. It would be interesting to identify the chiral and anti-chiral algebra at these special points in the moduli space with known algebras.

{remark}

The double coset structure (
342
) of the moduli space of LLCFTs was also obtained in
Moriwaki:2020cxf
. While the discussion of the result is complete in
Moriwaki:2020cxf
, it has not been explicitly checked that these theories are example of non-chiral CFTs defined in this paper. As discussed above, the proof of the moduli space structure of LLCFTs requires the truth of Conjecture
3.3.1
in our formalism. Such conditions do not appear in
Moriwaki:2020cxf
since the proof of the double coset structure (
342
) is given using current-current deformation of full vertex algebra in
Moriwaki:2020cxf
.

6
Narain CFT

Narain CFTs are a large class of conformal field theories which are constructed by compactifying free bosons on a torus and coupling them to a background antisymmetric
B
-field. Narain CFTs naturally appear in string theory when we perform toroidal compactification of strings in multiple directions. In this section, we will describe these CFTs and explain how they provide physical examples of the non-chiral VOA we constructed in Section
3
. We restrict to
m=n
case for this discussion.

6.1
Construction of Narain CFTs

We describe the construction of Narain CFTs. The exposition is based on
(
Dymarsky:2020qom
, Section 4.1)
and
(
Polchinski:1998rq
, Section 8.4)
.

Let
\Gamma\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be an
n
-dimensional Euclidean lattice and
2\pi\Gamma
be the rescaled lattice. Let
\mathbb{T}^{n}\equiv\mathbb{R}^{n}/(2\pi\Gamma)
be the
n
-dimensional torus obtained by imposing the equivalence relation

\bm{x}\sim\bm{x}^{\prime}\iff\bm{x}-\bm{x}^{\prime}\in 2\pi\Gamma\,.

(345)

We then consider
n
bosons
X^{\mu},\penalty\ \mu=1,\dots,n
on a two dimensional surface (worldsheet) moving on the torus
\mathbb{T}^{n}
(target space). Alternatively,
X^{\mu}
can be considered as coordinates on the torus
\mathbb{T}^{n}
. Note that

X^{\mu}\sim X^{\mu}+2\pi e^{\mu},\quad\vec{e}\in\Gamma\,.

(346)

Let us parameterize the worldsheet by
\sigma,t
. Then the action for the CFT is given by

S=\frac{1}{4\pi\alpha^{\prime}}\int dt\int d\sigma\penalty\ (\dot{X}^{2}-X^{\prime 2}-2B_{\mu\nu}\dot{X}^{\mu}X^{\prime\nu})\,,

(347)

where

\dot{X}^{2}=\sum_{\mu=1}^{n}\dot{X}^{\mu}\dot{X}^{\mu},\quad X^{\prime 2}=\sum_{\mu=1}^{n}X^{\prime\mu}X^{\prime\mu}\,,

(348)

with dot indicating derivative with respect to
t
and prime with respect to
\sigma
,

B_{\mu\nu}
is an anti-symmetric matrix and
\alpha^{\prime}
is a coupling constant (called
Regge slope
in string theory).
The equation of motion for
X^{\mu}
is given by

\ddot{X}^{\mu}-X^{\prime\prime\mu}=0\,,

(349)

which is the wave equation in 2 dimensions with solutions

X(\sigma,t)=X_{L}^{\mu}(t+\sigma)+X_{R}^{\mu}(t-\sigma)\,.

(350)

Here
X_{L},X_{R}
are called the left moving and right moving components. We now take the
\sigma
coordinate on the worldsheet to be periodic,
\sigma\sim\sigma+2\pi
so that

X^{\mu}(t,\sigma+2\pi)=X^{\mu}(t,\sigma)+2\pi e^{\mu},\quad\vec{e}\in\Gamma.

(351)

The periodicity implies that we have a Fourier expansion of the form

\displaystyle X_{L}^{\mu}(t+\sigma)=\frac{x^{\mu}}{2}+\alpha^{\prime}\frac{p_{L}^{\mu}}{2}(t+\sigma)+\frac{i}{2}\sum_{n\neq 0}\frac{a_{n}^{\mu}}{n}e^{-in(t+\sigma)}\,,

(352)

\displaystyle X_{R}^{\mu}(t-\sigma)=\frac{x^{\mu}}{2}+\alpha^{\prime}\frac{p_{R}^{\mu}}{2}(t-\sigma)+\frac{i}{2}\sum_{n\neq 0}\frac{b_{n}^{\mu}}{n}e^{-in(t-\sigma)}\,,

where

\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}\left(\vec{p}_{L}-\vec{p}_{R}\right)=\vec{e}\in\Gamma\,,

(353)

so that

\displaystyle X^{\mu}(t,\sigma)

\displaystyle=X_{L}^{\mu}(t+\sigma)+X_{R}^{\mu}(t-\sigma)

(354)

\displaystyle=x^{\mu}+\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}\left(p_{L}^{\mu}+p_{R}^{\mu}\right)t+\vec{e}\sigma+\frac{i}{2}\sum_{n\neq 0}\left(\frac{a_{n}^{\mu}}{n}e^{-in(t+\sigma)}+\frac{b_{n}^{\mu}}{n}e^{-in(t-\sigma)}\right)\,,

satisfies the periodicity (
351
). Note that the total momenta given by

P^{\mu}=\frac{1}{2\pi\alpha^{\prime}}\int_{0}^{2\pi}d\sigma\left(\dot{X}^{\mu}-B^{\mu\nu}X_{\nu}^{\prime}\right)=\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}\left(p_{L}^{\mu}+p_{R}^{\mu}\right)-B^{\mu\nu}e_{\nu}\,,

(355)

must be a vector of the dual lattice
\Gamma^{\star}
defined as

\Gamma^{\star}:=\left\{\vec{e}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\mid\vec{e}\cdot\vec{e^{\prime}}\in\mathbb{Z},\forall\penalty\ \vec{e^{\prime}}\in\Gamma\right\}\,,

(356)

since
X(t,\sigma)
is only defined up to arbitrary shifts by
2\pi\vec{e}
for
\vec{e}\in\Gamma
. We have

p_{L}^{\mu}=\frac{\alpha^{\prime}P^{\mu}+\left(B^{\mu\nu}+\delta^{\mu\nu}\right)e_{\nu}}{\alpha^{\prime}},\quad p_{R}^{\mu}=\frac{\alpha^{\prime}P^{\mu}+\left(B^{\mu\nu}-\delta^{\mu\nu}\right)e_{\nu}}{\alpha^{\prime}}\,.

(357)

Thus the set of vectors
28
28

28

We again take the lattice vectors to be rows in
\mathbb{R}^{n,n}
.

\lambda=\left(\vec{p}_{L},\vec{p}_{R}\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{n,n}
form a lattice
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{R}^{n,n}
. Moreover

\lambda\circ\lambda:=\left\|p_{L}\right\|^{2}-\left\|p_{R}\right\|^{2}=\frac{4}{\alpha^{\prime}}\vec{P}\cdot\vec{e}\,.

(358)

We now fix
\alpha^{\prime}=2
, so that
\lambda\circ\lambda\in 2\mathbb{Z}
. Next for any
\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}\in\Lambda

\displaystyle\lambda\circ\lambda^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\vec{p}_{L}\cdot\vec{p^{\prime}}_{L}-\vec{p}_{R}\cdot\vec{p^{\prime}}_{R}

(359)

\displaystyle=\vec{P}^{\prime}\cdot\vec{e}+\vec{P}\cdot\vec{e^{\prime}}\in\mathbb{Z}\,.

So
\Lambda
is an even
29
29

29

Note that an even lattice is necessarily integral.
Lorentzian lattice. In fact,
\Lambda
is self dual
Dymarsky:2020qom
. A generator matrix for
\Lambda
in the coordinates
Furuta:2023xwl

\lambda=(\alpha,\beta),\quad\alpha=\frac{\vec{p}_{L}+\vec{p}_{R}}{\sqrt{2}},\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \beta=\frac{\vec{p}_{L}-\vec{p}_{R}}{\sqrt{2}}\,,

(360)

is

\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\begin{array}[]{c|c}2\gamma^{\star}&0\\
\hline\cr\gamma B&\gamma\end{array}\right)\,,

(361)

where
\gamma
and
\gamma^{\star}=\left(\gamma^{-1}\right)^{T}
are the generator matrices for
\Gamma
and
\Gamma^{\star}
respectively.
Upon quantisation, we impose the commutators

\begin{split}&[a_{n}^{\mu},a_{m}^{\nu}]=n\delta_{n+m,0}\delta^{\mu\nu},\quad[b_{n}^{\mu},b_{m}^{\nu}]=n\delta_{n+m,0}\delta^{\mu\nu},\quad[a_{n}^{\mu},b_{m}^{\nu}]=0,\\
&[x^{\mu},p_{L}^{\mu}]=[x^{\mu},p_{R}^{\nu}]=i\delta^{\mu\nu}\,.\end{split}

(362)

For every
\left(\vec{p}_{L},\vec{p}_{R}\right)\in\Lambda
we have a primary operator given by

V_{p_{L},p_{R}}(z,\bar{z})=\typecolon e^{i\vec{p}_{L}\cdot\vec{X}_{L}(z)+i\vec{p}_{R}\cdot\vec{X}_{R}(\bar{z})}\typecolon\,,

(363)

where
z=e^{i(it+\sigma)},\bar{z}=e^{i(it-\sigma)}
which is obtained by Wick rotating
t\rightarrow it
. The normal ordering is defined via

\begin{split}&\typecolon a_{n}^{\mu}a_{m}^{\nu}\typecolon=\typecolon a_{m}^{\nu}\,a_{n}^{\mu}\typecolon=\begin{cases}a_{m}^{\nu}\,a_{n}^{\mu}&m\leq n,\\
a_{n}^{\mu}\,a_{m}^{\nu}&m\geq n,\end{cases}\\
&\typecolon a^{\mu}_{m}p_{L}^{\nu}\typecolon=\typecolon p_{L}^{\nu}\,a_{m}^{\mu}\typecolon=a_{m}^{\mu}p_{L}^{\nu},\\
&\typecolon x^{\mu}\,a_{n}^{\nu}\typecolon=\typecolon a_{n}^{\nu}x^{\mu}\typecolon=a_{n}^{\nu}x^{\mu},\\
&\typecolon x^{\mu}p_{L}^{\nu}\typecolon=\typecolon p_{L}^{\nu}\,x^{\mu}\typecolon=x^{\mu}p_{L}^{\nu}\,,\end{split}

(364)

and similarly for
b_{n}^{\mu},p_{R}^{\mu}
. The Virasoro generators are given by

L_{n}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\typecolon a_{m}\cdot a_{n-m}\typecolon,\quad\bar{L}_{n}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\typecolon b_{m}\cdot b_{n-m}\typecolon\,.

(365)

It is an easy exercise to show that
L_{n},\bar{L}_{n}
satisfy the Virasoro algebra with central charge
(c,\bar{c})=(n,n)
.
The OPE of these primary operators takes the form
(
Polchinski:1998rq
, Section 8.4)

V_{p_{L},p_{R}}(z,\bar{z})V_{p_{L}^{\prime},p_{R}^{\prime}}(w,\bar{w})\sim(z-w)^{p_{L}\cdot p_{L}^{\prime}}(\bar{z}-\bar{w})^{p_{R}\cdot p_{R}^{\prime}}V_{p_{L}+p_{L}^{\prime},p_{R}+p_{R}^{\prime}}(w,\bar{w})\,.

(366)

From the OPE we see that as the first vertex operator circles around the second, it picks up a factor of
e^{2\pi i(p_{L}\cdot p_{L}^{\prime}-p_{R}\cdot p_{R}^{\prime})}
. So for the OPE to be single valued, one requires the lattice
\Lambda
to be integral.

The torus partition function of the theory is

Z(\tau,\bar{\tau})=\frac{1}{|\eta(\tau)|^{2n}}\sum_{\left(\vec{p}_{L},\vec{p}_{R}\right)\in\Lambda}q^{\left\|\vec{p}_{L}\right\|^{2}/2}\bar{q}^{\left\|\vec{p}_{R}\right\|^{2}/2},\quad q=e^{2\pi i\tau},\bar{q}=e^{-2\pi i\bar{\tau}}\,,

(367)

where
\eta(\tau)
is the Dedekind eta function

\eta(\tau)=q^{\frac{1}{24}}\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}(1-q^{n})\,,

(368)

and
\tau
is the moduli of the torus. Here
\tau,\bar{\tau}
are complex conjugates of each other. The partition function (
367
) is modular invariant since
\Lambda
is self-dual. This construction gives a CFT for any even, self-dual Lorentzian lattice
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{R}^{n,n}
of signature
(n,n)
, this is called the Narain CFT associated to the lattice
\Lambda
.

It is easy to see that the partition function is invariant under an orthogonal action on
\vec{p}_{L},\vec{p}_{R}
separately. Thus two Narain CFT associated on lattices
\Lambda,\Lambda^{\prime}
are equivalent if
\Lambda,\Lambda^{\prime}
is related by the right action of
\mathrm{O}(n,\mathbb{R})\times\mathrm{O}(n,\mathbb{R})
, where
\mathrm{O}(n,\mathbb{R})\subset\mathrm{GL}(n,\mathbb{R})
acts on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and preserves the Euclidean inner product. Next, any two Lorentzian lattices are related by the left action of
\mathrm{O}(n,n,\mathbb{R})
, where
\mathrm{O}(n,n,\mathbb{R})\subset\mathrm{GL}(2n,\mathbb{R})
acts on
\mathbb{R}^{n,n}
and preserves the Lorentzian inner product of signature
(n,n)
. But note that if two lattices are related by an
\mathrm{O}(n,n,\mathbb{Z})
-transformation then the two lattices are the same. Thus the moduli space of Narain CFTs is given by the quotient

\frac{\mathrm{O}(n,n,\mathbb{R})}{\mathrm{O}(n,\mathbb{R})\times\mathrm{O}(n,\mathbb{R})\times\mathrm{O}(n,n,\mathbb{Z})}\,,

(369)

where the first two factors in the denominator act on the right and relate
physically equivalent lattices to each other while the last factor acts on the left.

It is immediate to see from the dictionary between conformal field theory in physics and our notion of non-chiral CFT described in Subection
1.1
that Narain CFTs are simply LLCFTs based on a Lorentzian lattice of signature
(n,n)
. The moduli space structure (
369
) is then a special case of Theorem
5.2
. The general case
m\neq n
also appears in toroidal compactification of heterotic string theory, see
Narain:1985jj
;
Narain:1986am
;
Narain:1986qm
;
Giveon:1994fu
for details. Our general result in Theorem
5.2
is a more mathematical statement of the moduli space of Narain compactifications for general signature, see
Harvey:2017rko
for more details from physical viewpoint.

Finally we note that
Dymarsky:2020qom
gives a construction of Lorentzian lattices of signature
(n,n)
from stabiliser codes. Our construction of LLVOA then completes the parallel with the construction of VOAs from codes. The code-Narain CFT correspondence has been explored extensively recently
Kawabata:2022jxt
;
Angelinos:2022umf
;
Henriksson:2022dnu
;
Dymarsky:2021xfc
;
Aharony:2023zit
. It would be interesting to study their implications on LLVOAs.

7
Conclusion and Future Directions

In this paper, we have initiated a mathematically rigorous study of non-chiral VOAs and presented the construction of a non-trivial example of our definition, namely the Lorentzian lattice vertex operator algebra and its modules. We also showed the relevance of the construction by demonstrating that Narain CFTs which appear in string compactifications are physical examples of our construction. In this section, we sketch some future directions of the study.

(1) Rationality, Regularity, and
C_{2}
-Cofiniteness:
We have defined the notion of non-chiral VOA. It is natural to introduce the notion of rationality , regularity, and strong regularity as in the theory of vertex operator algebras.
One would also like to define the notion of
C_{2}
-cofiniteness and and see if
C_{2}
-cofiniteness and rationality implies regularity as in
abe2002rationality
. The main result that we would like to prove is the theorem of Zhu
Zhu1995ModularIO
: assuming that the graded dimensions of the irreducible modules factorise into a product of holomorphic and anti-holomorphic function, we would like to prove that the set of (anti-)holomorphic characters of a
C_{2}
-cofinite VOA with certain additional properties is a representation of
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
. To be more precise, we would like to prove that if the graded characters decompose as
\chi_{W_{i}}(\tau,\bar{\tau})=\chi_{W_{i}}(\tau)\overline{\widetilde{\chi}_{W_{i}}(\tau)}
, then there exists a representation
\rho(\gamma)_{ij},\widetilde{\rho}(\gamma)_{ij}
of
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
such that

\begin{split}&\chi_{W_{i}}\left(\frac{a\tau+b}{c\tau+d}\right)=\sum_{i}\rho(\gamma)_{ij}\chi_{W_{j}}(\tau),\\
&\widetilde{\chi}_{W_{i}}\left(\frac{a\tau+b}{c\tau+d}\right)=\sum_{i}\widetilde{\rho}(\gamma)_{ij}\widetilde{\chi}_{W_{j}}(\tau),\quad\gamma=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})\penalty\ .\end{split}

(370)

Note that if the non-chiral CFT is modular invariant then we would also have that

\widetilde{\rho}(\gamma)^{\dagger}\rho(\gamma)=\mathds{1},\quad\text{for all}\quad\gamma\in\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})\penalty\ .

(371)

Furthermore, we would like to classify all irreducible modules of the LLVOA on the lines of Dong
Dong1993VertexAA
and obtain conditions on the Lorentzian lattice so that the associated LLVOA is, rational, regular and strongly regular. The first result in this direction is due to Katrin Wendland
Wendland:2000ye
, see also
Furuta:2023xwl
for some recent progress from physical viewpoint.

(2) Modular Tensor Categories and the Verlinde Conjecture:
In conformal field theory in physics, one expects the Verlinde conjecture
Verlinde:1988sn
to hold even for non-chiral CFTs. In
Moore:1988qv
, Moore and Seiberg showed that Verlinde conjecture follows from the axioms of a
rational conformal field theory
which they defined. Their axioms had an important
associativity
assumption. Huang
doi:10.1073/pnas.0409901102
established the associativity (axiom in
Moore:1988qv
) from the axioms of vertex operator algebra and its modules. It required the introduction of the notion of tensor product of modules
Huang:1995yw
. Huang also proved the Verlinde conjecture using the definition of tensor product of modules of a VOA and the associativity theorem.

We would like to define a similar notion of tensor product of modules for non-chiral VOA and then prove the associativity theorem and Verlinde conjecture. Additionally, one of the main results of
Moore:1988qv
was the realisation that conformal field theories can be understood as generalisation of group theory - the chiral (anti-chiral) algebra and its modules along with intertwining operators forms a category called a modular tensor category
bakalov2001lectures
. Huang proved that the vertex operator algebras and their modules are examples of
braided tensor categories

Huang1997TwoDimensionalCG
. We would like to follow a similar approach and establish these results for non-chiral VOAs. We discuss these questions for the LLCFT in the sequel paper
Singh2024c
.
