The authors would like to thank Anindya Banerjee, Anatoly Dymarsky, Yi-Zhi Huang, Gregory Moore, Ananda Roy, Siddhartha Sahi, Hubert Saleur, Ashoke Sen, and
Masahito Yamazaki for some useful correspondence and discussions. We especially thank Anatoly Dymarsky, Yi-Zhi Huang, and Hubert Saleur for comments on the manuscript and raising some interesting questions. We also thank Runkai Tao for proof-reading the manuscript. Finally we would like to thank the anonymous referee for comments which improved the presentation of certain parts of the paper. The work of R.K.S is supported by the US Department of Energy under grant DE-SC0010008.

Appendix A
Lattice central extensions

In this appendix, we collect some results about central extensions and refer the reader to
(
Frenkel:1988xz
, Chapter 5)
for more details. Recall that a central extension of
G
by
A
is a short exact sequence

0\longrightarrow A\overset{\iota}{\longrightarrow}\hat{G}\overset{-}{\longrightarrow}G\longrightarrow 0\,,

(372)

such that
\iota(A)
is contained in the center
Z(\hat{G})
of
\hat{G}
. Here
-
denotes the surjective (projection) map. We sometimes call
\hat{G}
as the central extension of
G
by
A
. Two central extensions
\hat{G}
and
\hat{G}^{\prime}
are said to be equivalent if there exists an isomorphism
\psi:\hat{G}\longrightarrow\hat{G}^{\prime}
such that the following diagrams commute

\tikzcd 0&A{\hat{G}}G0\\
0A{\hat{G}^{\prime}}G0\arrow["\iota",from=1-2,to=1-3]\arrow[from=1-1,to=1-2]\arrow["{-}",from=1-3,to=1-4]\arrow[from=2-1,to=2-2]\arrow["\iota",from=2-2,to=2-3]\arrow["{-}",from=2-3,to=2-4]\arrow[from=1-2,to=2-2]\arrow["\psi",from=1-3,to=2-3]\arrow[from=1-4,to=1-5]\arrow[from=2-4,to=2-5]\arrow[from=1-4,to=2-4]

We now specialize to an abelian group
G
, written additively, and
A=\mathbb{Z}_{2}=\{\pm 1\}
. A
2-cocycle
is a bilinear map
\epsilon:G\times G\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}
satisfying

\epsilon(a,b)+\epsilon(a+b,c)=\epsilon(b,c)+\epsilon(a,b+c).

(373)

A 2-cocycle
\epsilon
is called a
2-coboundary
if there exists
\eta:G\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}
such that

\epsilon(a,b)=\eta(a+b)-\eta(a)-\eta(b),\quad a,b\in G.

(374)

Two 2-cocycles differing by a 2-coboundary are said to be
cohomologous
.
A 2-cocycle determines a central extension
\hat{G}
of
G
by
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
as follows:

\hat{G}=\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times G=\{(\theta,a):\theta\in\mathbb{Z}_{2},a\in G\}

(375)

with group operation

\begin{split}(\theta,a)\cdot(\tau,b)=&\left(\theta\tau\,(-1)^{\epsilon(a,b)},a+b\right).\end{split}

(376)

A 2-coboundary determines a central extension equivalent to the trivial extension
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times G
(
i.e.
the direct product of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
and
G
) and two cohomologous 2-cocycles determine equivalent central extensions. It turns out that equivalence classes of central extensions is classified by the group cohomology
H^{2}(G,\mathbb{Z}_{2})
which is the quotient of 2-cocycles by 2-coboundaries (
(
Frenkel:1988xz
, Proposition 5.1.2)
).

We define the commutator map
c:G\times G\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}
of a central extension
\hat{G}
by the relation

(-1)^{c(\bar{a},\bar{b})}=aba^{-1}b^{-1},\quad a,b\in\hat{G}.

(377)

The following proposition will be useful.

{prop}
(
Frenkel:1988xz
, Proposition 5.2.3)

Two central extensions of
G
by
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
are equivalent if and only if their commutator maps are the same.

Let us now consider the Lorentzian lattice
\Lambda
as an abelian group. Let
\hat{\Lambda}
be the central extension of
\Lambda
by
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
determined by the cocycle
\epsilon
defined in (
154
).

{lemma}

The commutator function
c:\Lambda\times\Lambda\to\mathbb{Z}_{2}
for the central extension
\hat{\Lambda}
in (
156
) is given by
c(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})=\mu_{1}\circ\mu_{2}\,\bmod

2\mathbb{Z}
.

{proof}

We use the notation
\mathrm{e}_{\lambda}=(1,\lambda)
, like in Section
3
. Using the group operation (
376
), it can be shown that

\begin{split}{\rm e}_{\mu_{1}}{\rm e}_{\mu_{2}}&=(-1)^{\epsilon(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}\,{\rm e}_{\mu_{1}+\mu_{2}}\,,\\
{\rm e}_{\mu_{2}}{\rm e}_{\mu_{1}}&=(-1)^{\epsilon(\mu_{2},\mu_{1})}\,{\rm e}_{\mu_{1}+\mu_{2}}\,.\\
\end{split}

(378)

Using the above equations, the commutator function is seen to be

(-1)^{c(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}={\rm e}_{\mu_{1}}{\rm e}_{\mu_{2}}{\rm e}_{\mu_{1}}^{-1}{\rm e}_{\mu_{2}}^{-1}=(-1)^{\epsilon(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})-\epsilon(\mu_{2},\mu_{1})}\,.

(379)

If we take the basis of the lattice to be given by
\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m+n}
, then we have say

\mu_{1}=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{m+n}c_{i}\,\lambda_{i},\quad\mu_{2}=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{m+n}d_{i}\,\lambda_{i}\,,\quad c_{i},d_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}.

(380)

Using this, we can write

(-1)^{\epsilon(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})-\epsilon(\mu_{2},\mu_{1})}=(-1)^{\sum\limits_{i,j=1}^{m+n}c_{i}d_{j}\epsilon(\lambda_{i},\lambda_{j})-c_{i}d_{j}\epsilon(\lambda_{j},\lambda_{i})}=\prod\limits_{i,j=1}^{m+n}(-1)^{c_{i}d_{j}\left(\epsilon\left(\lambda_{i},\lambda_{j}\right)-\epsilon\left(\lambda_{j},\lambda_{i}\right)\right)}\,.

(381)

Using (
154
), each term in the above product can be written as

(-1)^{c_{i}d_{j}\left(\epsilon(\lambda_{i},\lambda_{j})-\epsilon(\lambda_{j},\lambda_{i})\right)}=\begin{cases}(-1)^{c_{i}d_{j}\,\lambda_{i}\circ\lambda_{j}},&i>j,\\
(-1)^{-c_{i}d_{j}\,\lambda_{i}\circ\lambda_{j}}=(-1)^{c_{i}d_{j}\,\lambda_{i}\circ\lambda_{j}},&i<j,\\
1=(-1)^{c_{i}d_{j}\,\lambda_{i}\circ\lambda_{i}},&i=j,\end{cases}\,\\

(382)

where the equality in
i<j
case follows as
(-1)^{n}=(-1)^{-n}
, when
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, and the equality in case
i=j
follows as the lattice is even. Hence, we can simplify (
381
) as

(-1)^{\epsilon(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})-\epsilon(\mu_{2},\mu_{1})}=\prod_{i,j=1}^{m+n}(-1)^{c_{i}d_{j}\,\lambda_{i}\circ\lambda_{j}}=(-1)^{\mu_{1}\circ\mu_{2}}=(-1)^{c(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}\,.

(383)

Hence, we conclude
c(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})=\mu_{1}\circ\mu_{2}\,\bmod 2\mathbb{Z}
.

{prop}

Let
\{\tilde{\lambda}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m+n}
be a basis of
\Lambda
different from
\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m+n}
. Then the cocycle
\tilde{\epsilon}:\Lambda\times\Lambda\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}
defined as in (
154
)

\tilde{\epsilon}(\tilde{\lambda}_{i},\tilde{\lambda}_{j})=\begin{cases}\tilde{\lambda}_{i}\circ\tilde{\lambda}_{j},&i>j,\\
0,&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

(384)

and extended to
\Lambda
by
\mathbb{Z}
-bilinearity, determines a central extension equivalent to the one determined by
\epsilon
.

{proof}

From Lemma
A
, the commutator maps of
\epsilon
and
\tilde{\epsilon}
are the same. Thus by Proposition
A
, the two central extensions are equivalent.

Appendix B
Locality of product of multiple vertex operators

In this appendix, we show that that the product of multiple vertex operators of LLVOA and its modules exists and is local in the sense of the locality property
9
.

We first prove the existence and locality for product of three vertex operators. For three vectors
{\rm e}^{\lambda},{\rm e}^{\lambda^{\prime}},{\rm e}^{\lambda^{\prime\prime}}\in\mathbb{C}[\Lambda]
, we want to calculate the product

Y_{V_{\Lambda}}({\rm e}^{\lambda},x_{1},\bar{x}_{1})Y_{V_{\Lambda}}({\rm e}^{\lambda^{\prime}},x_{2},\bar{x}_{2})Y_{V_{\Lambda}}({\rm e}^{\lambda^{\prime\prime}},x_{3},\bar{x}_{3}).

(385)

From (
232
) and (
169
), we have

\begin{split}&Y_{V_{\Lambda}}({\rm e}^{\lambda},x_{1},\bar{x}_{1})Y_{V_{\Lambda}}({\rm e}^{\lambda^{\prime}},x_{2},\bar{x}_{2})Y_{V_{\Lambda}}({\rm e}^{\lambda^{\prime\prime}},x_{3},\bar{x}_{3})\\
&=\left(x_{2}-x_{3}\right)^{\langle\alpha^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime\prime}\rangle}\left(\bar{x}_{2}-\bar{x}_{3}\right)^{\langle\beta^{\prime},\beta^{\prime\prime}\rangle}Y_{V_{\Lambda}}({\rm e}^{\lambda},x_{1},\bar{x}_{1})\exp\left(\int\alpha^{\prime}(x_{2})^{-}\right)\exp\left(\int\alpha^{\prime\prime}(x_{3})^{-}\right)\\
&\quad\exp\left(\int\alpha^{\prime}(x_{2})^{+}\right)\quad\exp\left(\int\alpha^{\prime\prime}(x_{3})^{+}\right)\exp\left(\int\beta^{\prime}(\bar{x}_{2})^{-}\right)\exp\left(\int\beta^{\prime\prime}(\bar{x}_{3})^{-}\right)\\
&\quad\exp\left(\int\beta^{\prime}(\bar{x}_{2})^{+}\right)\exp\left(\int\beta^{\prime\prime}(\bar{x}_{3})^{+}\right){\rm e}_{\lambda^{\prime}}{\rm e}_{\lambda^{\prime\prime}}x_{2}^{\alpha^{\prime}}\bar{x}_{2}^{\beta^{\prime}}x_{3}^{\alpha^{\prime\prime}}\bar{x}_{3}^{\beta^{\prime\prime}}\\
&=\left(x_{2}-x_{3}\right)^{\langle\alpha^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime\prime}\rangle}\left(\bar{x}_{2}-\bar{x}_{3}\right)^{\langle\beta^{\prime},\beta^{\prime\prime}\rangle}\exp\left(\int\alpha(x_{1})^{-}\right)\exp\left(\int\alpha(x_{1})^{+}\right)\\
&\quad\exp\left(\int\beta(\bar{x}_{1})^{-}\right)\exp\left(\int\beta(\bar{x}_{1})^{+}\right){\rm e}_{\lambda}\,x_{1}^{\alpha}\bar{x}_{1}^{\beta}\exp\left(\int\alpha^{\prime}(x_{2})^{-}\right)\exp\left(\int\alpha^{\prime\prime}(x_{3})^{-}\right)\\
&\quad\exp\left(\int\alpha^{\prime}(x_{2})^{+}\right)\quad\exp\left(\int\alpha^{\prime\prime}(x_{3})^{+}\right)\exp\left(\int\beta^{\prime}(\bar{x}_{2})^{-}\right)\exp\left(\int\beta^{\prime\prime}(\bar{x}_{3})^{-}\right)\\
&\quad\exp\left(\int\beta^{\prime}(\bar{x}_{2})^{+}\right)\exp\left(\int\beta^{\prime\prime}(\bar{x}_{3})^{+}\right){\rm e}_{\lambda^{\prime}}{\rm e}_{\lambda^{\prime\prime}}x_{2}^{\alpha^{\prime}}\bar{x}_{2}^{\beta^{\prime}}x_{3}^{\alpha^{\prime\prime}}\bar{x}_{3}^{\beta^{\prime\prime}}.\end{split}

(386)

Now using (
226
) and (
227
) we get

\begin{split}&Y_{V_{\Lambda}}({\rm e}^{\lambda},x_{1},\bar{x}_{1})Y_{V_{\Lambda}}({\rm e}^{\lambda^{\prime}},x_{2},\bar{x}_{2})Y_{V_{\Lambda}}({\rm e}^{\lambda^{\prime\prime}},x_{3},\bar{x}_{3})\\
&=\left(x_{1}-x_{2}\right)^{\langle\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\rangle}\left(\bar{x}_{1}-\bar{x}_{2}\right)^{\langle\beta,\beta^{\prime}\rangle}\left(x_{1}-x_{3}\right)^{\langle\alpha,\alpha^{\prime\prime}\rangle}\left(\bar{x}_{1}-\bar{x}_{3}\right)^{\langle\beta,\beta^{\prime\prime}\rangle}\left(x_{2}-x_{3}\right)^{\langle\alpha^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime\prime}\rangle}\left(\bar{x}_{2}-\bar{x}_{3}\right)^{\langle\beta^{\prime},\beta^{\prime\prime}\rangle}\\
&\quad\exp\left(\int\alpha(x_{1})^{-}\right)\exp\left(\int\alpha^{\prime}(x_{2})^{-}\right)\exp\left(\int\alpha^{\prime\prime}(x_{3})^{-}\right)\exp\left(\int\alpha(x_{1})^{+}\right)\\
&\quad\exp\left(\int\alpha^{\prime}(x_{2})^{+}\right)\quad\exp\left(\int\alpha^{\prime\prime}(x_{3})^{+}\right)\exp\left(\int\beta(\bar{x}_{1})^{-}\right)\exp\left(\int\beta^{\prime}(\bar{x}_{2})^{-}\right)\\
&\quad\exp\left(\int\beta^{\prime\prime}(\bar{x}_{3})^{-}\right)\exp\left(\int\beta(\bar{x}_{1})^{+}\right)\exp\left(\int\beta^{\prime}(\bar{x}_{2})^{+}\right)\exp\left(\int\beta^{\prime\prime}(\bar{x}_{3})^{+}\right)\\
&\quad{\rm e}_{\lambda}\,{\rm e}_{\lambda^{\prime}}{\rm e}_{\lambda^{\prime\prime}}x_{1}^{\alpha}\bar{x}_{1}^{\beta}x_{2}^{\alpha^{\prime}}\bar{x}_{2}^{\beta^{\prime}}x_{3}^{\alpha^{\prime\prime}}\bar{x}_{3}^{\beta^{\prime\prime}}\\
&\equiv\left(x_{1}-x_{2}\right)^{\langle\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\rangle}\left(\bar{x}_{1}-\bar{x}_{2}\right)^{\langle\beta,\beta^{\prime}\rangle}\left(x_{1}-x_{3}\right)^{\langle\alpha,\alpha^{\prime\prime}\rangle}\left(\bar{x}_{1}-\bar{x}_{3}\right)^{\langle\beta,\beta^{\prime\prime}\rangle}\left(x_{2}-x_{3}\right)^{\langle\alpha^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime\prime}\rangle}\left(\bar{x}_{2}-\bar{x}_{3}\right)^{\langle\beta^{\prime},\beta^{\prime\prime}\rangle}\\
&\quad F(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})G(\bar{x}_{1},\bar{x}_{2},\bar{x}_{3}).\end{split}

(387)

Substituting complex variables
x_{1}=z_{1},x_{2}=z_{2}
and
x_{3}=z_{3}
, it is easy to show that the right hand side of (
387
) is the expansion of the function

\begin{split}&\exp\left(\langle\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\rangle\log(z_1-z_2)\right)\exp\left(\langle\beta,\beta^{\prime}\rangle\log(\bar{z}_1-\bar{z}_2)\right)\exp\left(\langle\alpha,\alpha^{\prime\prime}\rangle\log(z_1-z_3)\right)\\
&\exp\left(\langle\beta,\beta^{\prime\prime}\rangle\log(\bar{z}_1-\bar{z}_3)\right)\exp\left(\langle\alpha^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime\prime}\rangle\log(z_2-z_3)\right)\exp\left(\langle\beta^{\prime},\beta^{\prime\prime}\rangle\log(\bar{z}_2-\bar{z}_3)\right)\\
&F(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})G(\bar{z}_{1},\bar{z}_{2},\bar{z}_{3})\end{split}

(388)

in the region
|z_{1}|>|z_{2}|>|z_{3}|
. Following the same arguments as in Section
3.3.1
, we see that the three vertex operators satisfy locality for transpositions
(12),(23)\in S_{3}
. For the transposition
(13)\in S_{3}
, we get a sign
(-1)^{\lambda\circ\lambda^{\prime}+\lambda^{\prime}\circ\lambda^{\prime\prime}+\lambda\circ\lambda^{\prime\prime}}
from the exchange of operators
{\rm e}_{\lambda^{\prime\prime}}\,{\rm e}_{\lambda^{\prime}}{\rm e}_{\lambda}\to{\rm e}_{\lambda}\,{\rm e}_{\lambda^{\prime}}{\rm e}_{\lambda^{\prime\prime}}
. This sign can be used to show that the functions appearing in (
387
) after the permutation
z_{1}\leftrightarrow z_{3}
is the expansion of the function (
388
) in the region
|z_{3}|>|z_{2}|>|z_{1}|
. Since
S_{3}
is generated by the three permutations
(12),(23),(13)
, the locality property holds for any permutation in
S_{3}
.

The proof for an arbitrary number of vertex operator is entirely similar.

We now prove locality for general vectors of the form (
152
). We first want to calculate

Y_{V_{\Lambda}}(u,x_{1},\bar{x}_{1})Y_{V_{\Lambda}}(v,x_{2},\bar{x}_{2})Y_{V_{\Lambda}}(w,x_{3},\bar{x}_{3}),

(389)

with vectors
u,v,w
of the form (
152
):

\begin{split}&u=\left(\alpha_{1}(-l_{1})\cdot\alpha_{2}(-l_{2})\cdots\alpha_{k}(-l_{k})\cdot\beta_{1}(-\bar{l}_{1})\cdot\beta_{2}(-\bar{l}_{2})\cdots\beta_{\bar{k}}(-\bar{l}_{\bar{k}})\right)\otimes{\rm e}^{(\alpha,\beta)}\,,\\
&v=\left(\alpha^{\prime}_{1}(-m_{1})\cdot\alpha^{\prime}_{2}(-m_{2})\cdots\alpha^{\prime}_{\ell}(-m_{\ell})\cdot\beta^{\prime}_{1}(-\bar{m}_{1})\cdot\beta^{\prime}_{2}(-\bar{m}_{2})\cdots\beta^{\prime}_{\bar{\ell}}(-\bar{m}_{\bar{\ell}})\right)\otimes{\rm e}^{(\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})}\,,\\
&w=\left(\alpha^{\prime\prime}_{1}(-n_{1})\cdot\alpha^{\prime\prime}_{2}(-n_{2})\cdots\alpha^{\prime\prime}_{m}(-n_{m})\cdot\beta^{\prime\prime}_{1}(-\bar{n}_{1})\cdot\beta^{\prime\prime}_{2}(-\bar{n}_{2})\cdots\beta^{\prime\prime}_{\bar{m}}(-\bar{n}_{\bar{m}})\right)\otimes{\rm e}^{(\alpha^{\prime\prime},\beta^{\prime\prime})}\,,\end{split}

(390)

and we further use the notation that
\lambda=(\alpha,\beta),\lambda^{\prime}=(\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}),
and
\lambda^{\prime\prime}=(\alpha^{\prime\prime},\beta^{\prime\prime})
.
Using (
255
), we have

\begin{split}&Y_{V_{\Lambda}}(u,x_{1},\bar{x}_{1})Y_{V_{\Lambda}}(v,x_{2},\bar{x}_{2})Y_{V_{\Lambda}}(w,x_{3},\bar{x}_{3})=\left(x_{1}-x_{2}\right)^{\langle\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\rangle}\left(\bar{x}_{1}-\bar{x}_{2}\right)^{\langle\beta,\beta^{\prime}\rangle}Y_{V_{\Lambda}}(u,x_{1},\bar{x}_{1})\\
&\times\exp\left(\int\alpha^{\prime}(x_{2})^{-}\right)\exp\left(\int\alpha^{\prime\prime}(x_{3})^{-}\right)\exp\left(\int\beta^{\prime}(\bar{x}_{2})^{-}\right)\exp\left(\int\beta^{\prime\prime}(\bar{x}_{3})^{-}\right)\\
&\times\typecolon\prod_{r=1}^{\ell}\left[\frac{1}{(m_{r}-1)!}\frac{d^{m_{r}-1}\alpha^{\prime}_{r}(x_{2})}{dx_{2}^{m_{r}-1}}+(-1)^{m_{r}-1}\left(\frac{\langle\alpha^{\prime\prime},\alpha^{\prime}_{r}\rangle}{(x_{2}-x_{3})^{m_{r}}}-\frac{\langle\alpha^{\prime\prime},\alpha^{\prime}_{r}\rangle}{x_{2}^{m_{r}}}\right)\right]\\
&\times\prod_{s=1}^{\bar{\ell}}\left[\frac{1}{(\bar{m}_{s}-1)!}\frac{d^{\bar{m}_{s}-1}\beta^{\prime}_{s}(\bar{x}_{2})}{d\bar{x}_{2}^{\bar{m}_{s}-1}}+(-1)^{\bar{m}_{s}-1}\left(\frac{\langle\beta^{\prime\prime},\beta^{\prime}_{s}\rangle}{(\bar{x}_{2}-\bar{x}_{3})^{\bar{m}_{s}}}-\frac{\langle\beta^{\prime\prime},\beta^{\prime}_{s}\rangle}{\bar{x}_{2}^{\bar{m}_{s}}}\right)\right]\typecolon\\
\end{split}

\begin{split}&\times\typecolon\prod_{p=1}^{m}\left[\frac{1}{(n_{p}-1)!}\frac{d^{n_{p}-1}\alpha_{p}^{\prime\prime}(x_{3})}{dx_{3}^{n_{p}-1}}-\frac{\langle\alpha^{\prime},\alpha_{p}^{\prime\prime}\rangle}{(x_{2}-x_{3})^{n_{p}}}-(-1)^{n_{p}-1}\frac{\langle\alpha^{\prime},\alpha_{p}^{\prime\prime}\rangle}{x_{3}^{n_{p}}}\right]\\
&\times\prod_{q=1}^{\bar{m}}\left[\frac{1}{(\bar{n}_{q}-1)!}\frac{d^{\bar{n}_{q}-1}\beta_{q}^{\prime\prime}(\bar{x}_{3})}{d\bar{x}_{3}^{\bar{n}_{q}-1}}-\frac{\langle\beta^{\prime},\beta_{q}^{\prime\prime}\rangle}{(\bar{x}_{2}-\bar{x}_{3})^{\bar{n}_{q}}}-(-1)^{\bar{n}_{q}-1}\frac{\langle\beta^{\prime},\beta_{q}^{\prime\prime}\rangle}{\bar{x}_{3}^{\bar{n}_{q}}}\right]\typecolon\\
&\times\exp\left(\int\alpha^{\prime}(x_{2})^{+}\right)\exp\left(\int\beta^{\prime}(\bar{x}_{2})^{+}\right)\exp\left(\int\alpha^{\prime\prime}(x_{3})^{+}\right)\exp\left(\int\beta^{\prime\prime}(\bar{x}_{3})^{+}\right)\\
&\times{\rm e}_{\lambda^{\prime}}{\rm e}_{\lambda^{\prime\prime}}x_{2}^{\alpha^{\prime}}\bar{x}_{2}^{\beta^{\prime}}x_{3}^{\alpha^{\prime\prime}}\bar{x}_{3}^{\beta^{\prime\prime}}\,.\end{split}

(391)

Now using the general expression for vertex operator (
37
) and the relations (
247
), (
248
), (
249
), and (
251
) successively on the product in normal order, along with relations (
226
),(
227
) and the formal variable identity (
26
), we obtain

\begin{split}&Y_{V_{\Lambda}}(u,x_{1},\bar{x}_{1})Y_{V_{\Lambda}}(v,x_{2},\bar{x}_{2})Y_{V_{\Lambda}}(w,x_{3},\bar{x}_{3})\\
&=\left(x_{1}-x_{2}\right)^{\langle\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\rangle}\left(\bar{x}_{1}-\bar{x}_{2}\right)^{\langle\beta,\beta^{\prime}\rangle}\left(x_{1}-x_{3}\right)^{\langle\alpha,\alpha^{\prime\prime}\rangle}\left(\bar{x}_{1}-\bar{x}_{3}\right)^{\langle\beta,\beta^{\prime\prime}\rangle}\left(x_{2}-x_{3}\right)^{\langle\alpha^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime\prime}\rangle}\left(\bar{x}_{2}-\bar{x}_{3}\right)^{\langle\beta^{\prime},\beta^{\prime\prime}\rangle}\\
&\times\exp\left(\int\alpha(x_{1})^{-}\right)\exp\left(\int\alpha^{\prime}(x_{2})^{-}\right)\exp\left(\int\alpha^{\prime\prime}(x_{3})^{-}\right)\\
&\times\exp\left(\int\beta(\bar{x}_{1})^{-}\right)\exp\left(\int\beta^{\prime}(\bar{x}_{2})^{-}\right)\exp\left(\int\beta^{\prime\prime}(\bar{x}_{3})^{-}\right)\\
&\times\typecolon\prod_{i=1}^{k}\left[\frac{1}{(l_{i}-1)!}\frac{d^{l_{i}-1}\alpha_{i}(x_{1})}{dx_{1}^{l_{i}-1}}+(-1)^{l_{i}-1}\left(\frac{\langle\alpha^{\prime},\alpha_{i}\rangle}{(x_{1}-x_{2})^{l_{i}}}-\frac{\langle\alpha^{\prime},\alpha_{i}\rangle}{x_{1}^{l_{i}}}+\frac{\langle\alpha^{\prime\prime},\alpha_{i}\rangle}{(x_{1}-x_{3})^{l_{i}}}-\frac{\langle\alpha^{\prime\prime},\alpha_{i}\rangle}{x_{1}^{l_{i}}}\right)\right]\\
&\times\prod_{j=1}^{\bar{k}}\left[\frac{1}{(\bar{l}_{j}-1)!}\frac{d^{\bar{l}_{j}-1}\beta_{j}(\bar{x}_{1})}{d\bar{x}_{1}^{\bar{l}_{j}-1}}+(-1)^{\bar{l}_{j}-1}\left(\frac{\langle\beta^{\prime},\beta_{j}\rangle}{(\bar{x}_{1}-\bar{x}_{2})^{\bar{l}_{j}}}-\frac{\langle\beta^{\prime},\beta_{j}\rangle}{\bar{x}_{1}^{\bar{l}_{j}}}+\frac{\langle\beta^{\prime\prime},\beta_{j}\rangle}{(\bar{x}_{1}-\bar{x}_{3})^{\bar{l}_{j}}}-\frac{\langle\beta^{\prime\prime},\beta_{j}\rangle}{\bar{x}_{1}^{\bar{l}_{j}}}\right)\right]\typecolon\\
&\times\typecolon\prod_{r=1}^{\ell}\left[\frac{1}{(m_{r}-1)!}\frac{d^{m_{r}-1}\alpha^{\prime}_{r}(x_{2})}{dx_{2}^{m_{r}-1}}+(-1)^{m_{r}-1}\left(\frac{\langle\alpha^{\prime\prime},\alpha^{\prime}_{r}\rangle}{(x_{2}-x_{3})^{m_{r}}}-\frac{\langle\alpha^{\prime\prime},\alpha^{\prime}_{r}\rangle}{x_{2}^{m_{r}}}-\frac{\langle\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}_{r}\rangle}{x_{2}^{m_{r}}}\right)-\frac{\langle\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}_{r}\rangle}{(x_{1}-x_{2})^{m_{r}}}\right]\\
&\times\prod_{s=1}^{\bar{\ell}}\left[\frac{1}{(\bar{m}_{s}-1)!}\frac{d^{\bar{m}_{s}-1}\beta^{\prime}_{s}(\bar{x}_{2})}{d\bar{x}_{2}^{\bar{m}_{s}-1}}+(-1)^{\bar{m}_{s}-1}\left(\frac{\langle\beta^{\prime\prime},\beta^{\prime}_{s}\rangle}{(\bar{x}_{2}-\bar{x}_{3})^{\bar{m}_{s}}}-\frac{\langle\beta^{\prime\prime},\beta^{\prime}_{s}\rangle}{\bar{x}_{2}^{\bar{m}_{s}}}-\frac{\langle\beta,\beta^{\prime}_{s}\rangle}{\bar{x}_{2}^{\bar{m}_{s}}}\right)-\frac{\langle\beta,\beta^{\prime}_{s}\rangle}{(\bar{x}_{1}-\bar{x}_{2})^{\bar{m}_{s}}}\right]\typecolon\\
&\times\typecolon\prod_{p=1}^{m}\left[\frac{1}{(n_{p}-1)!}\frac{d^{n_{p}-1}\alpha_{p}^{\prime\prime}(x_{3})}{dx_{3}^{n_{p}-1}}-\frac{\langle\alpha^{\prime},\alpha_{p}^{\prime\prime}\rangle}{(x_{2}-x_{3})^{n_{p}}}-\frac{\langle\alpha,\alpha_{p}^{\prime\prime}\rangle}{(x_{1}-x_{3})^{n_{p}}}-(-1)^{n_{p}-1}\left(\frac{\langle\alpha^{\prime},\alpha_{p}^{\prime\prime}\rangle}{x_{3}^{n_{p}}}+\frac{\langle\alpha,\alpha_{p}^{\prime\prime}\rangle}{x_{3}^{n_{p}}}\right)\right]\\
&\times\prod_{q=1}^{\bar{m}}\left[\frac{1}{(\bar{n}_{q}-1)!}\frac{d^{\bar{n}_{q}-1}\beta_{q}^{\prime\prime}(\bar{x}_{3})}{d\bar{x}_{3}^{\bar{n}_{q}-1}}-\frac{\langle\beta^{\prime},\beta_{q}^{\prime\prime}\rangle}{(\bar{x}_{2}-\bar{x}_{3})^{\bar{n}_{q}}}-\frac{\langle\beta,\beta_{q}^{\prime\prime}\rangle}{(\bar{x}_{1}-\bar{x}_{3})^{\bar{n}_{q}}}-(-1)^{\bar{n}_{q}-1}\left(\frac{\langle\beta^{\prime},\beta_{q}^{\prime\prime}\rangle}{\bar{x}_{3}^{\bar{n}_{q}}}+\frac{\langle\beta,\beta_{q}^{\prime\prime}\rangle}{\bar{x}_{3}^{\bar{n}_{q}}}\right)\right]\typecolon\\
&\times\exp\left(\int\alpha(x_{1})^{+}\right)\exp\left(\int\alpha^{\prime}(x_{2})^{+}\right)\exp\left(\int\alpha^{\prime\prime}(x_{3})^{+}\right)\\
&\times\exp\left(\int\beta(\bar{x}_{1})^{+}\right)\exp\left(\int\beta^{\prime}(\bar{x}_{2})^{+}\right)\exp\left(\int\beta^{\prime\prime}(\bar{x}_{3})^{+}\right)\\
&\times{\rm e}_{\lambda}{\rm e}_{\lambda^{\prime}}{\rm e}_{\lambda^{\prime\prime}}x_{1}^{\alpha}\bar{x}_{1}^{\beta}x_{2}^{\alpha^{\prime}}\bar{x}_{2}^{\beta^{\prime}}x_{3}^{\alpha^{\prime\prime}}\bar{x}_{3}^{\beta^{\prime\prime}}\,.\end{split}

(392)

To prove locality, we substitute formal variables with complex numbers
x_{i}\to z_{i},\bar{x}_{i}\to\bar{z}_{i},\penalty\ i=1,2,3
. We can write

\begin{split}&Y_{V_{\Lambda}}(u,z_{1},\bar{z}_{1})Y_{V_{\Lambda}}(v,z_{2},\bar{z}_{2})Y_{V_{\Lambda}}(w,z_{3},\bar{z}_{3})\\
&=f(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3},\bar{z}_{1},\bar{z}_{2},\bar{z}_{3})\times\exp\left(\int\alpha(z_{1})^{-}\right)\exp\left(\int\alpha^{\prime}(z_{2})^{-}\right)\exp\left(\int\alpha^{\prime\prime}(z_{3})^{-}\right)\\
&\times\exp\left(\int\beta(\bar{z}_{1})^{-}\right)\exp\left(\int\beta^{\prime}(\bar{z}_{2})^{-}\right)\exp\left(\int\beta^{\prime\prime}(\bar{z}_{3})^{-}\right)\\
&\times\typecolon\prod_{i=1}^{k}\left[\frac{1}{(l_{i}-1)!}\frac{d^{l_{i}-1}\alpha_{i}(z_{1})}{dz_{1}^{l_{i}-1}}+f_{1,i}(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})\right]\prod_{j=1}^{\bar{k}}\left[\frac{1}{(\bar{l}_{j}-1)!}\frac{d^{\bar{l}_{j}-1}\beta_{j}(\bar{z}_{1})}{d\bar{z}_{1}^{\bar{l}_{j}-1}}+g_{1,j}(\bar{z}_{1},\bar{z}_{2},\bar{z}_{3})\right]\typecolon\\
&\times\typecolon\prod_{r=1}^{\ell}\left[\frac{1}{(m_{r}-1)!}\frac{d^{m_{r}-1}\alpha^{\prime}_{r}(z_{2})}{dz_{2}^{m_{r}-1}}+f_{2,r}(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})\right]\prod_{s=1}^{\bar{\ell}}\left[\frac{1}{(\bar{m}_{s}-1)!}\frac{d^{\bar{m}_{s}-1}\beta^{\prime}_{s}(\bar{z}_{2})}{d\bar{z}_{2}^{\bar{m}_{s}-1}}+g_{2,s}(\bar{z}_{1},\bar{z}_{2},\bar{z}_{3})\right]\typecolon\\
&\times\typecolon\prod_{p=1}^{m}\left[\frac{1}{(n_{p}-1)!}\frac{d^{n_{p}-1}\alpha_{p}^{\prime\prime}(z_{3})}{dz_{3}^{n_{p}-1}}+f_{3,p}(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})\right]\prod_{q=1}^{\bar{m}}\left[\frac{1}{(\bar{n}_{q}-1)!}\frac{d^{\bar{n}_{q}-1}\beta_{q}^{\prime\prime}(\bar{z}_{3})}{d\bar{z}_{3}^{\bar{n}_{q}-1}}+g_{3,q}(\bar{z}_{1},\bar{z}_{2},\bar{z}_{3})\right]\typecolon\\
&\times\exp\left(\int\alpha(z_{1})^{+}\right)\exp\left(\int\alpha^{\prime}(z_{2})^{+}\right)\exp\left(\int\alpha^{\prime\prime}(z_{3})^{+}\right)\\
&\times\exp\left(\int\beta(\bar{z}_{1})^{+}\right)\exp\left(\int\beta^{\prime}(\bar{z}_{2})^{+}\right)\exp\left(\int\beta^{\prime\prime}(\bar{z}_{3})^{+}\right){\rm e}_{\lambda}{\rm e}_{\lambda^{\prime}}{\rm e}_{\lambda^{\prime\prime}}z_{1}^{\alpha}\bar{z}_{1}^{\beta}z_{2}^{\alpha^{\prime}}\bar{z}_{2}^{\beta^{\prime}}z_{3}^{\alpha^{\prime\prime}}\bar{z}_{3}^{\beta^{\prime\prime}}\,,\end{split}

(393)

in the domain
|z_{1}|>|z_{2}|>|z_{3}|
,
where

\begin{split}&f(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3},\bar{z}_{1},\bar{z}_{2},\bar{z}_{3})\\
&=\exp\left(\langle\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\rangle\log(z_1-z_2)\right)\exp\left(\langle\beta,\beta^{\prime}\rangle\log(\bar{z}_1-\bar{z}_2)\right)\exp\left(\langle\alpha,\alpha^{\prime\prime}\rangle\log(z_1-z_3)\right)\\
&\quad\exp\left(\langle\beta,\beta^{\prime\prime}\rangle\log(\bar{z}_1-\bar{z}_3)\right)\exp\left(\langle\alpha^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime\prime}\rangle\log(z_2-z_3)\right)\exp\left(\langle\beta^{\prime},\beta^{\prime\prime}\rangle\log(\bar{z}_2-\bar{z}_3)\right),\\
&f_{1,i}(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})=(-1)^{l_{i}-1}\left(\frac{\langle\alpha^{\prime},\alpha_{i}\rangle}{\exp(l_i\log(z_1-z_2))}-\frac{\langle\alpha^{\prime},\alpha_{i}\rangle}{\exp(l_i\log z_1)}\right.\\
&\left.\hskip 170.71652pt+\frac{\langle\alpha^{\prime\prime},\alpha_{i}\rangle}{\exp(l_i\log(z_1-z_3))}-\frac{\langle\alpha^{\prime\prime},\alpha_{i}\rangle}{\exp(l_i\log z_1)}\right)\\
&g_{1,j}(\bar{z}_{1},\bar{z}_{2},\bar{z}_{3})=(-1)^{\bar{l}_{j}-1}\left(\frac{\langle\beta^{\prime},\beta_{j}\rangle}{\exp(\bar{l}_j\log(\bar{z}_1-\bar{z}_2))}-\frac{\langle\beta^{\prime},\beta_{j}\rangle}{\exp(\bar{l}_j\log\bar{z}_1)}\right.\\
&\left.\hskip 170.71652pt+\frac{\langle\beta^{\prime\prime},\beta_{j}\rangle}{\exp(\bar{l}_j\log(\bar{z}_1-\bar{z}_3))}-\frac{\langle\beta^{\prime\prime},\beta_{j}\rangle}{\exp(\bar{l}_j\log\bar{z}_1)}\right),\end{split}

(394)

\begin{split}&f_{2,r}(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})=(-1)^{m_{r}-1}\left(\frac{\langle\alpha^{\prime\prime},\alpha^{\prime}_{r}\rangle}{\exp(m_r\log(z_2-z_3))}-\frac{\langle\alpha^{\prime\prime},\alpha^{\prime}_{r}\rangle}{\exp(m_r\log z_2)}-\frac{\langle\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}_{r}\rangle}{\exp(m_r\log z_2)}\right)\\
&\hskip 312.9803pt-\frac{\langle\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}_{r}\rangle}{\exp(m_r\log(z_1-z_2))},\\
&g_{2,s}(\bar{z}_{1},\bar{z}_{2},\bar{z}_{3})=(-1)^{\bar{m}_{s}-1}\left(\frac{\langle\beta^{\prime\prime},\beta^{\prime}_{s}\rangle}{\exp(\bar{m}_s\log(\bar{z}_2- \bar{z}_3))}-\frac{\langle\beta^{\prime\prime},\beta^{\prime}_{s}\rangle}{\exp(m_r\log(\bar{z}_2))}-\frac{\langle\beta,\beta^{\prime}_{s}\rangle}{\exp(m_r\log(\bar{z}_2))}\right)\\
&\hskip 312.9803pt-\frac{\langle\beta,\beta^{\prime}_{s}\rangle}{\exp(\bar{m}_s\log(\bar{z}_1-\bar{z}_2))},\\
&f_{3,p}(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})=-\frac{\langle\alpha^{\prime},\alpha_{p}^{\prime\prime}\rangle}{\exp(n_p\log(z_2-z_3))}-\frac{\langle\alpha,\alpha_{p}^{\prime\prime}\rangle}{\exp(n_p\log(z_1-z_3))}\\
&\hskip 85.35826pt-(-1)^{n_{p}-1}\left(\frac{\langle\alpha^{\prime},\alpha_{p}^{\prime\prime}\rangle}{\exp(n_p\log z_3)}+\frac{\langle\alpha,\alpha_{p}^{\prime\prime}\rangle}{\exp(n_p\log z_3)}\right),\\
&g_{3,q}(\bar{z}_{1},\bar{z}_{2},\bar{z}_{3})=-\frac{\langle\beta^{\prime},\beta_{q}^{\prime\prime}\rangle}{\exp(\bar{n}_q\log(\bar{z}_2-\bar{z}_3))}-\frac{\langle\beta,\beta_{q}^{\prime\prime}\rangle}{\exp(\bar{n}_q\log(\bar{z}_1-\bar{z}_3))}\\
&\hskip 85.35826pt-(-1)^{\bar{n}_{q}-1}\left(\frac{\langle\beta^{\prime},\beta_{q}^{\prime\prime}\rangle}{\exp(\bar{n}_q\log\bar{z}_3)}+\frac{\langle\beta,\beta_{q}^{\prime\prime}\rangle}{\exp(\bar{n}_q\log\bar{z}_3)}\right).\end{split}

(395)

One can check that under any permutation
\sigma\in S_{3}
of the vertex operators, the product is given by the right hand side of (
393
) in the domain
|z_{\sigma(1)}|>|z_{\sigma(2)}|>|z_{\sigma(3)}|
.

For more than three vertex operators, the locality can be proved in a similar way.

Appendix C
Heisenberg algebras and their representations

In this appendix, we prove some properties of Heisenberg algebras which is used in the paper. See
(
Frenkel:1988xz
, Section 1.7)
for definitions. We start with some results about Lie algebras.

C.1
Preliminaries

Let
U
be a left
R
-module and
D=\mathrm{End}_{R}(U)
be the ring of
R
-linear endomorphisms of
U
. One can consider
U
as a left
D
-module by defining

\varphi\cdot u=\varphi(u),\quad\varphi\in D,\penalty\ \penalty\ u\in U\,.

(396)

The
D
-linear (in)dependence of a subset of
U
is defined in an obvious way.
A
D
-linear operator on
U
is a map
f:U\longrightarrow U
such that

f(\varphi\cdot u-v)=\varphi\cdot f(u)-f(v)=\varphi(f(u))-f(v),\quad\varphi\in D,\penalty\ \penalty\ u,v\in U\,.

(397)

{thm}

(Jacobson Density Theorem,
(
isaacs
, Theorem 13.14)
)

Let
U
be a simple left
R
-module and write
D=\operatorname{End}_{R}(U)
. Let
\alpha
be any
D
-linear operator on
U
and let
X\subseteq U
be any finite
D
-linearly independent subset. Then there exists an element
r\in R
such that
rx=\alpha x
for all
x\in X
.

Finally, we will need a generalisation of Schur’s lemma to countably-infinite dimensional representations.

{lemma}
(Dixmier’s Lemma,
(
libine2021introduction
, Lemma 100)
)

Suppose
V
is a vector space over

\mathbb{C}
of countable dimension and that
S\subset\mathrm{End}(V)
acts irreducibly. If
T\in\mathrm{End}(V)
commutes with every element of
S
,
then
T
is a scalar multiple of the identity operator.

{prop}

Let
\mathfrak{g}_{1},\mathfrak{g}_{2}
be two complex Lie algebras (possibly countably infinite dimensional). Then every irreducible
(\mathfrak{g}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{2})
-module which contains an irreducible
\mathfrak{g}_{1}
or
\mathfrak{g}_{2}
-module is isomorphic to the tensor product of two irreducible
\mathfrak{g}_{1},\mathfrak{g}_{2}
-modules. Conversely, the tensor product of irreducible
\mathfrak{g}_{1},\mathfrak{g}_{2}
-modules of countable dimension is an irreducible
(\mathfrak{g}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{2})
-module.

{proof}

Let
V
be an irreducible
(\mathfrak{g}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{2})
-module and suppose
Y\subseteq V
is an irreducible
\mathfrak{g}_{2}
-module.
X=\text{Hom}_{\mathfrak{g}_{2}}(Y,V)
is the space of
\mathfrak{g}_{2}
-linear maps from
Y
to
V
, i.e.

\begin{split}X=&\{\phi:Y\to V\,\lvert\,\phi(g_{2}\cdot v-w)=g_{2}\cdot\phi(v)-\phi(w),\penalty\ \forall g_{2}\in\mathfrak{g}_{2},v,w\in Y\}\,.\end{split}

(398)

Then
X
is a
\mathfrak{g}_{1}
-module under the bilinear map

(g_{1},\phi)\mapsto g_{1}\cdot\phi:y\mapsto g_{1}\cdot(\phi(y))\,.

(399)

It is easy to see that
X\otimes Y
is a
(\mathfrak{g}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{2})
-module with the action

(g_{1},g_{2})\cdot(\phi\otimes y)=(g_{1}\cdot\phi_{g})\otimes y+\phi\otimes(g_{2}\cdot y)\,.

(400)

Moreover the natural map from
X\otimes Y
to
V

\phi\otimes y\mapsto\phi(y)\,,

(401)

is a
(\mathfrak{g}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{2})
-module map. We first show the map (
401
) is non-zero. Take
\phi=\text{id}:Y\to V
, which clearly lies in
X
, then
\text{id}\otimes y\mapsto\text{id}(y)=y
. Hence, the (non-zero) image of this
(\mathfrak{g}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{2})
-module map, (
401
), is a
(\mathfrak{g}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{2})
-module. As
V
is an irreducible
(\mathfrak{g}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{2})
-module, the image is entire
V
, and the map (
401
) is surjective. Finally, we show this map is injective. Indeed suppose
\phi\otimes v\mapsto\phi(v)=0
. Then it suffices to show that either
v=0
or
\phi=0
. Suppose
v\neq 0
. By
\mathfrak{g}_{2}
-linearity of
\phi
, we see that

0=g_{2}\cdot\phi(v)=\phi(g_{2}\cdot v),\quad\forall g_{2}\in\mathfrak{g}_{2}\,.

(402)

By irreducibility of
Y
, we see that
\phi=0
. This implies that
V\cong X\otimes Y
as
(\mathfrak{g}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{2})
-modules, and that the latter is also an irreducible module. As
X\otimes Y
is an irreducible
(\mathfrak{g}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{2})
-module,
X
must be an irreducible
\mathfrak{g}_{1}
module. Similar arguments apply when
Y
is an irreducible
\mathfrak{g}_{1}
-module.

To prove the converse, we follow
stex1
. We want to show that if
X,Y
are irreducible
\mathfrak{g}_{1},\mathfrak{g}_{2}
-modules, then
X\otimes Y
with
(\mathfrak{g}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{2})
-action defined by

(g_{1},g_{2})\cdot(x\otimes y)=(g_{1}\cdot x)\otimes y+x\otimes(g_{2}\cdot y)\,,

(403)

is an irreducible
(\mathfrak{g}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{2})
-module. Suppose
M\subset X\otimes Y
be a non-trivial
(\mathfrak{g}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{2})
-submodule. It suffices to show that
M
contains non-trivial pure tensors and then the irreducibility of
X,Y
will imply that
M=X\otimes Y
. Let us assume that
x\otimes y
is a pure tensor in
M
. The irreducibility of
X,Y
guarantees that
\mathfrak{g}_{1}\cdot x=X
and
\mathfrak{g}_{2}\cdot y=Y
, which guarantees that
x\otimes Y
and
X\otimes y\subset M
. Consider any pure tensor
\tilde{x}\otimes\tilde{y}\in V
, we know that
\tilde{x}\otimes y
belongs in
M
. Applying only
\mathfrak{g}_{2}
on this vector, we can show that
\tilde{x}\otimes\tilde{y}\in M
, which implies that
M=X\otimes Y
, since pure tensors span
X\otimes Y
.

We now show that
M
contains a pure tensor. Since any
\mathfrak{g}
-module is also a
U(\mathfrak{g})
-module, our strategy is to produce a pure tensor from an arbitrary vector in
M
using the action of an element of
U(\mathfrak{g}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{2})
.
Start with an arbitrary non-zero vector

v=\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}\otimes y_{i}\in M\,.

(404)

Without loss of generality, we may choose
\left\{x_{i}\right\}
to be linearly independent over
\mathbb{C}
and
y_{i}\neq 0
for all
i
. By Dixmier’s Lemma (or Schur’s lemma when
X,Y
are finite dimensional), we have

\operatorname{End}_{U(\mathfrak{g}_{1})}\left(X\right)\cong\mathbb{C},\quad\operatorname{End}_{U(\mathfrak{g}_{2})}\left(Y\right)\cong\mathbb{C}\,.

(405)

In the statement of Jacobson Density Theorem, choose
U=X,R=U(\mathfrak{g}_{1})
, hence
D=\text{End}_{U(\mathfrak{g}_{1})}(X)=\mathbb{C}
, due to (
405
). Thus, let us take
\{x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\}
, which is a finite
D
-linearly independent subset. Consider a
\mathbb{C}
- linear map
\alpha
, such that

\alpha x_{i}=\begin{cases}x_{1}&i=1,\\
0&i>1.\end{cases}

(406)

We can then use Jacobson Density Theorem to conclude that there exists
u\in U(\mathfrak{g}_{1})
such that

\alpha x_{i}=ux_{i}\,,

(407)

\forall\,1\leq i\leq n
. Hence, for
u\otimes 1\in U(\mathfrak{g}_{1})\otimes U(\mathfrak{g}_{2})\cong U\left(\mathfrak{g}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{2}\right)
,

(u\otimes 1)v=x_{1}\otimes y_{1}\,,

(408)

which is a pure tensor.

C.2
Modules of direct sum of Heisenberg algebras

We now prove some basic results about the modules of direct sum of Heisenberg algebras. We will use the notations of
(
Frenkel:1988xz
, Chapter 1)
in this section.

For a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded vector space

\mathfrak{g}=\bigoplus_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathfrak{g}_{n}\,,

(409)

we will write

\mathfrak{g}^{+}:=\bigoplus_{n>0}\mathfrak{g}_{n},\quad\mathfrak{g}^{-}:=\bigoplus_{n<0}\mathfrak{g}_{n},\quad\mathfrak{g}^{0}:=\mathfrak{g}_{0}\,.

(410)

Let
\mathfrak{g}_{1},\mathfrak{g}_{2}
be two Heisenberg algebras with central elements
k
and
\bar{\textbf{k}}
respectively.
Analogous to the
\mathfrak{C}_{k}
condition for a module of a Heisenberg algebra (see
(
Frenkel:1988xz
, Page 23)
), we define
the
\mathfrak{C}_{k,\bar{k}}
condition for an
(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R})
- graded module
V
of the direct sum
\mathfrak{g}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{2}
of two Heisenberg algebras
\mathfrak{g}_{1},\mathfrak{g}_{2}
. We say that
V
satisfies the
\mathfrak{C}_{k,\bar{k}}
condition if

1.

k
and
\bar{\textbf{k}}
act on
V
by multiplication with
k
and
\bar{k}
respectively.

2.

There exist
M_{1},M_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
, such that
V_{m,n}=0
, if either
m>M_{1}
and
n>M_{2}
.

The
vacuum space
of
V
, denoted by
\Omega_{V}\subset V
, is a subspace of non-zero vectors such that any
v\in\Omega_{V}
satisfies
(\mathfrak{g}_{1}^{+}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{2}^{+})\cdot v=0
. Let
M(k),M(\bar{k})
denote the unique (up to isomorphism) irreducible module of
\mathfrak{g}_{1},\mathfrak{g}_{2}
respectively. In particular, we have
(
Frenkel:1988xz
, Section 1.7)

M(k)\cong S(\mathfrak{g}_{1}^{-}),\quad M(\bar{k})\cong S(\mathfrak{g}_{2}^{-})\,.

(411)

Then we have the following proposition.

{prop}

Let
\mathfrak{g}_{1},\mathfrak{g}_{2}
be two Heisenberg algebras with central elements
\mathbf{k},\bar{\mathbf{k}}
respectively. Then the following are true:

1.

M(k)\otimes M(\bar{k})
is the unique (up to isomorphism) irreducible
(\mathfrak{g}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{2})
-module satisfying condition
\mathfrak{C}_{k,\bar{k}}
.

2.

The vacuum space
\Omega_{M(k)\otimes M(\bar{k})}
is one-dimensional and

\Omega_{M(k)\otimes M(\bar{k})}=\mathbb{C}(1_{M(k)}\otimes 1_{M(\bar{k})})\,.

(412)

3.

For any
(\mathfrak{g}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{2})
-module satisfying condition
\mathfrak{C}_{k,\bar{k}}
, the
(\mathfrak{g}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{2})
-module generated by a vacuum vector is equivalent to
M(k)\otimes M(\bar{k})
.

{proof}

(1) Any
(\mathfrak{g}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{2})
-module is in particular a
\mathfrak{g}_{2}
-module and hence completely reducible by
(
Frenkel:1988xz
, Proposition 1.7.2)
. Thus it contains an irreducible
\mathfrak{g}_{2}
-module. By Proposition
C.1
, every such irreducible
(\mathfrak{g}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{2})
-module is isomorphic to a tensor product
X\otimes Y
where
X
,
Y
are irreducible
\mathfrak{g}_{1},\mathfrak{g}_{2}
-module respectively. By
(
Frenkel:1988xz
, Proposition 1.7.2)
we have
X\otimes Y\cong M(k)\otimes M(\bar{k})
.

(2) Since
M(k),M(\bar{k})
satisfies the conditions
\mathfrak{C}_{k},\mathfrak{C}_{\bar{k}}
of
(
Frenkel:1988xz
, Page 23)
, it is clear that
M(k)\otimes M(\bar{k})
satisfies the condition
\mathfrak{C}_{k,\bar{k}}
. The vacuum space of
M(k)\otimes M(\bar{k})
is easily seen to be the tensor product of the vacuum spaces of
M(k)
and
M(\bar{k})
respectively and hence is one-dimensional.

(3) Let
V
be any
(\mathfrak{g}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{2})
-module satisfying condition
\mathfrak{C}_{k,\bar{k}}
. Let
v\in V
and consider the
(\mathfrak{g}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{2})
-submodule of
V

\{(g_{1},g_{2})\cdot v:(g_{1},g_{2})\in\mathfrak{g}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{2}\}\,.

(413)

It is easily seen that this gives an irreducible
(\mathfrak{g}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{2})
-module and is isomorphic to
M(k)\otimes M(\bar{k})
by (1).

We now have the following theorem.

{thm}

Any
(\mathfrak{g}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{2})
-module is completely reducible and is isomorphic to copies of
M(k)\otimes M(\bar{k})
. More precisely, for any such module
V
, the (well-defined) canonical linear map

\begin{split}&f\,:\,U(\mathfrak{g}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{2})\otimes_{U(\mathfrak{b}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{b}_{2})}\Omega_{V}\longrightarrow V\,,\\
&u\otimes v\mapsto u\cdot v,\quad u\in U(\mathfrak{g}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{2}),v\in\Omega_{V}\,,\end{split}

(414)

is a
(\mathfrak{g}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{2})
-module isomorphism. In particular, the linear map

\begin{split}M(k)\otimes M(\bar{k})\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\Omega_{V}\,\cong&U(\mathfrak{g}_{1}^{-}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{2}^{-})\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\Omega_{V}\longrightarrow V\,,\\
&u\otimes v\mapsto u\cdot v,\quad u\in U(\mathfrak{g}_{1}^{-}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{2}^{-}),v\in\Omega_{V}\,,\end{split}

(415)

defines a
(\mathfrak{g}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{2})
-module isomorphism,
\Omega_{V}
now regarded as a trivial
(\mathfrak{g}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{2})
-module.

{proof}

We closely follow
Frenkel:1988xz
for this proof.
First, we show the
f
in (
414
) is an injective map. Note that from the action of
f
, it is clear that
f
is injective on
1\otimes\Omega_{V}\hookrightarrow U(\mathfrak{g}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{2})\otimes_{U(\mathfrak{b}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{b}_{2})}\Omega_{V}
. Let
K
be the kernel of
f
. Then it is easy to see that
K
is a
(\mathfrak{g}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{2})
-submodule of
U(\mathfrak{g}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{2})\otimes_{U(\mathfrak{b}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{b}_{2})}\Omega_{V}
and has a grading induced from
U(\mathfrak{g}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{2})\otimes_{U(\mathfrak{b}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{b}_{2})}\Omega_{V}
. Thus
K
satisfies the condition

\mathfrak{C}_{k,\bar{k}}
. It follows that it has a vacuum vector, say
v\in K
. But then
v\in\Omega_{V}
because the vacuum space of
U(\mathfrak{g}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{2})\otimes_{U(\mathfrak{b}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{b}_{2})}\Omega_{V}
is precisely
\Omega_{V}
. Since
f(v)=0
, it contradicts the fact that
f
is injective on
\Omega_{V}
.

We now prove the surjectivity of
f
. Suppose
V/\text{Im}(f)\neq 0
. Since
\text{Im}(f)
is a
(\mathfrak{g}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{2})
-submodule of
V
,
V/\text{Im}(f)
is naturally a
(\mathfrak{g}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{2})
-module and satisfies the condition
\mathfrak{C}_{k,\bar{k}}
since
V
does. Then there exists a vacuum vector
w\in V/\text{Im}(f)
. Let
w=[v]
for some
v\in V
. It follows that
v\not\in\text{Im}(f)
and

x_{1i}\cdot v,x_{2i}\cdot v\in\text{Im}(f),\quad i\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}\,,

(416)

since
x_{1i}\cdot[v],x_{2i}\cdot[v]=0
. Moreover, due to the
\mathfrak{C}_{k,\bar{k}}
property, there exists
i_{0},j_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
such that

x_{1i}\cdot v,x_{2j}\cdot v=0\penalty\ \penalty\ \text{for all}\penalty\ \penalty\ i>i_{0},j>j_{0}\,.

(417)

We will now show that there exists
t\in\text{Im}(f)
such that

x_{ki}\cdot t=x_{ki}\cdot v,\penalty\ \penalty\ \text{for all}\penalty\ \penalty\ i\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}\text{ and }k\in\{1,2\}\,.

(418)

This will imply that
t-v
is a vacuum vector but
t-v\in V\setminus\Omega_{V}
, since
t-v\not\in\text{Im}(f)
and
\Omega_{V}\subset\text{Im}(f)
, which is a contradiction. To this end, choose a basis
\{\omega_{\gamma}\}_{\gamma\in\Gamma}
(
\Gamma
an index set) of
\Omega_{V}
. Then by injectivity of
f
and the first isomorphism theorem

\text{Im}(f)\cong\coprod_{\gamma\in\Gamma}U(\mathfrak{g}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{2})\otimes_{U(\mathfrak{b}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{b}_{2})}\mathbb{C}\omega_{\gamma}\,.

(419)

Let
s^{1}_{i\gamma},s^{2}_{j\gamma}
be the component of
x_{1i}\cdot v,x_{2j}\cdot v
respectively under this decomposition. Then for any
i,i^{\prime},j,j^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
we have

x_{1i}x_{1i^{\prime}}\cdot v=x_{1i^{\prime}}x_{1i}\cdot v,\quad x_{2i}x_{2i^{\prime}}\cdot v=x_{2i^{\prime}}x_{2i}\cdot v.

(420)

as the Lie Bracket is zero on
\mathfrak{g}_{1}^{+}
and
\mathfrak{g}_{2}^{+}
. This implies that for all
\gamma\in\Gamma

x_{1i}\cdot s^{1}_{i^{\prime}\gamma}=x_{1i^{\prime}}\cdot s^{1}_{i\gamma},\quad x_{2j}\cdot s^{2}_{j^{\prime}\gamma}=x_{2j^{\prime}}\cdot s^{2}_{j\gamma}.

(421)

It is also clear from (
417
) that

s^{1}_{i\gamma}=s^{2}_{j\gamma}=0,\quad\text{for all}\penalty\ \penalty\ i>i_{0},j>j_{0}\,.

(422)

Moreover there is a finite subset
\Gamma_{0}\subset\Gamma
such that

s^{1}_{i\gamma}=s^{2}_{j\gamma}=0,\quad\text{for all}\penalty\ \penalty\ \gamma\in\Gamma\setminus\Gamma_{0}\,,

(423)

since any vector in Im
(f)
, in particular
x_{1i}\cdot v
and
x_{2i}\cdot v
, has a finite decomposition in (
419
). Now, fix a
\gamma\in\Gamma_{0}
and identify
U(\mathfrak{g}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{2})\otimes_{U(\mathfrak{b}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{b}_{2})}\mathbb{C}\omega_{\gamma}
as the polynomial algebra over generators
y_{1i},y_{2i}
. Then (
421
) implies that

\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{1i}}s^{1}_{i^{\prime}\gamma}=\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{1i^{\prime}}}s^{1}_{i\gamma},\quad\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{2j}}s^{2}_{j^{\prime}\gamma}=\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{2j^{\prime}}}s^{2}_{j\gamma},\quad i,i^{\prime},j,j^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}\,.

(424)

Since
s^{1}_{i\gamma},s^{2}_{j\gamma}=0
for
i>i_{0},j>j_{0}
, it is clear from (
424
) that
s^{1}_{i\gamma},s^{2}_{j\gamma}
lie in the polynomial algebra generated by finitely many
y_{1i},y_{2j}
respectively for
i\leq i_{0},j\leq j_{0}
. Thus there exists
s^{1},s^{2}
in these algebras such that

k\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{1i}}s^{1}=s^{1}_{i\gamma},\quad\bar{k}\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{2i}}s^{2}=s^{2}_{i\gamma}\,,

(425)

for
i\leq i_{0},j\leq j_{0}
and hence for all
i,j\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
. We then take
t^{1}_{\gamma}=s^{1},t^{2}_{\gamma}=s^{2}
and put

t:=\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}(t^{1}_{\gamma}+t^{2}_{\gamma})\,.

(426)

Note that
x_{2j}\cdot t^{1}_{\gamma}=x_{1i}\cdot t^{2}_{\gamma}=0
and hence (
418
) holds.

Appendix D
Proof of Conjecture
3.3.1
for
m=n
Case

Suppose
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{R}^{m,m}
is an even, self-dual Lorentzian lattice. We want to show that for any
f\in\text{Aut}(\Lambda)
we have

f(\Lambda_{1}^{0})=\Lambda_{1}^{0},\quad f(\Lambda_{2}^{0})=\Lambda_{2}^{0}.

(427)

We will prove this in a series of results.

{lemma}

Let
\Lambda
and
\tilde{\Lambda}
be two Lorentzian lattice related by an
\mathrm{O}(m,\mathbb{R})\times\mathrm{O}(m,\mathbb{R})
transformation
O
, i.e.

\mathcal{G}_{\tilde{\Lambda}}=\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}O.

(428)

Then we have

\mathrm{Aut}(\tilde{\Lambda})=O\mathrm{Aut}(\Lambda)O^{-1}.

(429)

Moreover
\mathrm{Aut}(\Lambda)
preserves
\Lambda_{i}^{0}
if and only if
\mathrm{Aut}(\tilde{\Lambda})
preserves
\tilde{\Lambda}_{i}^{0}
, where
i\in\{1,2\}
.

{proof}

Let
f\in\text{Aut}(\Lambda)
, then
\tilde{f}=OfO^{-1}\in\text{Aut}(\tilde{\Lambda})
. Thus
O\mathrm{Aut}(\Lambda)O^{-1}\subseteq\mathrm{Aut}(\tilde{\Lambda})
. The reverse containment is similar.

Now suppose Aut
(\tilde{\Lambda})
preserves
\tilde{\Lambda}_{1}^{0}
. For any
(\alpha,0)\in\Lambda_{1}^{0}
, we have
f(\alpha,0)=(O^{-1}\tilde{f}O)(\alpha,0)
for some
\tilde{f}\in\mathrm{Aut}(\tilde{\Lambda})
. Since
O,O^{-1}
preserves
\langle\alpha,\alpha\rangle
and
\langle\alpha,\alpha\rangle
for any
(\alpha,\beta)\in\Lambda
it is clear that

f(\alpha,0)=(\alpha^{\prime},0)\in\Lambda_{1}^{0}.

(430)

Similarly,
f
preserves
\Lambda_{2}^{0}
. The converse is analogous.

{thm}

Let
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{R}^{m,m}
be any even, self-dual Lorentzian lattice, then their exists an
\mathrm{O}(m,\mathbb{R})\times\mathrm{O}(m,\mathbb{R})
transformation which relates
\Lambda
to a Lorentzian lattice
\Lambda_{S}
with generator matrix of the form

\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda_{S}}=\begin{pmatrix}\gamma^{\star}&\gamma^{\star}\\
\gamma\frac{B+\mathds{1}}{2}&\gamma\frac{B-\mathds{1}}{2}\end{pmatrix}\,,

(431)

where
B
is an anti-symmetric matrix,
\gamma
is the generator matrix for a lattice
\Gamma
in
\mathbb{R}^{m}
, and
\gamma^{\star}
is the generator matrix for the dual lattice
\Gamma^{\star}
.

{proof}

We will use the result of
(
Dymarsky:2020qom
, Appendix C)
for the proof of this theorem. Let
\{e_{i}\}_{i=1}^{2m}
be the standard basis of
\mathbb{R}^{m,m}
. Then we change the basis of
\mathbb{R}^{m,m}
from standard basis to the basis
\{f_{i}\}_{i=1}^{2m}
:

\begin{split}f_{i}&=\frac{e_{i}+e_{m+i}}{\sqrt{2}},\\
f_{m+i}&=\frac{e_{i}-e_{m+i}}{\sqrt{2}},\end{split}

(432)

where
i\in\{1,\dots,m\}
. Let
\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\Lambda}
be the generator matrix for
\Lambda
in the
\{e_{i}\}
and
\{f_{i}\}
basis respectively. Now from Appendix C of
Dymarsky:2020qom
, by an
\mathrm{O}(m,\mathbb{R})\times\mathrm{O}(m,\mathbb{R})
transformation, we can transform
\Lambda
into the lattice
\Lambda_{S}
with generator matrix
30
30

30

Note that in our convention, the rows of the generator matrix are basis for the lattice while in
Dymarsky:2020qom
it is the columns.

\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\Lambda_{S}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\begin{array}[]{c|c}2\gamma^{\star}&0\\
\hline\cr\gamma B&\gamma\end{array}\right),

(433)

in the
\{f_{i}\}
basis for some antisymmetric matrix
B
. Changing the basis of
\mathbb{R}^{m,m}
back to the standard basis
\{e_{i}\}
amounts to right multiplying the generator matrix (
433
) by

\begin{pmatrix}\frac{\mathds{1}}{\sqrt{2}}&\frac{\mathds{1}}{\sqrt{2}}\\
\frac{\mathds{1}}{\sqrt{2}}&-\frac{\mathds{1}}{\sqrt{2}}\end{pmatrix},

(434)

where
\mathds{1}
is the
m\times m
identity matrix. This gives the generator matrix in (
431
).

We now have the following important theorem.

{thm}

Let
\Lambda_{S}\subset\mathbb{R}^{m,m}
be the Lorentzian lattice with generator matrix
\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda_{S}}
given in (
431
). Then
\mathrm{Aut}(\Lambda_{S})
preserves
(\Lambda_{S})_{i}^{0},\penalty\ i=1,2
.

{proof}

Let
\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}
be an integral basis of
\Gamma
and
\{\alpha^{\star}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}
be the basis of
\Gamma^{\star}
dual to
\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}
, i.e.

\sum_{k=1}^{m}(\alpha_{i})^{k}(\alpha^{\star}_{j})^{k}=\delta_{i}^{j}\,,\quad\sum_{k=1}^{m}(\alpha^{\star}_{k})^{i}(\alpha_{k})^{j}=\delta_{i}^{j}.

(435)

Here the superscript
j
over the vector denotes the
j
th component of the vector in the standard basis of
\mathbb{R}^{m}
.
A general vector
\lambda\in\Lambda_{S}
can be written as
\lambda=(\alpha,\beta)
where

\begin{split}&\alpha^{j}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}m_{i}(\alpha^{\star}_{i})^{j}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{m}B_{jk}(\alpha_{i})^{k}+(\alpha_{i})^{j}\right)n_{i},\\
&\beta^{j}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}m_{i}(\alpha^{\star}_{i})^{j}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{m}B_{jk}(\alpha_{i})^{k}-(\alpha_{i})^{j}\right)n_{i},\end{split}

(436)

and
m_{i},n_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
. In vector notation we have

\begin{split}&\vec{\alpha}=\vec{m}^{T}\gamma^{\star}+\vec{n}^{T}\left(\gamma\frac{B+\mathds{1}}{2}\right),\\
&\vec{\beta}=\vec{m}^{T}\gamma^{\star}+\vec{n}^{T}\left(\gamma\frac{B-\mathds{1}}{2}\right).\end{split}

(437)

We then have

\begin{split}\langle\alpha,\alpha\rangle&=\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}\left[m_{i}m_{j}\langle\alpha^{\star}_{i},\alpha^{\star}_{j}\rangle+2\times\frac{1}{2}m_{i}n_{j}\sum_{k,\ell=1}^{m}B_{k\ell}(\alpha_{i}^{\star})^{k}(\alpha_{j})^{\ell}+2\times\frac{1}{2}m_{i}n_{j}\langle\alpha^{\star}_{i},\alpha_{j}\rangle\right.\\
&\left.+\frac{1}{4}\sum_{k,\ell,p=1}^{m}n_{i}n_{p}B_{jk}(\alpha_{i})^{k}B_{j,\ell}(\alpha_{p})^{\ell}+2\times\frac{1}{4}\sum_{k,\ell=1}^{m}n_{i}n_{\ell}B_{jk}(\alpha_{i})^{k}(\alpha_{l})^{j}+\frac{1}{4}n_{i}n_{j}\langle\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j}\rangle\right]\\
&=\vec{m}^{T}\mathbf{g}^{-1}\vec{m}+\sum_{i,j,k,\ell,p,q=1}^{m}m_{i}n_{j}(\alpha^{\star}_{i})^{p}(\alpha^{\star}_{q})^{p}(\alpha_{q})^{k}B_{k\ell}(\alpha_{j})^{\ell}+\vec{m}^{T}\vec{n}\\
&+\frac{1}{4}\sum_{i,j,k,\ell,p,u,v,s,t=1}^{m}n_{i}n_{p}(\alpha^{\star})^{j}_{u}(\alpha_{u})^{s}B_{sk}(\alpha_{i})^{k}(\alpha^{\star})^{j}_{v}(\alpha_{v})^{t}B_{t\ell}(\alpha_{p})^{\ell}+\frac{1}{4}\vec{n}^{T}\mathbf{g}\vec{n}\\
&=\vec{m}^{T}\mathbf{g}^{-1}\vec{m}+\vec{m}^{T}\mathbf{bg}^{-1}\vec{n}+\vec{m}^{T}\vec{n}-\frac{1}{4}\vec{n}^{T}\mathbf{b}\mathbf{g}^{-1}\mathbf{b}\vec{n}+\frac{1}{4}\vec{n}^{T}\mathbf{g}\vec{n}\\
&=\vec{m}^{T}\mathbf{g}^{-1}\vec{m}+\vec{m}^{T}\mathbf{bg}^{-1}\vec{n}+\vec{m}^{T}\vec{n}+\frac{1}{4}\vec{n}^{T}(\mathbf{g}-\mathbf{b}\mathbf{g}^{-1}\mathbf{b})\vec{n},\end{split}

(438)

where
\vec{m},\vec{n}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}
are column vectors,
\mathbf{g}_{ij}=\langle\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j}\rangle
and
\mathbf{g}^{\star}_{ij}=\mathbf{g}^{-1}_{ij}=\langle\alpha^{\star}_{i},\alpha_{j}^{\star}\rangle
are the Gram matrices of
\Gamma
and
\Gamma^{\star}
respectively and
\mathbf{b}_{ij}=(\alpha_{i})^{k}B_{k\ell}(\alpha_{j})^{\ell}
is the antisymmetric matrix
B
in the
\{\alpha_{i}\}
basis of
\mathbb{R}^{m}
. In the last step we used (
435
) and the fact that
B
and
\mathbf{b}
are antisymmetric. In terms of matrices we have

\begin{split}\mathbf{g}=\gamma\gamma^{T},&\quad\mathbf{g}^{\star}=\gamma^{\star}(\gamma^{\star})^{T}=(\gamma^{T})^{-1}\gamma^{-1}=\mathbf{g}^{-1},\\
&\mathbf{b}=\gamma B\gamma^{T},\quad B=\gamma^{-1}\mathbf{b}\gamma^{\star}.\end{split}

(439)

Similarly we have

\langle\beta,\beta\rangle=\vec{m}^{T}\mathbf{g}^{-1}\vec{m}+\vec{m}^{T}\mathbf{bg}^{-1}\vec{n}-\vec{m}^{T}\vec{n}+\frac{1}{4}\vec{n}^{T}(\mathbf{g}-\mathbf{b}\mathbf{g}^{-1}\mathbf{b})\vec{n}.

(440)

From
(
Blumenhagen:2013fgp
, Chapter 10)
, we have that the following transformations generate the group
\mathrm{O}(m,m,\mathbb{Z})
:

\displaystyle(1)\penalty\ \penalty\ \vec{m}\leftrightarrow\vec{n}\quad\text{ and }\quad 2\mathbf{g}^{-1}

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathbf{g}-\mathbf{bg}^{-1}\mathbf{b}\right)

(441)

\displaystyle\mathbf{bg}^{-1}

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow-\mathbf{g}^{-1}\mathbf{b}\,,

\displaystyle(2)\penalty\ \penalty\ \vec{m}\rightarrow\vec{m}-\mathbf{N}\vec{n}\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle\mathbf{b}\rightarrow\mathbf{b}+2\mathbf{N}\,,

where
\mathbf{N}
is an arbitrary anti-symmetric matrix with integer entries. These transformations must be understood in the following way: a transformation on the lattice can be implemented in two ways, first by action on the integer coordinates
(\vec{m}^{T},\vec{n}^{T})
and second, by acting on the generator matrix
\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda_{S}}
from the left. The transformations in (
441
) is a composition of both of these. To show that these transformations indeed generate the automorphism group
\mathrm{O}_{\Lambda_{S}}(m,m,\mathbb{Z})
we need to show that these transformations leave the inner product invariant and preserves the lattice.
It is easy to check that under these transformations the
\langle\alpha,\alpha\rangle
and
\langle\beta,\beta\rangle
are preserved
Blumenhagen:2013fgp
. In particular these transformations preserve the Lorentzian inner product. We now show that these transformations preserve the lattice. Let us start with (1). It is clear that
\vec{m}\leftrightarrow\vec{n}
preserves the lattice. We now check that the transformation on the generator matrix preserves the lattice. First note that from (
441
) we get
31
31

31

Note that we are assuming that
b
and
B
are invertible in writing this transformation but one can also write the transformation in a nonsingular way even when
b
is not invertible
Giveon:1994fu
. The manipulations below will also be modified in a nonsingular way.

\displaystyle\mathbf{g}^{-1}\leftrightarrow\frac{1}{4}\left(\mathbf{g}-\textbf{b}\mathbf{g}^{-1}\textbf{b}\right)\,,

(442)

\displaystyle\mathbf{b}\leftrightarrow 4\left(\textbf{b}-\mathbf{g}\textbf{b}^{-1}\mathbf{g}\right)^{-1}\,.

From these transformations, we want to find how
\gamma
and
\gamma^{\star}
transform. Using the fact that
\mathbf{b}
is antisymmetric, we guess the transformation to be

\displaystyle\gamma\leftrightarrow\pm 2\left(\gamma\pm\mathbf{b}\gamma^{\star}\right)^{\star}=:\pm\gamma_{\pm}\,,

(443)

\displaystyle\gamma^{\star}\leftrightarrow\pm\frac{1}{2}\left(\gamma\pm\mathbf{b}\gamma^{\star}\right)=:\pm\gamma^{\star}_{\pm}\,.

It is easy to check that (
443
) reproduces the first equation in (
442
). These are all the solutions to the first equation in (
441
) but as we will show below, only two of them preserve the lattice. We also see that under the second equation of (
442
) we have

B=\gamma^{-1}\mathbf{b}\gamma^{\star}\leftrightarrow\left(\gamma+\mathbf{b}\gamma^{\star}\right)^{T}\left(\mathbf{b}-\mathbf{g}\mathbf{b}^{-1}\mathbf{g}\right)^{-1}\left(\gamma+\mathbf{b}\gamma^{\star}\right)=:B^{\prime}\text{. }

(444)

We claim that the following two transformations on the generator matrix preserve the lattice:

\begin{split}(\text{i})\penalty\ \penalty\ &\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda_{S}}=\begin{pmatrix}\gamma^{\star}&\gamma^{\star}\\
\gamma\frac{B+\mathds{1}}{2}&\gamma\frac{B-\mathds{1}}{2}\end{pmatrix}\to\mathcal{G}^{\text{(i)}}_{\Lambda_{S}}:=\begin{pmatrix}\gamma^{\star}_{+}&-\gamma^{\star}_{-}\\
\gamma_{+}\frac{B^{\prime}+\mathds{1}}{2}&-\gamma_{-}\frac{B^{\prime}-\mathds{1}}{2}\end{pmatrix},\\
(\text{ii})\penalty\ \penalty\ &\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda_{S}}=\begin{pmatrix}\gamma^{\star}&\gamma^{\star}\\
\gamma\frac{B+\mathds{1}}{2}&\gamma\frac{B-\mathds{1}}{2}\end{pmatrix}\to\mathcal{G}^{\text{(ii)}}_{\Lambda_{S}}:=\begin{pmatrix}-\gamma^{\star}_{+}&\gamma^{\star}_{-}\\
-\gamma_{+}\frac{B^{\prime}+\mathds{1}}{2}&\gamma_{-}\frac{B^{\prime}-\mathds{1}}{2}\end{pmatrix}.\end{split}

(445)

We now do some manipulations to prove our claim. We have

\gamma_{+}^{\star}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\gamma+\textbf{b}\gamma^{\star}\right)=\frac{1}{2}\gamma\big(B+\mathds{1}\big).

(446)

Similarly

\gamma_{-}^{\star}=-\frac{1}{2}\gamma\big(B-\mathds{1}\big).

(447)

Next we have

\begin{split}\gamma_{+}\frac{B^{\prime}+\mathds{1}}{2}&=2\left(\gamma+\textbf{b}\gamma^{\star}\right)^{\star}\left[\frac{\left(\gamma+\textbf{b}\gamma^{\star}\right)^{T}\left(\textbf{b}-\mathbf{g}\textbf{b}^{-1}\mathbf{g}\right)^{-1}\left(\gamma+\textbf{b}\gamma^{\star}\right)+\mathds{1}}{2}\right]\\
&=\left(\textbf{b}-\mathbf{g}\textbf{b}^{-1}\mathbf{g}\right)^{-1}\left(\gamma+\textbf{b}\gamma^{\star}\right)+\left(\gamma+\textbf{b}\gamma^{\star}\right)^{\star}.\end{split}

(448)

Observe that

\displaystyle\left(\textbf{b}-\mathbf{g}\textbf{b}^{-1}\mathbf{g}\right)^{-1}\left(\gamma+\textbf{b}\gamma^{\star}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\textbf{b}-\mathbf{g}\textbf{b}^{-1}\mathbf{g}\right)^{-1}(\gamma+\gamma B)

(449)

\displaystyle=\left(\textbf{b}-\mathbf{g}\textbf{b}^{-1}\mathbf{g}\right)^{-1}\gamma(\mathds{1}+B)

\displaystyle=\left(\gamma^{-1}\textbf{b}+\gamma^{-1}\mathbf{g}\textbf{b}^{-1}\mathbf{g}\right)^{-1}(\mathds{1}+B)

\displaystyle=\left(B\gamma^{T}-\gamma^{T}\textbf{b}^{-1}\gamma\gamma^{T}\right)^{-1}(\mathds{1}+B)

\displaystyle=\left(B\gamma^{T}-(\gamma B)^{-1}\gamma\gamma^{T}\right)^{-1}(\mathds{1}+B)

\displaystyle=\left(\left(B-B^{-1}\right)\gamma^{T}\right)^{-1}(\mathds{1}+B)

\displaystyle=\gamma^{\star}\left(B-B^{-1}\right)(\mathds{1}+B).

Next note that

B-B^{-1}=(\mathds{1}+B)\left(\mathds{1}-B^{-1}\right).

(450)

Then we get

\displaystyle\left(\textbf{b}-\mathbf{g}\textbf{b}^{-1}\mathbf{g}\right)^{-1}\left(\gamma+\textbf{b}\gamma^{\star}\right)

\displaystyle=\gamma^{\star}\left(\mathds{1}-B^{-1}\right)^{-1}

(451)

\displaystyle=\gamma^{\star}\left((B-\mathds{1})B^{-1}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=-\gamma^{\star}B(\mathds{1}-B)^{-1}.

We also have

\displaystyle\left(\gamma+\textbf{b}\gamma^{\star}\right)^{\star}

\displaystyle=\big(\gamma(\mathds{1}+B)\big)^{\star}

(452)

\displaystyle=\gamma^{\star}(\mathds{1}-B)^{-1}.

Putting all this together we get

\gamma_{+}\frac{B^{\prime}+\mathds{1}}{2}=\gamma^{\star}.

(453)

Similarly we have

\gamma_{-}\frac{B^{\prime}-\mathds{1}}{2}=\gamma^{\star}\left[\left(B-B^{-1}\right)^{-1}(\mathds{1}-B)-(\mathds{1}+B)^{-1}\right].

(454)

We now use

B-B^{-1}=-(\mathds{1}-B)\left(\mathds{1}+B^{-1}\right)\,,

(455)

to get

\gamma_{-}\frac{B^{\prime}-\mathds{1}}{2}=-\gamma^{\star}.

(456)

From (
446
), (
447
), (
453
) and (
456
) we see that

\mathcal{G}^{\text{(i)}}_{\Lambda_{S}}=\begin{pmatrix}\gamma\frac{B+\mathds{1}}{2}&\gamma\frac{B-\mathds{1}}{2}\\
\gamma^{\star}&\gamma^{\star}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}0&\mathds{1}\\
\mathds{1}&0\end{pmatrix}\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda_{S}}

(457)

Since
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&\mathds{1}\\
\mathds{1}&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)
is determinant
-1
and integral, the transformation (i) of (
445
) preserves the lattice. Similarly we have

\mathcal{G}^{\text{(ii)}}_{\Lambda_{S}}=\begin{pmatrix}-\gamma\frac{B+\mathds{1}}{2}&\gamma\frac{B-\mathds{1}}{2}\\
-\gamma^{\star}&\gamma^{\star}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}0&-\mathds{1}\\
-\mathds{1}&0\end{pmatrix}\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda_{S}}

(458)

and hence preserves the lattice. The full transformation (1) of (
441
) acting on the integer coordinates as well as the generator matrix can be written as a transformation acting only on the integer coordinates as follows:

\text{(i)}\penalty\ \penalty\ (\vec{m}^{T},\vec{n}^{T})\to(\vec{m}^{T},\vec{n}^{T}),\quad\text{(ii)}\penalty\ \penalty\ (\vec{m}^{T},\vec{n}^{T})\to(-\vec{m}^{T},-\vec{n}^{T}).

(459)

This generates a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
subgroup of the automorphism group
\mathrm{O}_{\Lambda_{S}}(m,m,\mathbb{Z})
.
Obviously these transformations preserve
\langle\alpha,\alpha\rangle
and
\langle\beta,\beta\rangle
and hence the full Lorentzian norm.
One could check directly that other choices in (
443
) does not preserve the lattice.

Now, we will show that the second transformation in (
441
) preserves the lattice too. Using the transformation of
\textbf{b}\to\textbf{b}+2\textbf{N}
, we obtain

\gamma B=\mathbf{b}\gamma^{\star}\to\mathbf{b}\gamma^{\star}+2\textbf{N}\gamma^{\star}=\gamma B+2\textbf{N}\gamma^{\star}\,.

(460)

We can input the above relation into the generator matrix to see how it transforms:

\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda_{S}}=\begin{pmatrix}\gamma^{\star}&\gamma^{\star}\\
\gamma\frac{B+\mathds{1}}{2}&\gamma\frac{B-\mathds{1}}{2}\end{pmatrix}\to\mathcal{G}^{\prime}_{\Lambda_{S}}:=\begin{pmatrix}\gamma^{\star}&\quad\gamma^{\star}\\
\gamma\frac{B+\mathds{1}}{2}+\textbf{N}\gamma^{\star}&\quad\gamma\frac{B-\mathds{1}}{2}+\textbf{N}\gamma^{\star}\end{pmatrix}\,.

(461)

From the above equation it follows that

\mathcal{G}^{\prime}_{\Lambda_{S}}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathds{1}&0\\
\mathbf{N}&\mathds{1}\end{pmatrix}\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda_{S}}\,.

(462)

Again since
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\mathds{1}&0\\
\mathbf{N}&\mathds{1}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
is unimodular and integral, this transforamtion preserves the lattice.
The transformation on integer coordinates in (2) of (
441
) can also be nicely represented as

(\vec{m}^{T},\vec{n}^{T})\to((\vec{m}-\mathbf{N}\vec{n})^{T},\vec{n}^{T})=(\vec{m}+\vec{n}^{T}\mathbf{N},\vec{n}^{T})=(\vec{m}^{T},\vec{n}^{T})\begin{pmatrix}\mathds{1}&0\\
\textbf{N}&\mathds{1}\end{pmatrix}\,,

(463)

where we have used that
N
is an anti-symmetric integral matrix. To get the complete transformation on the integer coordinates fixing without transforming the generator matrix, we compose these two transformations to see that

(\vec{m}^{T},\vec{n}^{T})\to(\vec{m}^{T},\vec{n}^{T})\begin{pmatrix}\mathds{1}&0\\
\textbf{N}&\mathds{1}\end{pmatrix}\,\begin{pmatrix}\mathds{1}&0\\
\textbf{N}&\mathds{1}\end{pmatrix}\,=(\vec{m}^{T}+2\vec{n}^{T}\textbf{N},\vec{n}^{T})\,.

(464)

As
N
is integral, we see that the transformed vector lies on the lattice again. Checking that this transformation preserves the norm is a straightforward computation.

Thus all automorphisms of
\Lambda_{S}
will preserve
\langle\alpha,\alpha\rangle
and
\langle\beta,\beta\rangle
. Now suppose
(\alpha,0)\in(\Lambda_{S})_{1}^{0}
maps to
(\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})\in\Lambda_{S}
under some automorphism. Since
\langle\beta^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}\rangle=0
and the norm on
\mathbb{R}^{m}
is positive definite, we conclude that
\beta^{\prime}=0
and the automorphism preserves
(\Lambda_{S})_{1}^{0}
. Similarly
(\Lambda_{S})_{2}^{0}
is also preserved under any automorphims of
\Lambda_{S}
.

Combining Theorem
D
, Theorem
D
and Lemma
D
proves Conjecture
3.3.1
for
m=n
case. We remark that the methods of this Appendix clearly do not apply to
m\neq n
case. One needs new tricks to prove the general result.
